Normalizing the Taylor expansion of non-deterministic λ \lambda -terms, via parallel reduction of resource vectors Thanks: This work was supported by French ANR projects Coquas (number ANR-12-JS02-0006) and Récré (number ANR-11-BS02-0010), as well as the French-Italian Groupement de Recherche International on Linear Logic.
Lionel Vaux

Abstract
. It has been known since Ehrhard and Regnier’s seminal work on the Taylor expansion of λ \lambda -terms that this operation commutes with normalization: the expansion of a λ \lambda -term is always normalizable and its normal form is the expansion of the Böhm tree of the term. We generalize this result to the non-uniform setting of the algebraic λ \lambda -calculus, i.e. , λ \lambda -calculus extended with linear combinations of terms. This requires us to tackle two difficulties: foremost is the fact that Ehrhard and Regnier’s techniques rely heavily on the uniform, deterministic nature of the ordinary λ \lambda -calculus, and thus cannot be adapted; second is the absence of any satisfactory generic extension of the notion of Böhm tree in presence of quantitative non-determinism, which is reflected by the fact that the Taylor expansion of an algebraic λ \lambda -term is not always normalizable. Our solution is to provide a fine grained study of the dynamics of β \beta -reduction under Taylor expansion, by introducing a notion of reduction on resource vectors, i.e. infinite linear combinations of resource λ \lambda -terms. The latter form the multilinear fragment of the differential λ \lambda -calculus, and resource vectors are the target of the Taylor expansion of λ \lambda -terms. We show the reduction of resource vectors contains the image of any β \beta -reduction step, from which we deduce that Taylor expansion and normalization commute on the nose. We moreover identify a class of algebraic λ \lambda -terms, encompassing both normalizable algebraic λ \lambda -terms and arbitrary ordinary λ \lambda -terms: the expansion of these is always normalizable, which guides the definition of a generalization of Böhm trees to this setting.

\titlecomment

An extended abstract
[
Vau17
]
of this paper, focused on the
translation of parallel
\beta
-reduction steps through Taylor expansion, appeared
in the proceedings of
CSL 2017
.

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 15, Issue 3, Paper 9

1.
Introduction

Quantitative semantics was first proposed by Girard
[
Gir88
]

as an alternative to domains and continuous functionals, for defining
denotational models of
\lambda
-calculi with a natural interpretation of
non-determinism: a type is given by a collection of “atomic states”; a term
of type
A
is then represented by a vector (
i.e.
a possibly infinite formal linear
combination) of states. The main matter is the treatment of the
function space: the construction requires the interpretation of function terms to be analytic,
i.e.
defined by power series.

This interpretation of
\lambda
-terms was at the origin of linear
logic: the application of an analytic map to its argument boils down to the
linear application of its power series (seen as a matrix) to the vector of
powers of the argument; similarly, linear logic decomposes the
application of
\lambda
-calculus into the
linear cut rule and the promotion operator. Indeed, the seminal model of linear
logic, namely coherence spaces and stable/linear functions, was introduced as a
qualitative version of quantitative semantics
[
Gir86
, especially Appendix C]
.

Dealing with power series, quantitative semantics must account for infinite
sums. The interpretations of terms in Girard’s original model can be seen as a special case of Joyal’s analytic
functors
[
Joy86
]
: in particular, coefficients are sets
and infinite sums are given by coproducts. This allows to give a semantics to fixed
point operators and to the pure, untyped
\lambda
-calculus. On the other hand, it does
not provide a natural way to deal with weighted (
e.g.
, probabilistic)
non-determinism, where coefficients are taken in an external semiring of
scalars.

In the early 2000’s, Ehrhard introduced an alternative presentation of
quantitative semantics
[
Ehr05
]
, limited to a typed setting, but where
types can be interpreted as particular vector spaces, or more generally
semimodules over an arbitrary fixed semiring; called
finiteness spaces
,
these are moreover equipped with a linear topology, allowing to interpret
linear logic proofs as linear and continuous maps, in a standard sense.
In this setting, the formal operation of differentiation of power series
recovers its usual meaning of linear approximation of a function, and morphisms
in the induced model of
\lambda
-calculus are subject to Taylor expansion:
the application
\varphi(\alpha)
of the
analytic function
\varphi
to the vector
\alpha
boils down to the sum

\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}\frac{1}{n\,\mathord{!}}{\left(\frac{\partial^{n}\varphi}{\partial x^{n}}\right)}_{x=0}\cdot{\alpha^{n}}

where
{\left(\frac{\partial^{n}\varphi}{\partial x^{n}}\right)}_{x=0}

is the
n
-th derivative of
\varphi
computed at
0
,
which is an
n
-linear map, and
\alpha^{n}
is the
n
-th tensor power of
\alpha
.

Ehrhard and Regnier gave a computational meaning to such derivatives by
introducing linearized variants of application and substitution in the
\lambda
-calculus,
which led to the differential
\lambda
-calculus
[
ER03
]
,
and then the resource
\lambda
-calculus
[
ER08
]
—
the latter retains iterated derivatives at zero as the only form of application.
They were then able to recast the above Taylor expansion formula in a syntactic,
untyped setting: to every
\lambda
-term
M
, they associate a vector
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}
of
resource
\lambda
-terms,
i.e.
terms of the resource
\lambda
-calculus.

The Taylor expansion of a
\lambda
-term can be seen as an intermediate, infinite
object, between the term and its denotation in quantitative semantics. Indeed,
resource terms still retain a dynamics, if a very simple, finitary one: the size of
terms is strictly decreasing under reduction. Furthermore, normal resource terms are
in close relationship with the atomic states of quantitative semantics of the
pure
\lambda
-calculus (or equivalently with the elements of a reflexive object in the
relational model
[
BEM07
]
; or with normal type derivations in a
non-idempotent intersection type system
[
dC08
]
), so that the normal
form of
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}
can be considered as the denotation of
M
, which allows
for a very generic description of quantitative semantics.

Other approaches to quantitative semantics generally impose a constraint on the
computational model
a priori
. For instance, the model of finiteness
spaces
[
Ehr05
]
is, by design, limited to strongly normalizing
computation. Another example is that of probabilistic coherence spaces

[
DE11
]
, a model of untyped
\lambda
-calculi extended with probabilistic
choice, rather than arbitrary weighted superpositions.
Alternatively, one can interpret non-deterministic extensions of PCF

[
LMMP13
,
Lai16
]
, provided the semiring of scalars has
all infinite sums. By contrast, the “normalization of
Taylor expansion” approach is more canonical, as it does not rely on a
restriction on the scalars, nor on the terms to be interpreted.

Of course, there is a price attached to such canonicity:
in general, the normal form of a vector of resource

\lambda
-terms is not well defined, because we may have to consider infinite sums of
scalars.
Ehrhard and Regnier were nonetheless able to prove that the Taylor expansion

\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}
of a pure
\lambda
-term is always normalizable
[
ER08
]
. This
can be seen as a new proof of the fact that Girard’s quantitative semantics of pure

\lambda
-terms uses finite cardinals only
[
Has96
]
.
They moreover established that this normal form is exactly the Taylor expansion
of the Böhm tree
\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}{\left(M\right)}
of
M

[
ER06
]
(
\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}{\left(M\right)}
is the
possibly infinite tree obtained by hereditarily applying the head reduction
strategy in
M
).
Both results rely heavily on the uniformity property of the pure
\lambda
-calculus:
all the resource terms in
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}
follow a single syntactic tree pattern.
This is a bit disappointing since quantitative semantics
was introduced as a model of non-determinism,
which is ruled out by uniformity.

Actually, the Taylor expansion operator extends naturally to the algebraic

\lambda
-calculus
[
Vau09
]
: a generic, non-uniform extension of

\lambda
-calculus, augmenting the syntax with formal finite linear
combinations of terms. Then it is not difficult to find terms whose
Taylor expansion is not normalizable.
Nonetheless, interpreting types as finiteness spaces of resource terms,
Ehrhard
[
Ehr10
]
proved by a reducibility technique that
the Taylor expansion of algebraic
\lambda
-terms typed in a variant of system
F
is
always normalizable.

1.1.
Main results

In the present paper, we generalize Ehrhard’s result and show that all weakly
normalizable algebraic
\lambda
-terms have a normalizable Taylor expansion (Theorem

7
, p.
7
).
1
1

1

We had already obtained such a result for strongly normalizable
\lambda
-terms
in a previous work with Pagani and Tasson
[
PTV16
]
: there, we
further proved that the finiteness structure on resource
\lambda
-terms could be
refined to characterize exactly the strong normalizability property in a

\lambda
-calculus with finite formal sums of terms.
Here we rely on a much coarser notion of finiteness: see subsection

8.1
.

We moreover relate the normal form of the expansion of a term with the
normal form of the term itself, both in a computational sense (
i.e.
the
irreducible form obtained after a sequence of reductions) and in a more
denotational sense, via an analogue of the notion of Böhm tree:
Taylor expansion does commute with normalization, in both those senses
(Theorem
8
, p.
8
;
Theorem
9
, p.
9
).

When restricted to pure
\lambda
-terms, Theorem
9

provides a new proof, not relying on uniformity, that the normal form of

\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}
is isomorphic to
\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}{\left(M\right)}
.
In their full extent, our results provide a generalization of the notion of
non-deterministic Böhm tree
[
dLP95
]
in a weighted,
quantitative setting.

Let us stress that neither Ehrhard’s work
[
Ehr10
]
nor our own
previous work with Pagani and Tasson
[
PTV16
]
addressed the
commutation of normalization and Taylor expansion. Indeed, in the absence of
uniformity, the techniques used by Ehrhard and Regnier

[
ER08
,
ER06
]
are no longer available, and we had to design another
approach.
2
2

2

It is in fact possible to refine Ehrhard and Regnier’s approach,
via

the introduction of a
rigid
variant of Taylor expansion

[
TAO17
]
, which can then be adapted to the non-deterministic
setting.
This allows to describe the coefficients in the normal form of Taylor
expansion, like in the uniform case, and then prove that Taylor expansion
commutes with the computation of Böhm trees.
It does not solve the problem of possible divergence, though, and one has to
assume the semiring of coefficients is complete,
i.e.
that all sums converge.
See Subsection
1.3
on related work for more details.

Our solution is to introduce a notion of reduction on resource vectors,
so that: (i) this reduction contains the translation of any
\beta
-reduction step
(Lemma
7.10
, p.
7.10
);
(ii) normalizability (and the value of the normal form) of resource vectors is
preserved under reduction (Lemma
8.4
,
p.
8.4
). This approach turns out to be quite
delicate, and its development led us to two technical contributions that we
deem important enough to be noted here:

•

the notion of
reduction structure
(subsection

5.3
) that allows to control the families of resource
terms simultaneously involved in the reduction of a resource vector:
in particular this provides a novel, modular mean to circumvent the
inconsistency of
\beta
-reduction in presence of negative coefficients
(a typical deficiency of the algebraic
\lambda
-calculus
[
Vau09
]
);

•

our analysis of the effect of parallel reduction on the size of resource

\lambda
-terms (Section
6
): this constitutes the technical core
of our approach, and it plays a crucial rôle in establishing key additional
properties such as confluence (Lemma
6.26
,
p.
6.26
, and Corollary

6.46
,
p.
6.46
) and conservativity
(Lemma
7.24
,
p.
7.24
, and
Lemma
8.33
,
p.
8.33
).

1.2.
Structure of the paper

The paper begins with a few mathematical preliminaries, in section

2
: we recall some definitions
about semirings and semimodules (Subsection
2.1
), if only to fix
notations and vocabulary; we also provide a very brief review of finiteness
spaces (Subsection
2.2
),
then detail the particular case of
linear-continuous maps defined by summable families of vectors (subsection

2.3
), the latter notion pervading the paper.

In Section
3
we review the syntax and the reduction
relation of the resource
\lambda
-calculus, as introduced by Ehrhard and Regnier

[
ER08
]
. The subject is quite standard now, and the only new
material we provide is about minor and unsurprising combinatorial properties of
multilinear substitution.

Section
4
contains our first notable contribution: after
recalling the Taylor expansion construction, we prove that it is compatible
with substitution. This result is related with the functoriality of promotion
in quantitative denotational models and the proof technique is quite similar.
In the passing, we recall the syntax of the algebraic
\lambda
-calculus and briefly
discuss the issues raised by the contextual extension of
\beta
-reduction in
presence of linear combinations of terms, as evidenced by previous work

[
Vau07
,
AD08
,
Vau09
,
etc.
]
.

In Section
5
, we discuss the possible extensions
of the reduction of the resource
\lambda
-calculus to resource vectors,
i.e.
infinite
linear combinations of resource terms, and identify two main issues. First, in
order to simulate
\beta
-reduction, we
are led to consider the parallel reduction of resource terms in resource
vectors, which is not always well defined. Indeed, a single resource term
might have unboundedly many antecedents by parallel reduction, hence this
process might generate infinite sums of coefficients: we refer to this
phenomenon as the
size collapse
of parallel resource reduction
(Subsection
5.2
).
Second, similarly to the case of the algebraic
\lambda
-calculus, the induced
equational theory might become trivial, due the interplay between coefficients
in vectors and the reduction relation.
To address the latter problem we introduce the notion of reduction structure
(Subsection
5.3
) which allows us to modularly restrict
the set of resource terms involved in a reduction: later in the paper, we will
identify reduction structures ensuring the consistency of the reduction of
resource vectors (Subsection
8.4
).

In Section
6
, we introduce successive restrictions of the
parallel reduction of resource vectors, in order to avoid the abovementioned
size collapse. We first observe that, to bound the size of a term as a
function of the size of any of its reducts, it is sufficient to bound the
length of chains of immediately nested fired redexes in a single parallel
reduction step (Subsection
6.1
).
This condition does not allow us to close a pair of reductions to a common
reduct, because it is not stable under unions of fired redexes. We thus tighten
it to bounding the length of all chains of (not necessarily immediately) nested
fired redexes (Subsection
6.2
): this enables us to
obtain a strong confluence result, under a mild hypothesis on the semiring.
An even more demanding condition is to require the fired redexes as well
as the substituted variables to occur at a bounded depth (subsection

6.3
): then we can define a maximal parallel reduction
step for each bound, which entails strong confluence without any additional hypothesis.
Finally, we consider reduction structures involving resource terms of bounded
height (Subsection
6.4
): when restricting to such a
bounded reduction structure, the strongest of the above three conditions
is automatically verified.

We then show, in Section
7
, that the translation
of
\beta
-reduction through Taylor expansion fits into this setting: the height of
the resource terms involved in a Taylor expansion is bounded by that of the
original algebraic
\lambda
-term, and every
\beta
-reduction step is an instance of the
previously introduced parallel reduction of resource vectors. As a consequence
of our strongest confluence result, we moreover obtain that any reduction step
from the Taylor expansion of a
\lambda
-term can be extended into the translation of a
parallel
\beta
-reduction step.

We turn our attention to normalization in Section
8
.
We first show that normalizable resource vectors are stable under reduction.
We moreover establish that their normal form is obtained as the limit of the
parallel left reduction strategy (Subsection
8.1
).
Then we introduce
Taylor normalizable
algebraic
\lambda
-terms as those having a
normalizable Taylor expansion, and deduce from the previous results that they
are stable under
\beta
-reduction (Subsection
8.2
):
in particular, the normal form of Taylor expansion does define a denotational
semantics for that class of terms.
Then we establish that normalizable terms are Taylor normalizable (subsection

8.3
): it follows that normalization and Taylor
expansion commute on the nose.

We conclude with Section
9
, showing how our techniques
can be applied to the class of hereditarily determinable terms, that we
introduce
ad-hoc
: those include pure
\lambda
-terms as well as normalizable
algebraic
\lambda
-terms as a particular case, and we show that all
hereditarily determinable terms are Taylor normalizable and the
coefficients of the normal form are given by a sequence of approximants, close
to the Böhm tree construction.

1.3.
Related and future work

Besides the seminal work by Ehrhard and Regnier
[
ER08
,
ER06
]
in
the pure case, we have already cited previous approaches to the normalizability
of Taylor expansion based on finiteness conditions
[
Ehr10
,
PTV16
]
.

A natural question to ask is how our generic notion of normal form of Taylor
expansion compares with previously introduced notions of denotation in non-deterministic
settings: non-deterministic Böhm trees
[
dLP95
]
,
probabilistic Böhm trees
[
Lev16
]
, weighted relational models

[
DE11
,
LMMP13
,
Lai16
]
,
etc.

The very statement of such a question raises several difficulties, prompting
further lines of research.

One first obstacle is the fact that, by contrast with the uniform case of the
ordinary
\lambda
-calculus, the Taylor expansion operator is not injective on
algebraic
\lambda
-terms (see Subsection
4.5
), not even on the
partial normal forms that we use to introduce the approximants in section

9
. This is to be related with the quotient that the
non-deterministic Böhm trees of de’Liguoro and Piperno

[
dLP95
]
must undergo in order to capture observational
equivalence. On the other hand, to our knowledge, finding
sufficient conditions on the semiring of scalars ensuring that the Taylor
expansion becomes injective is still an open question.

Also, we define normalizable vectors based on the notion of summability: a sum
of vectors converges when it is componentwise finite
i.e.
, for each component,
only finitely many vectors have a non-zero coefficient (see subsection

2.3
). If more information is available on scalars,
namely if the semiring of scalars is complete in some topological or
order-theoretic sense, it becomes possible to normalize the Taylor expansion of
all terms.

Indeed, Tsukada, Asada and Ong have recently established
[
TAO17
]
the
commutation between computing Böhm trees and Taylor expansion with
coefficients taken in the complete semiring of positive reals
[0,+\infty]

where all sums converge.
Let us precise that they do not consider weighted non-determinism, only formal
binary sums of terms, and that the notion of Böhm tree they consider is a very
syntactic one, similar to the partial normal forms we introduce in section

9
.
Their approach is based on a precise description of the relationship
between the coefficients of resource terms in the expansion of a term and those in
the expansion of its Böhm-tree, using a
rigid
Taylor expansion as an
intermediate step: this avoids the ambiguity between the sums of coefficients
generated by redundancies in the expansion and those representing
non-deterministic superpositions.

Tsukada, Asada and Ong’s work can thus be considered as a refinement of Ehrhard
and Regnier’s method, that they are moreover able to generalize to the
non-deterministic case provided the semiring of scalars is complete.
By contrast, our approach is focused on
\beta
-reduction and identifies a class of
algebraic
\lambda
-terms for which the normalization of Taylor expansion converges
independently from the topology on scalars.
It seems only natural to investigate the connections between both approaches,
in particular to tackle the case of weighted non-determinism in a complete
semiring, as a first step towards the treatment of probabilistic or quantum
superposition, as also suggested by the conclusion of their paper.

In the probabilistic setting, though, the Böhm tree
construction
[
Lev16
]
relies on both the topological properties of
real numbers and the restriction to discrete probability subdistributions.
Relying on this, Dal Lago and Leventis have recently shown

[
DLL19
]
that the sum defining the normal form
of Taylor expansion of an arbitrary probabilistic
\lambda
-term always converges with
finite coefficients, and that this normal form is the Taylor expansion of its
probabilistic Böhm tree, in the non-extensional sense
[
Lev16
, section
4.2.1]
.
To get a better understanding of the shape of Taylor
expansions of probabilistic
\lambda
-terms and their stability under reduction, a possible
first step is to investigate probabilistic coherence spaces

[
DE11
]
on resource
\lambda
-terms: these would be the analogue, in the probabilistic setting, of
the finiteness structures ensuring the summability of normal forms in the
non-deterministic setting (see Subsection
8.3
).

Apart from relating our version of quantitative semantics with pre-existing
notions of denotation for non-deterministic
\lambda
-calculi, we plan to investigate
possible applications to other proof theoretic or computational frameworks:
namely, linear logic proof nets
[
Gir87
]
and infinitary
\lambda
-calculus

[
KKSdV97
]
.

The Taylor expansion of
\lambda
-terms can be generalized to linear logic proof nets:
the case of linear logic can even be considered as being more primitive, as it
is directly related with the structure of those denotational models that
validate the Taylor expansion formula
[
Ehr16
]
.
Proof nets, however, do not enjoy the uniformity property of
\lambda
-terms:
no general coherence relation is satisfied by the elements of the Taylor expansion of a
proof net
[
Tas09
, section V.4.1]
. This can be related with the
non-injectivity of coherence semantics
[
TdF03
]
.
In particular, it is really unclear how Ehrhard and Regnier’s methods, or even
Tsukada, Asada and Ong’s could be transposed to this setting.
By contrast, our recent work with Chouquet
[
CA18
]

shows that our study of reduction under Taylor expansion can be adapted to
proof nets.

It is also quite easy to extend the Taylor expansion operator to infinite

\lambda
-terms, at least for those of
\Lambda^{001}
, where only the argument
position of applications is treated coinductively.
For infinite
\lambda
-terms, it is no longer the case that the support of Taylor
expansion involves resource
\lambda
-terms of bounded height only.
Fortunately, we can still rely on the results of subsection

6.2
, where we only require a bound on the nesting of
fired redexes: this should allow us to give a counterpart, through Taylor
expansion, of the strongly converging reduction sequences of infinite
\lambda
-terms.
More speculatively, another possible outcome is a characterization of
hereditarily head normalizable terms via their Taylor expansion, adapting our
previous work on normalizability with Pagani and Tasson
[
PTV16
]
.

2.
Technical preliminaries

We write:

•

\mathbf{N}
for the semiring of natural numbers;

•

\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(X\right)}
for the powerset of a set
X
:
\mathcal{X}\in\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(X\right)}
iff
\mathcal{X}\subseteq X
;

•

\mathord{\#}{X}
for the cardinal of a finite set
X
;

•

\oc X
for the set of finite multisets of elements of
X
;

•

{\left[x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n}\right]}\in\oc X
for the
multiset with elements
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n}\in X

(taking repetitions into account),
and then
\mathord{\#}{{\left[x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n}\right]}}=n
for its
cardinality;

•

\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}
and
\sum_{i\in I}X_{i}
respectively
for the product and sum of a family
{\left(X_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}
of sets:
in particular
\sum_{i\in I}X_{i}=\bigcup_{i\in I}{\left\{i\right\}}\times X_{i}
;

•

X^{I}=\prod_{i\in I}X
for the set of applications from
I
to
X
or,
equivalently, for the set of
I
indexed families of
elements of
X
.

Throughout the paper we will be led to consider various categories of sets and
elements associated with a single base set
X
: elements of
X
, subsets of

X
, finite multisets of elements of
X
,
etc.
In order to help keeping
track of those categories, we generally adopt the following typographic
conventions:

•

we use small latin letters for
the elements of
X
, say
a,b,c\in X
;

•

for subsets of
X
, we use
cursive capitals, say
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\in\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(X\right)}
;

•

for sets of subsets of
X
, we use
Fraktur capitals, say
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B},\mathfrak{C}\subseteq\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(X\right)}
;

•

for (possibly infinite) linear combinations of elements of
X
, we use
small greek letters, say
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\mathbf{S}^{X}
,
where
\mathbf{S}
denotes some set of scalar coefficients;

•

we transpose all of the above conventions to the
set
\oc X
of finite multisets by overlining:

e.g.
, we write
\overline{a}={\left[a_{1},\dotsc,a_{n}\right]}\in\oc X
,

\overline{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq\oc X
or
\overline{\alpha}\in\mathbf{S}^{\oc X}
.

In the remaining of this section, we introduce basic mathematical content that
will be used throughout the paper.

2.1.
Semirings and semimodules

A
semiring
3
3

3

The terminology of semirings is much less well established
than that of rings, and one can find various non equivalent
definitions depending on the presence of units or on commutativity
requirements. Following Golan’s terminology
[
Gol13
]
,
our semirings are
commutative semirings
, which is required
here because we consider multilinear applications between
modules.

\mathbf{S}
is the data of a carrier set
\underline{\mathbf{S}}
, together
with commutative monoids
(\underline{\mathbf{S}},+_{\mathbf{S}},0_{\mathbf{S}})

and
(\underline{\mathbf{S}},\cdot_{\mathbf{S}},1_{\mathbf{S}})
such that
the multiplicative structure distributes over the additive one,
i.e.
for all
a,b,c\in\underline{\mathbf{S}}
,

a\mathbin{\cdot_{\mathbf{S}}}0_{\mathbf{S}}=0_{\mathbf{S}}
and

a\cdot_{\mathbf{S}}(b+_{\mathbf{S}}c)=a\cdot_{\mathbf{S}}b+_{\mathbf{S}}a\cdot_{\mathbf{S}}c
.

We will in general abuse notation and identify
\mathbf{S}
with its carrier set
\underline{\mathbf{S}}
.
We will moreover omit the subscripts on symbols
+
,
\cdot
,
0
and
1
, and
denote multiplication by concatenation:
ab=a\cdot b
.
We also use standard notations for finite sums and products in
\mathbf{S}
,
e.g.

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}
.
For any semiring
\mathbf{S}
, there is a unique semiring morphism (in the obvious
sense) from
\mathbf{N}
to
\mathbf{S}
: to
n\in\mathbf{N}
we associate the sum

\sum_{i=1}^{n}1\in\mathbf{S}
that we also write
n\in\mathbf{S}
, although this morphism
is not necessarily injective. Consider for instance the semiring
\mathbf{B}

of booleans, with
\underline{\mathbf{B}}={\left\{0,1\right\}}
,
\mathord{+}_{\mathbf{B}}=\mathord{\max}
and
\mathord{\cdot}_{\mathbf{B}}=\mathord{\times}
.

We finish this subsection by recalling the definitions of
semimodules and their morphisms.
A
(left)
\mathbf{S}
-semimodule

\mathcal{M}
is the data of a commutative
monoid
(\underline{\mathcal{M}},0_{\mathcal{M}},+_{\mathcal{M}})
together with an
external product
._{\mathcal{M}}:\mathbf{S}\times\underline{\mathcal{M}}\to\underline{\mathcal{M}}

subject to the following identities:

\displaystyle 0._{\mathcal{M}}m

\displaystyle=0_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle 1._{\mathcal{M}}m

\displaystyle=m

\displaystyle(a+b)._{\mathcal{M}}m

\displaystyle=a._{\mathcal{M}}m+_{\mathcal{M}}b._{\mathcal{M}}m

\displaystyle a._{\mathcal{M}}(b._{\mathcal{M}}m)

\displaystyle=ab._{\mathcal{M}}m

\displaystyle a._{\mathcal{M}}0_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=0_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle a._{\mathcal{M}}(m+_{\mathcal{M}}n)

\displaystyle=a._{\mathcal{M}}m+_{\mathcal{M}}a._{\mathcal{M}}n

for all
a,b\in\mathbf{S}
and
m,n\in\underline{\mathcal{M}}
.
Again, we will in general abuse notation and identify
\mathcal{M}
with its carrier set
\underline{\mathcal{M}}
,
and omit the subscripts on symbols
+
,
.
and
0
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
be
\mathbf{S}
-semimodules.
We say
\phi:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
is
linear
if

\phi{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}.m_{i}\right)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}.\phi{\left(m_{i}\right)}

for all
m_{1},\dotsc,m_{n}\in\mathcal{M}
and all
a_{1},\dotsc,a_{n}\in\mathbf{S}
.
If moreover
\mathcal{M}_{1},\dotsc,\mathcal{M}_{n}
are
\mathbf{S}
-semimodules,
we say
\psi:\mathcal{M}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{M}_{n}\to\mathcal{N}
is
n
-linear

if it is linear in each component.

Given a set
X
,
\mathbf{S}^{X}
is
the semimodule of formal linear combinations of elements of
X
:
a
vector

\xi\in\mathbf{S}^{X}
is nothing but an
X
-indexed
family of scalars
{\left(\xi_{x}\right)}_{x\in X}
, that we may
also denote by
\sum_{x\in X}\xi_{x}.x
.
The
support

{\left|\xi\right|}
of a vector
\xi\in\mathbf{S}^{X}
is the set of elements
of
X
having a non-zero coefficient in
\xi
:

{\left|\xi\right|}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\{x\in X\mathrel{;}\xi_{x}\not=0\right\}}.

We write
\mathbf{S}{\left[X\right]}
for the set of vectors with finite support:

\mathbf{S}{\left[X\right]}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\{\xi\in\mathbf{S}^{X}\mathrel{;}{\left|\xi\right|}\text{ is finite}\right\}}.

In particular
\mathbf{S}{\left[X\right]}
is the semimodule freely generated by
X
,
and is a subsemimodule of
\mathbf{S}^{X}
.

2.2.
Finiteness spaces

A finiteness space
[
Ehr05
]
is a subsemimodule of
\mathbf{S}^{X}

obtained by imposing a restriction on the support of vectors, as follows.

If
X
is a set, we call
structure on
X
any set
\mathfrak{S}\subseteq\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(X\right)}
,
and then the dual structure is

\mathfrak{S}^{\perp}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\subseteq X\mathrel{;}\text{ for all $\mathcal{X}\in\mathfrak{S}$,
$\mathcal{X}\mathbin{\cap}\mathcal{X}^{\prime}$ is finite}\}}.

A
relational finiteness space
is a pair
(X,\mathfrak{F})
,
where
X
is a set (the
web
of the finiteness space) and

\mathfrak{F}\subseteq\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(X\right)}
is a structure on
X

such that
\mathfrak{F}=\mathfrak{F}^{\perp\perp}
:
\mathfrak{F}
is then called
a
finiteness structure
, and we say
\mathcal{X}\subseteq X

is
finitary
in
(X,\mathfrak{F})
iff
\mathcal{X}\in\mathfrak{F}
.
The
finiteness space
generated by
(X,\mathfrak{F})
,
denoted by
\mathbf{S}{\left\langle X,\mathfrak{F}\right\rangle}
,
or simply
\mathbf{S}{\left\langle\mathfrak{F}\right\rangle}
,
is then the set of vectors on
X
with finitary support:

\xi\in\mathbf{S}{\left\langle\mathfrak{F}\right\rangle}
iff
{\left|\xi\right|}\in\mathfrak{F}
.
By this definition, if
\xi\in\mathbf{S}{\left\langle\mathfrak{F}\right\rangle}
and

\xi^{\prime}\in\mathbf{S}{\left\langle\mathfrak{F}^{\perp}\right\rangle}
then the sum
\sum_{x\in X}\xi_{x}\xi^{\prime}_{x}
involves
finitely many nonzero summands.

Finitary subsets are downwards closed for
inclusion, and finite unions of finitary subsets are finitary,
hence
\mathbf{S}{\left\langle X,\mathfrak{F}\right\rangle}
is a subsemimodule of
\mathbf{S}^{X}
.
Moreover, the least (resp. greatest) finiteness structure
on
X
is the set
\mathord{\mathfrak{P}_{f}}{\left(X\right)}
of finite subsets of
X
(resp. the powerset
\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(X\right)}
),
generating the finiteness space
\mathbf{S}{\left[X\right]}
(resp.
\mathbf{S}^{X}
).

We do not describe the whole category of finiteness spaces and linear-continuous
maps here. In particular we do not recall the details of the linear topology induced
on
\mathbf{S}{\left\langle X,\mathfrak{F}\right\rangle}
by
\mathfrak{F}
: the reader
may refer to Ehrhard’s original paper
[
Ehr05
]
or his survey presentation of
differential linear logic
[
Ehr16
]
.

In the following, we focus on a very particular case, where the
finiteness structure on base types is trivial (
i.e.
there is no restriction on the
support of vectors):
linear-continuous maps are then univocally generated by
summable functions
.

We started with the general notion of finiteness space
nonetheless, because it provides a good background for the general spirit of
our contributions: we are interested in infinite objects restricted so that,
componentwise, all our constructions involve finite sums only.
Also, the semimodule of normalizable resource vectors introduced in section

8
is easier to work with once its finiteness space
structure is exposed.

2.3.
Summable functions

Let
\overrightarrow{\xi}={\left(\xi_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}\in{\left(\mathbf{S}^{X}\right)}^{I}
be a family of vectors:
write
\xi_{i}=\sum_{x\in X}\xi_{i,x}.x
.
We say
\overrightarrow{\xi}
is
summable

if, for all
x\in X
,

{\left\{i\in I\mathrel{;}x\in{\left|\xi_{i}\right|}\right\}}
is finite.
In this case, we define the sum
\sum\overrightarrow{\xi}=\sum_{i\in I}\xi_{i}\in\mathbf{S}^{X}

in the obvious, pointwise way:
4
4

4

The reader can check that the family
\overrightarrow{\xi}
is summable iff
the support set

{\left\{(i,x)\in I\times X\mathrel{;}\xi_{i,x}\not=0\right\}}

is finitary
in the relational arrow finiteness space
(I\times X,\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(I\right)}\multimap\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(X\right)})

as defined by Ehrhard
[
Ehr05
, see in particular Lemma 3]
.
Then
\sum\overrightarrow{\xi}
is the result of applying the matrix
{\left(\xi_{i,x}\right)}_{i\in I,x\in X}

to the vector
{\left(1\right)}_{i\in I}\in\mathbf{S}{\left\langle\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(I\right)}\right\rangle}=\mathbf{S}^{I}
.

{\left(\sum\overrightarrow{\xi}\right)}_{x}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\sum_{i\in I}\xi_{i,x}.

Of course, any finite family of vectors is summable and, fixing an index set

I
and a base set
X
, summable families in
{\left(\mathbf{S}^{X}\right)}^{I}
form an

\mathbf{S}
-semimodule, with operations defined pointwise.

Moreover, if
{\left(\xi_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}\in{\left(\mathbf{S}^{X}\right)}^{I}
is summable,
then it follows from the inclusion

{\left|a_{i}.\xi_{i}\right|}\subseteq{\left|\xi_{i}\right|}
that
{\left(a_{i}.\xi_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}

is also summable for any family of scalars
{\left(a_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}\in\mathbf{S}^{I}
.
Whenever the
n
-ary function

f:X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{n}\to\mathbf{S}^{Y}

(
i.e.
the family

{\left(f(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\right)}_{{\left(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n}\right)}\in X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{n}}
)
is summable,
we can thus define its
extension

{\left\langle f\right\rangle}:\mathbf{S}^{X_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbf{S}^{X_{n}}\to\mathbf{S}^{Y}

by

{\left\langle f\right\rangle}(\xi_{1},\dotsc,\xi_{n})\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\sum_{{\left(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n}\right)}\in X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{n}}\xi_{1,x_{1}}\cdots\xi_{n,x_{n}}.f(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n}).

Note that we can consider
f:X\to\mathbf{S}^{Y}
as a
Y\times X
matrix:

f_{y,x}=f(x)_{y}
. Then if
f
is summable and
\xi\in\mathbf{S}^{X}
,

{\left\langle f\right\rangle}({\xi})
is nothing but the application of the matrix

f
to the column
\xi
: the summability hypothesis ensures that
this is well defined.

It turns out that the linear extensions of summable functions
are exactly the linear-continuous maps, defined as follows:

Definition
\thethm
.

Let
\varphi:\mathbf{S}^{X_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbf{S}^{X_{n}}\to\mathbf{S}^{Y}
.
We say
{\varphi}
is
n
-linear-continuous
if, for all
summable families

\overrightarrow{\xi_{1}}={\left(\xi_{1,i}\right)}_{i\in I_{1}}\in{\left(\mathbf{S}^{X_{1}}\right)}^{I_{1}},\dotsc,\overrightarrow{\xi_{n}}={\left(\xi_{n,i}\right)}_{i\in I_{n}}\in{\left(\mathbf{S}^{X_{n}}\right)}^{I_{n}}
,
the family

{\left(\varphi(\xi_{1,i_{1}},\dotsc,\xi_{n,i_{n}})\right)}_{(i_{1},\dotsc,i_{n})\in I_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{n}}
is summable and,
for all families of scalars,

\overrightarrow{a_{1}}={\left(a_{1,i}\right)}_{i\in I_{1}}\in{\mathbf{S}}^{I_{1}},\dotsc,\overrightarrow{a_{n}}={\left(a_{n,i}\right)}_{i\in I_{n}}\in{\mathbf{S}}^{I_{n}}
,
we have

\varphi{\left(\sum_{i_{1}\in I_{1}}a_{1,i_{1}}.\xi_{1,i_{1}},\dotsc,\sum_{i_{n}\in I_{n}}a_{n,i_{n}}.\xi_{n,i_{n}}\right)}=\sum_{(i_{1},\dotsc,i_{n})\in I_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{n}}a_{1,i_{1}}\cdots a_{n,i_{n}}.{\varphi}(\xi_{1,i_{1}},\dotsc,\xi_{n,i_{n}}).

Lemma
\thethm
.

If
\varphi:\mathbf{S}^{X_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbf{S}^{X_{n}}\to\mathbf{S}^{Y}

is
n
-linear-continuous then its restriction

\varphi\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{n}}

is a summable
n
-ary function and

\varphi={\left\langle\varphi\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{n}}\right\rangle}
.
Conversely, if
f:X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{n}\to\mathbf{S}^{Y}
is
a summable
n
-ary function then
{\left\langle f\right\rangle}
is
n
-linear-continuous.

Proof.

It is possible to derive both implications from general results
on finiteness spaces.
5
5

5

One might check that a map

\varphi:\mathbf{S}^{X_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbf{S}^{X_{n}}\to\mathbf{S}^{Y}

is
n
-linear-continuous in the sense of Definition
2.3

iff it is
n
-linear and continuous in the sense of the linear topology of
finiteness spaces, observing that the topology on
\mathbf{S}^{X}=\mathbf{S}{\left\langle\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(X\right)}\right\rangle}
is the product topology (
\mathbf{S}
being
endowed with the discrete topology)
[
Ehr05
, Section 3]
.
Moreover,
n
-ary summable functions
f:X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{n}\to\mathbf{S}^{Y}

are the elements of the finiteness space

\mathbf{S}{\left\langle\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(X_{1}\right)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(X_{n}\right)}\multimap\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(Y\right)}\right\rangle}
.
As a general fact, the
linear-continuous maps
\mathbf{S}{\left\langle\mathfrak{F}\right\rangle}\to\mathbf{S}{\left\langle\mathfrak{G}\right\rangle}
are exactly
the linear extensions of vectors in
\mathbf{S}{\left\langle\mathfrak{F}\multimap\mathfrak{G}\right\rangle}
.
But linear-continuous maps from a tensor product of
finiteness spaces correspond with multilinear-
hypocontinuous
maps

[
Ehr05
, Section 3]

rather than the more restrictive multilinear-continuous maps.
In the very simple setting of summable functions, though, both notions
coincide, since
\mathbf{S}^{X}
is always locally linearly compact

[
Ehr05
, Proposition 15]
.

We also sketch a direct proof.

The first implication follows directly from the definitions, observing
that each diagonal family of vectors
(x)_{x\in X_{i}}
is obviously summable.

For the converse:
let
\overrightarrow{\xi_{1}}={\left(\xi_{1,i}\right)}_{i\in I_{1}}\in{\left(\mathbf{S}^{X_{1}}\right)}^{I_{1}},\dotsc,\overrightarrow{\xi_{n}}={\left(\xi_{n,i}\right)}_{i\in I_{n}}\in{\left(\mathbf{S}^{X_{n}}\right)}^{I_{n}}

be summable families.
We first prove that the family

{\left(\xi_{1,i_{1},x_{1}}\cdots\xi_{n,i_{n},x_{n}}.f(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\right)}_{(i_{1},\dotsc,i_{n})\in I_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{n},(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\in X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{n}}

is summable. Fix
y\in Y
.
If
y\in{\left|\xi_{1,i_{1},x_{1}}\cdots\xi_{n,i_{n},x_{n}}.f(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\right|}
then in particular

y\in{\left|f(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\right|}
: since
f
is summable, there are finitely many
such tuples
(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})\in X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{n}
.
For each such tuple
(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{n})
and each
k\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
,
since
\overrightarrow{\xi}_{k}
is summable, there are finitely many
i_{k}
’s such that

\xi_{k,i_{k},x_{k}}\not=0
. The necessary equation then follows from the
associativity of sums.
∎

From now on, we will identify summable functions with their
multilinear-continuous extensions. Moreover, it should be clear that
multilinear-continuous maps compose.

3.
The resource
\lambda
-calculus

In this section, we recall the syntax and reduction of the resource
\lambda
-calculus,
that was introduced by Ehrhard and Regnier
[
ER08
]
as the
multilinear fragment of the differential
\lambda
-calculus
[
ER03
]
.
The syntax is very similar to that of Boudol’s resource
\lambda
-calculus

[
Bou93
]
but the intended meaning (multilinear approximations of

\lambda
-terms) as well as the dynamics is fundamentally different.

We also recall the definitions of the multilinear counterparts of term substitution:
partial differentiation and multilinear substitution.

In the passing, we introduce various quantities on resource
\lambda
-terms (size,
height, and number and maximum depth of occurrences of a variable) and we state
basic results that will be used throughout the paper.

Finally, we present the dynamics of the calculus: resource reduction and
normalization.

3.1.
Resource expressions

In the remaining of the paper, we suppose an infinite, countable set

\mathcal{V}
of variables is fixed: we use small letters
x,y,z
to denote
variables.

We define the sets
\Delta
of
resource terms

and
\oc\Delta
of
resource monomials
by mutual induction as follows:
6
6

6

We use a self explanatory if not standard variant of BNF notation
for introducing syntactic objects:

\oc\Delta\ni\overline{s},\overline{t},\overline{u},\overline{v},\overline{w}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left[\right]}\mid{\left[s\right]}\cdot\overline{t}

means that we define the set
\oc\Delta
of resource monomials
as that inductively generated by the empty monomial, and
addition of a term to a monomial, and that we will denote resource monomials
using overlined letters among
\overline{s},\overline{t},\overline{u},\overline{v},\overline{w}
, possibly with
sub- and superscripts.

\begin{array}[]{rclcl}\Delta&\ni&s,t,u,v,w&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}&x\mid\lambda x\,{s}\mid{\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\\
\oc\Delta&\ni&\overline{s},\overline{t},\overline{u},\overline{v},\overline{w}&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}&{\left[\right]}\mid{\left[s\right]}\cdot\overline{t}.\end{array}

Terms are considered up to
\alpha
-equivalence and monomials up to permutativity:
we write
{\left[t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n}\right]}
for
{\left[t_{1}\right]}\cdot{\left(\cdots\cdot{\left({\left[t_{n}\right]}\cdot{\left[\right]}\right)}\right)}

and equate
{\left[t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n}\right]}
with
{\left[t_{f(1)},\dotsc,t_{f(n)}\right]}
for all
permutation
f
of
{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
,
so that resource monomials coincide with finite multisets of resource terms.
7
7

7

Resource monomials are often called
bags
,
bunches
or
poly-terms
in the
literature, but we prefer to strengthen the analogy with power series here.

We will then write
\overline{s}\cdot\overline{t}
for the multiset union of
\overline{s}
and
\overline{t}
,
and
\mathord{\#}{{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right]}}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}n
.

We call
resource expression
any resource term or resource monomial
and write
(\oc)\Delta
for either
\Delta
or
\oc\Delta
:
whenever we use this notation several times in the same context,
all occurrences consistently denote the same set. When we make a definition
or a proof by induction on resource expressions, we actually use a mutual induction
on resource terms and monomials.

Definition
\thethm
.

We define by induction over a resource expression

e\in(\oc)\Delta
, its
size

\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}\in\mathbf{N}

and its
height

\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}\in\mathbf{N}
:

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(x\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}1

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(x\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}1

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\lambda x\,{s}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}1+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s\right)}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\lambda x\,{s}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}1+\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(s\right)}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left({\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}1+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left({\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\max{\left\{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(s\right)},1+\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\right\}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left({\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right]}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s_{i}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left({\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right]}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\max{\left\{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(s_{i}\right)}\mathrel{;}1\leq i\leq n\right\}}.

It should be clear that, for all
e\in(\oc)\Delta
,

\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}\leq\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}
. Also observe that
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s\right)}>0
and
\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(s\right)}>0
for
all
s\in\Delta
, and
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}\geq\mathord{\#}{\overline{s}}
for all
\overline{s}\in\oc\Delta
.
In the application case, we chose not to increment the height of the function:
this is not crucial but it will allow to simplify some of our
computations in Section
6
. In particular,
in the case of a redex we have

\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left({\left\langle\lambda x\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\right)}=1+\max{\left\{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(s\right)},\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\right\}}
.

For all resource expression
e
, we write

\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}
for the set of its free variables.
In the remaining of the paper, we will often have to prove that
some set
\mathcal{E}\subseteq(\oc)\Delta
is finite:
we will generally use the fact that
\mathcal{E}
is finite
iff both
{\left\{\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}\mathrel{;}e\in\mathcal{E}\right\}}
and
\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\mathcal{E}\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\bigcup_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}

are finite.

Besides the size and height of an expression,
we will also need finer grained information on
occurrences of variables, providing a quantitative counterpart to the
set of free variables:

Definition
\thethm
.

We define by induction over resource expressions
the
number
\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(e\right)}\in\mathbf{N}

of occurrences
and the
set
\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(e\right)}\in\mathbf{N}
of
occurrence depths
of a variable
x
in
e\in(\oc)\Delta
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(y\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\begin{cases}1&\text{if $x=y$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(\lambda y\,{s}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(s\right)}

\displaystyle(\text{choosing }y\not=x)

\displaystyle\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left({\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(s\right)}+\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left({\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right]}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(s_{i}\right)}

and

\begin{array}[]{rlr}\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(y\right)}&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\begin{cases}{\left\{1\right\}}&\text{if $x=y$}\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\\
\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(\lambda y\,{s}\right)}&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\{d+1\mathrel{;}d\in\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(s\right)}\right\}}&(\text{choosing }y\not=x)\\
\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left({\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right]}\right)}&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(s_{i}\right)}\\
\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left({\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\right)}&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(s\right)}\mathbin{\cup}{\left\{d+1\mathrel{;}d\in\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\right\}}.\end{array}

We then write
\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(e\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\max\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(e\right)}

for the
maximal depth of occurrences
of
x
in
e
.

Again, it should be clear that

\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(e\right)}\leq\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}
and
\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(e\right)}\leq\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}
.
Moreover,

x\in\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}
iff
\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(e\right)}\not=0
iff
\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(e\right)}\not=\emptyset
iff
\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(e\right)}\not=0
.

3.2.
Partial derivatives

In the resource
\lambda
-calculus, the substitution
e{\left[s/x\right]}
of a term
s
for a variable
x
in
e
admits a
linear counterpart: this operator was initially introduced in the differential

\lambda
-calculus
[
ER03
]
in the form of a partial differentiation operation,
reflecting the interpretation of
\lambda
-terms as analytic maps in quantitative
semantics.

Partial differentiation enforces the introduction of formal finite sums of
resource expressions: these are the actual objects of the resource
\lambda
-calculus,
and in particular the dynamics will act on finite sums of terms rather than on
simple resource terms (see Subsection
3.4
).
We extend all syntactic constructs to finite sums of resource expressions
by linearity: if
\sigma=\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[\Delta\right]}

and
\overline{\tau}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\overline{t}_{j}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[\oc\Delta\right]}
,
we set
\lambda x\,{\sigma}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda x\,{s_{i}}
,

{\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau}}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{p}{\left\langle s_{i}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}_{j}}

and
{\left[\sigma\right]}\cdot{\overline{\tau}}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{p}{\left[s_{i}\right]}\cdot{\overline{t}_{j}}
.

This linearity of syntactic constructs will be generalized to vectors
of resource expressions in the next section.
For now, up to linearity, it is already possible to consider the substitution

e{\left[\sigma/x\right]}
of a finite sum of terms
\sigma
for a variable term
x
in an
expression
e
: in particular
e{\left[0/x\right]}=0
whenever
x\in\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}
.
This is in turn extended to sums by linearity:
\epsilon{\left[\sigma/x\right]}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}{\left[\sigma/x\right]}
when
\epsilon=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}
. Observe that this is
not

linear in
\sigma
, because
x
may occur several times in
e
:
for instance, with a monomial of degree 2,

{\left[x,x\right]}{\left[t+u/x\right]}={\left[t,t\right]}+{\left[t,u\right]}+{\left[u,t\right]}+{\left[u,u\right]}
.

Partial differentiation is then defined as follows:

Definition
\thethm
.

For all
u\in\Delta
and
x\in\mathcal{V}
,
we define the
partial derivative

\frac{\partial e}{\partial x}\cdot u\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}

of
e\in(\oc)\Delta
, by induction on
e
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial y}{\partial x}\cdot u

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\begin{cases}u&\text{if $x=y$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\lambda y\,{s}}{\partial x}\cdot u

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda y\,{\left(\frac{\partial s}{\partial x}\cdot u\right)}

(choosing
y\not\in{\left\{x\right\}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(u\right)}
)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}}{\partial x}\cdot u

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\langle\frac{\partial s}{\partial x}\cdot u\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}+{\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\left(\frac{\partial\overline{t}}{\partial x}\cdot u\right)}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right]}}{\partial x}\cdot u

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,\frac{\partial s_{i}}{\partial x}\cdot u,\dotsc,s_{n}\right]}.

Partial differentiation is extended to
finite sums of expressions by bilinearity: if

\epsilon=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}
and

\sigma=\sum_{j=1}^{p}s_{j}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[\Delta\right]}
,
we set

\frac{\partial\epsilon}{\partial x}\cdot\sigma=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{\partial e_{i}}{\partial x}\cdot s_{j}.

{lemC}

[
[
ER08
, Lemma 2]
]

If
x\not\in\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(u\right)}
then

\frac{\partial}{\partial y}{\left(\frac{\partial e}{\partial x}\cdot t\right)}\cdot u=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}{\left(\frac{\partial e}{\partial y}\cdot u\right)}\cdot t+\frac{\partial e}{\partial x}\cdot{\left(\frac{\partial t}{\partial y}\cdot u\right)}.

If moreover
y\not\in\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(t\right)}
, we obtain a version of Schwarz’s theorem on
the symmetry of second derivatives:

\frac{\partial}{\partial y}{\left(\frac{\partial e}{\partial x}\cdot t\right)}\cdot u=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}{\left(\frac{\partial e}{\partial y}\cdot u\right)}\cdot t.

If
x\not\in\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(s_{i}\right)}
for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
, we write

\frac{\partial^{n}e}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot{\left(s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}{\left(\cdots\frac{\partial e}{\partial x}\cdot s_{1}\cdots\right)}\cdot s_{n}.

More generally, we write

\frac{\partial^{n}e}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot{\left(s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left(\frac{\partial^{n}e{\left[y/x\right]}}{\partial y^{n}}\cdot{\left(s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right)}\right)}{\left[x/y\right]}

for any
y\not\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(s_{i}\right)}\mathbin{\cup}(\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}\setminus{\left\{x\right\}})
: it should
be clear that this definition does not depend on the choice of such a variable
y
.
By the previous lemma,

\frac{\partial^{n}e}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot{\left(s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right)}=\frac{\partial^{n}e}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot{\left(s_{f(1)},\dotsc,s_{f(n)}\right)}

for any permutation
f
of
{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
and we will thus write

\frac{\partial^{n}e}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot\overline{s}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\frac{\partial^{n}e}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot{\left(s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right)}

whenever
\overline{s}={\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right]}
.

An alternative, more direct presentation of iterated partial derivatives
is as follows.
Suppose
\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(e\right)}=m
, and write
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{m}
for the occurrences of
x
in
e
.
Then:

\frac{\partial^{n}e}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right]}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}\to{\left\{1,\dotsc,m\right\}}\\
\text{$f$ injective}\end{subarray}}e{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}/x_{f(1)},\dotsc,x_{f(n)}\right]}

More formally, we obtain:

Lemma
\thethm
.

For all monomial
\overline{u}={\left[u_{1},\dotsc,u_{n}\right]}\in\oc\Delta
and all variable

x\in\mathcal{V}
:
8
8

8

In this definition and in the remaining of the paper, we say a tuple

(I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n})\in\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(I\right)}^{n}
is a partition of
I

if
I=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}I_{k}
, and the
I_{k}
’s are pairwise disjoint.
We do not require the
I_{k}
’s to be nonempty. Hence a partition of
I

into a
n
-tuple is uniquely defined by a function from
I
to
{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
.

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}y}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot\overline{u}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}y&\text{if $n=0$}\\
u_{1}&\text{if $x=y$ and $n=1$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}\lambda y\,{s}}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot\overline{u}

\displaystyle=\lambda y\,{\left(\frac{\partial^{n}s}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot\overline{u}\right)}

(choosing
y\not\in{\left\{x\right\}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\overline{u}\right)}
)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}{\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot\overline{u}

\displaystyle=\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\sum_{(I,J)\text{ partition of }{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}}{\left\langle\frac{\partial^{\mathord{\#}{I}}s}{\partial x^{\mathord{\#}{I}}}\cdot\overline{u}_{I}\right\rangle}\,{\frac{\partial^{\mathord{\#}{J}}\overline{t}}{\partial x^{\mathord{\#}{J}}}\cdot\overline{u}_{J}}$\hss}\hskip 30.00005pt

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{k}\right]}}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot\overline{u}

\displaystyle=\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\sum_{(I_{1},\dotsc,I_{k})\text{ partition of }{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}}{\left[\frac{\partial^{\mathord{\#}{I_{1}}}s_{1}}{\partial x^{\mathord{\#}{I_{1}}}}\cdot\overline{u}_{I_{1}}\dotsc,\frac{\partial^{\mathord{\#}{I_{k}}}s_{k}}{\partial x^{\mathord{\#}{I_{k}}}}\cdot\overline{u}_{I_{k}}\right]}$\hss}\hskip 80.00012pt

where
\overline{u}_{I}
denotes
{{\left[u_{i_{1}},\dotsc,u_{i_{p}}\right]}}

whenever
I={\left\{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{p}\right\}}
with
p=\mathord{\#}{I}
.

Proof.

Easy, by induction on
n
.
∎

Lemma
\thethm
.

For all
e\in(\oc)\Delta
,
\overline{s}\in\oc\Delta
,
x\not=y\in\mathcal{V}

and
{e^{\prime}}\in{\left|\frac{\partial^{n}e}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot\overline{s}\right|}
with
n=\mathord{\#}{\overline{s}}
,
moreover assuming that
x\not\in\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}
:

•

\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(e\right)}\geq n
and
\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}=\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(e\right)}-n
;

•

\mathbf{n}_{y}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}=\mathbf{n}_{y}{\left(e\right)}+\mathbf{n}_{y}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}
;

•

\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}\subseteq\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(e\right)}
;

•

\mathbf{d}_{y}{\left(e\right)}\subseteq\mathbf{d}_{y}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}\subseteq\mathbf{d}_{y}{\left(e\right)}\mathbin{\cup}{\left\{d+d^{\prime}-1\mathrel{;}d\in\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(e\right)},d^{\prime}\in\mathbf{d}_{y}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}\right\}}
;

•

\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}=\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}-n
;

•

\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}\leq\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}\leq\max{\left\{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)},\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(e\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}-1\right\}}
.

Proof.

Each result is easily established by induction on
e
,
using the previous lemma to enable the induction.
∎

3.3.
Multilinear substitution

Recall that Taylor expansion involves iterated derivatives at
0
.
If
n=\mathord{\#}{\overline{s}}
and
x\not\in\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}
we write

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{s}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left(\frac{\partial^{n}e}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot\overline{s}\right)}{\left[0/x\right]}.

Observe that by Lemma
3.2
: if
n>\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(e\right)}
then
\frac{\partial^{n}e}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot\overline{s}=0
;
and if
n<\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(e\right)}
then
x\in\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}
for all
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\frac{\partial^{n}e}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot\overline{s}\right|}
,
and then
e^{\prime}{\left[0/x\right]}=0
.
In other words,

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{s}=\begin{cases}\frac{\partial^{n}e}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot\overline{s}&\text{if $n=\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(e\right)}$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\quad.

We say
\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{s}
is the
n
-linear substitution

of
\overline{s}
for
x
in
e
.
More generally, we write

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{s}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left(\partial_{y}e{\left[y/x\right]}\cdot\overline{s}\right)}{\left[x/y\right]}

for any
y\not\in\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}\mathbin{\cup}(\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}\setminus x)
and it should again
be clear that this definition does not depend of the choice of such a
y
.
By a straightforward application of Lemma
3.2
, we obtain:

Lemma
\thethm
.

For all
e\in(\oc)\Delta
,
\overline{s}\in\oc\Delta
,
x\not=y\in\mathcal{V}

and
{e^{\prime}}\in{\left|\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{s}\right|}
, assuming
x\not\in\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}
:

•

\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(e\right)}=\mathord{\#}{\overline{s}}
and
\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}=0
;

•

\mathbf{n}_{y}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}=\mathbf{n}_{y}{\left(e\right)}+\mathbf{n}_{y}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}
;

•

\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}=\emptyset
;

•

\mathbf{d}_{y}{\left(e\right)}\subseteq\mathbf{d}_{y}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}\subseteq\mathbf{d}_{y}{\left(e\right)}\mathbin{\cup}{\left\{d+d^{\prime}-1\mathrel{;}d\in\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(e\right)},d^{\prime}\in\mathbf{d}_{y}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}\right\}}
;

•

\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}=\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}-\mathord{\#}{\overline{s}}
;

•

\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}\leq\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}\leq\max{\left\{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)},\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(e\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}-1\right\}}
.

In particular,
\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}=(\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}\setminus{\left\{x\right\}})\mathbin{\cup}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}
, and

\max{\left\{\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)},\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}\right\}}\leq\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}\leq\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}
.

Again, we can give a direct presentation of multilinear substitution.
Suppose
\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(e\right)}=m
, and
write
x_{1},\dotsc,x_{m}
for the occurrences of
x
in
e
. Then:

\partial_{x}e\cdot{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right]}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}\to{\left\{1,\dotsc,m\right\}}\\
\text{$f$ bijective}\end{subarray}}e{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}/x_{f(1)},\dotsc,x_{f(n)}\right]}.

More formally, as a consequence of Lemma
3.2
:

Lemma
\thethm
.

For all monomial
\overline{u}={\left[u_{1},\dotsc,u_{n}\right]}\in\oc\Delta
and all variable

x\in\mathcal{V}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{x}y\cdot\overline{u}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}y&\text{if $y\not=x$ and $n=0$}\\
u_{1}&\text{if $y=x$ and $n=1$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\lambda y\,{s}\cdot\overline{u}

\displaystyle=\lambda y\,{\left(\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}\right)}

(choosing
y\not\in{\left\{x\right\}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\overline{u}\right)}
)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}{\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\cdot\overline{u}

\displaystyle=\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(I,J)\text{ partition of }{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}\\
\text{ s.t. }\mathord{\#}{I}=\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(s\right)}\text{ and }\mathord{\#}{J}=\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\end{subarray}}{\left\langle\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}_{I}\right\rangle}\,{\partial_{x}\overline{t}\cdot\overline{u}_{J}}$\hss}

\displaystyle\partial_{x}{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{k}\right]}\cdot\overline{u}

\displaystyle=\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(I_{1},\dotsc,I_{k})\text{ partition of }{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}\\
{\text{ s.t. }\forall j,\ \mathord{\#}{I_{j}}=\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(s_{j}\right)}}\end{subarray}}{\left[{\partial_{x}s_{1}\cdot\overline{u}_{I_{1}}},\dotsc,{\partial_{x}s_{k}\cdot\overline{u}_{I_{k}}}\right]}$\hss}

where the conditions on cardinalities of subsets of
{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}

in the application and monomial cases may be omitted.

A similar result is the commutation of multilinear substitutions:

Lemma
\thethm
.

If
x\not\in\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\overline{u}\right)}
then:

\partial_{y}{\left(\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{t}\right)}\cdot\overline{u}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(I,J)\text{ partition of }{\left\{1,\dotsc,\mathord{\#}{\overline{u}}\right\}}\\
\text{ s.t. }\mathord{\#}{I}=\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(e\right)}\text{ and }\mathord{\#}{J}=\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\end{subarray}}\partial_{x}{\left(\partial_{y}e\cdot\overline{u}_{I}\right)}\cdot{\left(\partial_{y}\overline{t}\cdot\overline{u}_{J}\right)}.

Proof.

Write
n=\mathord{\#}{\overline{t}}
and
p=\mathord{\#}{\overline{u}}
.
It is sufficient to prove

\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial y^{p}}{\left(\frac{\partial^{n}e}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot\overline{t}\right)}\cdot\overline{u}=\sum_{(I,J)\text{ partition of }{\left\{1,\dotsc,p\right\}}}\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial x^{n}}{\left(\frac{\partial^{\mathord{\#}{I}}s}{\partial y^{\mathord{\#}{I}}}\cdot\overline{u}_{I}\right)}\cdot{\left(\frac{\partial^{\mathord{\#}{J}}\overline{t}}{\partial y^{\mathord{\#}{J}}}\cdot\overline{u}_{J}\right)}

by induction on
n
and
p
, using Lemma
3.2
.
∎

3.4.
Resource reduction

If
\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}
is a reduction relation, we will write
\mathrel{{\rightarrow^{?}}}
(resp.

\mathrel{{\rightarrow^{+}}}
;
\mathrel{{\rightarrow^{*}}}
) for its reflexive (resp. transitive; reflexive and
transitive) closure.

In the resource
\lambda
-calculus, a redex is a term of the form

{\left\langle\lambda x\,{t}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{u}}\in\Delta
and its reduct is

\partial_{x}t\cdot\overline{u}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[\Delta\right]}
.
The resource reduction
\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}
is then the contextual closure
of this reduction step on finite sums of resource expressions. More precisely:

Definition
\thethm
.

We define the
resource reduction
relation

\mathord{\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}}\subseteq(\oc)\Delta\times\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}

inductively as follows:

•

{\left\langle\lambda x\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{t}
for all
s\in\Delta

and
\overline{t}\in\oc\Delta
;

•

\lambda x\,{s}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\lambda x\,{\sigma^{\prime}}
as soon as
s\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\sigma^{\prime}
;

•

{\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}{\left\langle\sigma^{\prime}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}
as soon as
s\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\sigma^{\prime}
;

•

{\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}{\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau}^{\prime}}
as soon as
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\overline{\tau}^{\prime}
;

•

{\left[s\right]}\cdot\overline{t}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}{\left[\sigma^{\prime}\right]}\cdot\overline{t}
as soon as
s\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\sigma^{\prime}
.

We extend this reduction to finite sums of resource expressions:
write
\epsilon\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
if
\epsilon=\sum_{i=0}^{n}e_{i}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}

with
e_{0}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{0}
and, for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
,

e_{i}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{?}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
.

Observe that we allow for parallel reduction of any nonzero number of summands in a finite sum.
This reduction is particularly well behaved. In particular, it is confluent in a strong sense:

Lemma
\thethm
.

For all
\epsilon,\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{1}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}
,
if
\epsilon\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon_{0}
and
\epsilon\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon_{1}
then
there is
\epsilon^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}
such
that
\epsilon_{0}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{?}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
and
\epsilon_{1}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{?}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The proof follows a well-trodden path for proving confluence.

One first proves by induction on
s
that if
s\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\sigma^{\prime}

then
\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{t}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{?}}}\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{t}
,
and if
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\overline{\tau}^{\prime}

then
\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{t}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{?}}}\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{\tau}^{\prime}
.
Note that the reflexive closure is made necessary by
the possibility that
\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{t}=0
,
and the transitive closure is not needed because
there is no duplication of the redexes of
\overline{t}
in the
summands of the multilinear substitution
\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{t}
.

One then proves that if
e\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon_{0}
and
e\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon_{1}
then
there is
\epsilon^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}
such
that
\epsilon_{0}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{?}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
and
\epsilon_{1}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{?}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
.
The proof is straightforward, by induction on the pair of reductions

e\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon_{0}
and
e\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon_{1}
, using the previous
result in case
e
is a redex which is reduced in

\epsilon_{0}
but not in
\epsilon_{1}
(or
vice versa
).
∎

In other words,
\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{?}}}
enjoys the diamond property.
9
9

9

This strong confluence result was not mentioned
in Ehrhard and Regnier’s papers about resource
\lambda
-calculus

[
ER08
,
ER06
]
but they established
a very similar result for differential nets
[
ER05
, Section 4]
:
Lemma
3.4
can be understood as a
reformulation of the latter in the setting of resource calculus.

Moreover, the effect of reduction on the size of terms is very regular.
First introduce some useful notation:
write
e\succ_{\partial}e^{\prime}
if
e\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
with
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}\right|}
.

Lemma
\thethm
.

Let
e\succ_{\partial}e^{\prime}
.
Then
\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}=\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}
, and
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}+2\leq\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}\leq 2\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}+2
.

Proof.

By induction on the reduction
e\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
with
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}\right|}
.
The inductive contextuality cases are easy,
and we only detail the base case,
i.e.

e={\left\langle\lambda x\,{t}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{u}}

and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\partial_{x}t\cdot\overline{u}
.

Write
n=\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(t\right)}
.
The result then follows from Lemma
3.3
, observing that

\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}=\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(t\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{u}\right)}-n=\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}-2-n
and
n\leq\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{u}\right)}\leq\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}
.
∎

We will write
\geq_{\partial}
(resp.
>_{\partial}
) for
\succ_{\partial}^{*}

(resp.
\succ_{\partial}^{+}
).
Observe that
e\geq_{\partial}e^{\prime}
(resp.
e>_{\partial}e^{\prime}
)
iff there is
\epsilon^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}
such that

e^{\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}\right|}
and
e\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{*}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
(resp.
e\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{+}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
).
Moreover,
{\left\{e^{\prime}\mathrel{;}e\geq_{\partial}e^{\prime}\right\}}
is always finite and

>_{\partial}
defines a well-founded strict partial order.
A direct consequence is that
\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}
always converges
to a unique normal form:

Lemma
\thethm
.

The reduction
\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}
is confluent and strongly normalizing.
Moreover, for all
\epsilon\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}
, the set
{\left\{\epsilon^{\prime}\mathrel{;}\epsilon\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{*}}}\epsilon^{\prime}\right\}}

is finite.

Proof.

Confluence is a consequence of Lemma
3.4
.
By Lemma
3.4
,
the transitive closure
\succ_{\partial}^{+}

is a well-founded strict partial order.
Observe that the elements of
\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}

can be considered as finite multisets of resource expressions:
then
\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{+}}}
is included in the multiset ordering induced by

\succ_{\partial}^{+}
, and
it follows that
\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{+}}}
defines a well-founded strict partial order
on
\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}
,
i.e.

\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}
is strongly
normalizing.

The final property follows from strong normalizability applying König’s lemma
to the tree of possible reductions, observing that each
\epsilon
has finitely
many
\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}
-reducts.
∎

If
\epsilon\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}
, we then write
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}
for the
unique sum of normal resource expressions such that
\epsilon\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{*}}}\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}
.
A consequence of the previous lemma is that any reduction discipline
reaches this normal form:

Corollary
\thethm
.

Let
\mathord{\to}\subseteq\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}\times\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}

be such that
\mathord{\to}\subseteq\mathord{\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{*}}}}
. Moreover assume
that, for all non normal
\epsilon\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}

there is
\epsilon^{\prime}\not=\epsilon
such that
\epsilon\to\epsilon^{\prime}
.
Then
\epsilon\to^{*}\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}
for all
\epsilon\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}
.

4.
Vectors of resource expressions and Taylor expansion of algebraic
\lambda
-terms

4.1.
Resource vectors

A vector
\sigma=\sum_{s\in\Delta}\sigma_{s}.s
of
resource terms will be called a
term vector
whenever its set of free
variables
\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\sigma\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\bigcup_{s\in{\left|\sigma\right|}}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(s\right)}
is finite. Similarly,
we will call
\emph{monomialvector}
any vector of resource monomials
whose set of free variables is finite.
We will abuse notation and write
\mathbf{S}^{\Delta}
for the set of term vectors and

\mathbf{S}^{\oc\Delta}
for the set of monomial vectors.
10
10

10

The restriction to vectors with finitely many free variables is purely technical.
For instance, it allows us to assume that a sum of abstractions

\sigma=\sum_{i\in I}\lambda x_{i}\,{s_{i}}
can always use a common abstracted
variable:
\sigma=\sum_{i\in I}\lambda x\,{\left(s_{i}{\left[x/x_{i}\right]}\right)}
, with

x\not\in\mathbin{\cup}_{i\in I}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\lambda x_{i}\,{s_{i}}\right)}
.
Working without this restriction would
only lead to more contorted statements and tedious bookkeeping:
consider,
e.g.
, what would happen to the definition of the substitution of a term vector for a
variable (Definition
4.3
),
especially the abstraction case.

A
resource vector
will be any of a term vector or a monomial vector,
and we will write
\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
for either
\mathbf{S}^{\Delta}
or
\mathbf{S}^{\oc\Delta}
:
as for resource expressions, whenever we use this notation several times in the same context,
all occurrences consistently denote the same set.

The syntactic constructs are extended to resource vectors by linearity:
for all
\sigma\in\mathbf{S}^{\Delta}
and
\overline{\sigma},\overline{\tau}\in\mathbf{S}^{\oc\Delta}
, we set

\displaystyle\lambda x\,{\sigma}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\Delta}{\sigma}_{s}.\lambda x\,{s},

\displaystyle{\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\Delta,\overline{t}\in\oc\Delta}{\sigma}_{s}{\overline{\tau}}_{\overline{t}}.{\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}},

\displaystyle\text{ and }{\left[\sigma_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{n}\right]}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\in\Delta}(\sigma_{1})_{s_{1}}\cdots(\sigma_{n})_{s_{n}}.{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right]}.

This poses no problem for finite vectors:
e.g.
, if
{\left|\sigma\right|}

is finite then finitely many of the vectors
\sigma_{s}.\lambda x\,{s}
are non-zero,
hence the sum is finite. In the general case, however,
we actually need to prove that the above sums are well defined:
the constructors of the calculus define summable functions,
which thus extend to multilinear-continuous maps.
11
11

11

The one-to-one correspondence between summable
n
-ary
functions and multilinear-continuous maps was established
for semimodules of the form
\mathbf{S}^{X}
,
i.e.
the semimodules
of all vectors on a fixed set. Due to the restriction we put on free
variables,
\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
is not of this form: it should rather
be written
\bigcup_{V\in\mathord{\mathfrak{P}_{f}}{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}}\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta_{V}}

where
(\oc)\Delta_{V}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\{e\in(\oc)\Delta\mathrel{;}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}\subseteq V\right\}}
.
So when we say a function is multilinear-continuous on
\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
,
we actually mean that its restriction to each
\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta_{V}}
with

V\in\mathord{\mathfrak{P}_{f}}{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}
is multilinear-continuous.
In the present case, keeping this precision implicit is quite innocuous,
but we will be more careful when considering the restriction to bounded vectors
in Subsection
6.4
, and to normalizable vectors in
Section
8
.

Lemma
\thethm
.

The following families of vectors are summable:

{\left(\lambda x\,{s}\right)}_{s\in\Delta},\quad{\left({\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\right)}_{s\in\Delta,\overline{t}\in\oc\Delta},\quad{\left({\left[s\right]}\right)}_{s\in\Delta}\quad\text{and}\quad{\left({\overline{s}\cdot{\overline{t}}}\right)}_{\overline{s},\overline{t}\in\oc\Delta}.

Proof.

The proof is direct, but we detail it if only to make the requirements explicit.

For all
u\in\Delta
there is at most one
s
such that

u\in{\left|\lambda x\,{s}\right|}
(in which case
u=\lambda x\,{s}
) and at most one pair
(s,\overline{t})
such that

u\in{\left|{\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\right|}
(in which case
u={\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}
).

For all
\overline{u}\in\oc\Delta
there is at most one
s
such that

\overline{u}\in{\left|{\left[s\right]}\right|}
(in which case
\overline{u}={\left[s\right]}
),
and there are finitely many
\overline{s}
and
\overline{t}
such that

\overline{u}\in{\left|{\overline{s}\cdot{\overline{t}}}\right|}

(those such that
\overline{u}=\overline{s}\cdot\overline{t}
).
∎

For each term vector
\sigma
, we then write
\sigma^{n}
for the monomial vector

{[\overbrace{\sigma,\dotsc,\sigma}^{\text{$n$ times}}]}.

4.2.
Partial differentiation of resource vectors.

We can extend partial derivatives to vectors by linear-continuity
(recall that, via the unique semiring morphism from
\mathbf{N}
to
\mathbf{S}
, we
can consider that
\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}\subseteq\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
).

Lemma
\thethm
.

The function

\displaystyle(\oc)\Delta\times\oc\Delta

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}

\displaystyle(e,{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right]})

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}e}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right]}

is summable.

Proof.

Let
