e^{\prime}\in(\oc)\Delta
and assume that

e^{\prime}\in{\left|\frac{\partial^{n}e}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot\overline{s}\right|}
with
\mathord{\#}{\overline{s}}=n
.
By Lemma
3.2
,
\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}\subseteq\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}\mathbin{\cup}{\left\{x\right\}}
,

\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}\subseteq\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}
,
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}\leq\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}
and
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{s}\right)}\leq\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}
:

e^{\prime}
being fixed, there are finitely many
(e,\overline{s})
satisfying these constraints.
∎

The characterization of iterated partial derivatives given in Lemma

3.2
extends directly to resource vectors, by
the linear-continuity of syntactic constructs and partial derivatives.
For instance, given term vectors
\sigma,\rho_{1},\dotsc,\rho_{n}\in\mathbf{S}^{\Delta}
and a
monomial vector
\overline{\tau}\in\mathbf{S}^{\oc\Delta}
, we obtain:

\frac{\partial^{n}{\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau}}}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot{\left[\rho_{1},\dotsc,\rho_{n}\right]}=\sum_{(I,J)\text{ partition of }{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}}{\left\langle\frac{\partial^{\mathord{\#}{I}}\sigma}{\partial x^{\mathord{\#}{I}}}\cdot\overline{\rho}_{I}\right\rangle}\,{\frac{\partial^{\mathord{\#}{J}}\overline{\tau}}{\partial x^{\mathord{\#}{J}}}\cdot\overline{\rho}_{J}}.

Now we can consider iterated differentiation along a fixed term vector
\rho
:

\frac{\partial^{n}\epsilon}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot\rho^{n}
. We obtain:

Lemma
\thethm
.

For all
\sigma,\tau_{1},\dotsc,\tau_{n},\rho\in\mathbf{S}^{\Delta}
and
all
\overline{\tau}\in\mathbf{S}^{\oc\Delta}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{k}{\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau}}}{\partial x^{k}}\cdot\rho^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{k}{\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{l,k-l}\right]}{\left\langle\frac{\partial^{l}\sigma}{\partial x^{l}}\cdot\rho^{l}\right\rangle}\,{\frac{\partial^{k-l}\overline{\tau}}{\partial x^{k-l}}\cdot\rho^{k-l}}\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{k}{\left[\tau_{1},\dotsc,\tau_{n}\right]}}{\partial x^{k}}\cdot\rho^{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},\dotsc,k_{n}\in\mathbf{N}\\
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}=k\end{subarray}}{\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{k_1,\dotsc,k_n}\right]}{\left[\frac{\partial^{k_{1}}\tau_{1}}{\partial x^{k_{1}}}\cdot\rho^{k_{1}},\dotsc,\frac{\partial^{k_{n}}\tau_{n}}{\partial x^{k_{n}}}\cdot\rho^{k_{n}}\right]}.

Proof.

First recall that, if
k=\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}
,
the
multinomial coefficient

{\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{k_1,\dotsc,k_n}\right]}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\frac{k!}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}!}

is nothing but the number of partitions of
{\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}}
into
n
sets

I_{1},\dotsc,I_{n}
such that
\mathord{\#}{I}_{j}=k_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq n

[
DLMF
, §26.4]
.
Then both results derive directly from Lemma
3.2
.
∎

4.3.
Substitutions

Since
{\left|\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{s}\right|}\subseteq{\left|\frac{\partial^{n}e}{\partial x^{n}}\cdot\overline{s}\right|}
,
multilinear substitution also defines a summable binary function
and we will write

\partial_{x}\epsilon\cdot\overline{\sigma}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\sum_{e\in(\oc)\Delta,\overline{s}\in\oc\Delta}\epsilon_{e}{\overline{\sigma}}_{\overline{s}}.\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{s}.

By contrast with partial derivatives, the usual substitution is not linear, so
the substitution of resource vectors must be defined directly.

Definition
\thethm
.

We define by induction over resource expressions
the
substitution

e{\left[\sigma/x\right]}\in\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
of

\sigma\in\mathbf{S}^{\Delta}
for a variable
x
in
e\in(\oc)\Delta
:

\displaystyle x{\left[\sigma/x\right]}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\begin{cases}\sigma&\text{if $x=y$}\\
y&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle{\left(\lambda y\,{s}\right)}{\left[\sigma/x\right]}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda y\,{s{\left[\sigma/x\right]}}

(choosing
y\not\in\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\sigma\right)}\mathbin{\cup}{\left\{x\right\}}
)

\displaystyle{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right]}{\left[\sigma/x\right]}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left[s_{1}{\left[\sigma/x\right]},\dotsc,s_{n}{\left[\sigma/x\right]}\right]}

\displaystyle{\left({\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\right)}{\left[\sigma/x\right]}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\langle s{\left[\sigma/x\right]}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}{\left[\sigma/x\right]}}

Lemma
\thethm
.

For all
e\in(\oc)\Delta
,
x\in\mathcal{V}
and
\sigma\in\mathbf{S}^{\Delta}
:

•

if
\sigma\in\Delta
then
e{\left[\sigma/x\right]}\in\Delta
;

•

if
\sigma\in\mathbf{S}{\left[\Delta\right]}
then
e{\left[\sigma/x\right]}\in\mathbf{S}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}
;

•

if
x\not\in\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}
then
e{\left[\sigma/x\right]}=e
;

•

if
x\in\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}
then
e{\left[0/x\right]}=0
;

•

for all
e^{\prime}\in{\left|e{\left[\sigma/x\right]}\right|}
,

\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}\setminus{\left\{x\right\}}\subseteq\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}\subseteq{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}\setminus{\left\{x\right\}}\right)}\mathbin{\cup}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\sigma\right)}

and
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}\geq\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}
.

Proof.

Each statement follows easily by induction on
e
.
∎

A consequence of the last item is that the function

\displaystyle(\oc)\Delta

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle e{\left[\sigma/x\right]}

is summable: we thus write

\epsilon{\left[\sigma/x\right]}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\sum_{e\in\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}}{\epsilon}_{e}.e{\left[\sigma/x\right]}.

4.4.
Promotion

Observe that the family
{\left(\sigma^{n}\right)}_{n\in\mathbf{N}}

is summable because the supports
{\left|\sigma^{n}\right|}
for
n\in\mathbf{N}
are
pairwise disjoint.
We then define the
promotion
of
\sigma
as

{\sigma}^{\oc}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}\frac{1}{n!}.\sigma^{n}
.

For this definition to make sense, we need inverses of natural numbers to be available:
we say
\mathbf{S}

has fractions
if every
n\in\mathbf{N}\setminus{\left\{0\right\}}

admits a multiplicative inverse in
\mathbf{S}
.
This inverse is necessarily unique and we write it
\frac{1}{n}
.
Observe that
\mathbf{S}
has fractions iff there is a semiring morphism
from the semiring
\mathbf{Q}^{+}
of non-negative rational numbers
to
\mathbf{S}
, and then this morphism is unique, but not necessarily injective:
consider the semiring
\mathbf{B}
of booleans.

Semifields
,
i.e.
commutative semirings in which every non-zero element
admits an inverse, obviously have fractions:

\mathbf{Q}^{+}
and
\mathbf{B}
are actually semifields.
In the following, we will keep this requirement implicit:
whenever we use quotients by natural numbers,
it means we assume
\mathbf{S}
has fractions.

Lemma
\thethm
.

For all
\sigma
and
\tau\in\mathbf{S}^{\Delta}
,

{\sigma}^{\oc}{\left[\tau/x\right]}={\left(\sigma{\left[\tau/x\right]}\right)}^{\oc}
.

Proof.

By the linear-continuity of

\epsilon\mapsto\epsilon{\left[\sigma/x\right]}
, it is sufficient
to prove that

\sigma^{n}{\left[\tau/x\right]}={\left(\sigma{\left[\tau/x\right]}\right)}^{n}

which follows from the
n
-linear-continuity of

(\sigma_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{n})\mapsto{\left[\sigma_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{n}\right]}

and the definition of substitution.
∎

Lemma
\thethm
.

The following identities hold:

\displaystyle\partial_{x}x\cdot{\rho}^{\oc}

\displaystyle=\rho

\displaystyle\partial_{x}y\cdot{\rho}^{\oc}

\displaystyle=y

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\lambda y\,{\sigma}\cdot{\rho}^{\oc}

\displaystyle=\lambda y\,{\left(\partial_{x}\sigma\cdot{\rho}^{\oc}\right)}

(choosing
y\not\in{\left\{x\right\}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\rho\right)}
)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}{\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau}}\cdot{\rho}^{\oc}

\displaystyle={\left\langle\partial_{x}\sigma\cdot{\rho}^{\oc}\right\rangle}\,{\partial_{x}\overline{\tau}\cdot{\rho}^{\oc}}

\displaystyle\partial_{x}{\left[\sigma_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{n}\right]}\cdot{\rho}^{\oc}

\displaystyle={\left[\partial_{x}\sigma_{1}\cdot{\rho}^{\oc},\dotsc,\partial_{x}\sigma_{n}\cdot{\rho}^{\oc}\right]}

Proof.

Since each syntactic constructor is multilinear-continuous,
it is sufficient to consider the case of
\partial_{x}e\cdot{\rho}^{\oc}

for a resource expression
e\in(\oc)\Delta
.
First observe that, if
k=\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(e\right)}
then

\partial_{x}e\cdot{\rho}^{\oc}=\frac{1}{k!}.{\frac{\partial^{k}e}{\partial x^{k}}\cdot\rho^{k}}
.
In particular the case of variables is straightforward.

The case of abstractions follows directly, since

\frac{\partial^{k}\lambda x\,{s}}{\partial x^{k}}\cdot\rho^{k}=\lambda x\,{\left(\frac{\partial^{k}s}{\partial x^{k}}\cdot\rho^{k}\right)}
.

If
e={\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}
, write
l=\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(s\right)}
and
m=\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}
.
It follows from Lemma
4.2
that

\partial_{x}e\cdot\rho^{k}={\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{l,m}\right]}.{\left\langle\partial_{x}s\cdot\rho^{l}\right\rangle}\,{\partial_{x}\overline{t}\cdot\rho^{m}}

and then

\frac{1}{k!}.\partial_{x}e\cdot\rho^{k}={\left\langle\frac{1}{l!}.\partial_{x}s\cdot\rho^{l}\right\rangle}\,{\frac{1}{m!}.\partial_{x}\overline{t}\cdot\rho^{m}}
.

Similarly, if
e={\left[t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n}\right]}
,
write
k_{i}=\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(t_{i}\right)}
for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
.
It follows from Lemma
4.2
that

\partial_{x}e\cdot\rho^{k}={\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k}{k_1,\dotsc,k_n}\right]}.{\left[\partial_{x}t_{1}\cdot\rho^{k_{1}},\dotsc,\partial_{x}t_{n}\cdot\rho^{k_{n}}\right]}

and then

\frac{1}{k!}.\partial_{x}e\cdot\rho^{k}={\left[\frac{1}{k_{1}!}\partial_{x}t_{1}\cdot\rho^{k_{1}},\dotsc,\frac{1}{k_{n}!}\partial_{x}t_{n}\cdot\rho^{k_{n}}\right]}
.
∎

Lemma
\thethm
.

For all
\epsilon\in\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
an
\sigma\in\mathbf{S}^{\Delta}
,

\epsilon{\left[\sigma/x\right]}=\partial_{x}\epsilon\cdot{\sigma}^{\oc}.

Proof.

By the linear-continuity of

\epsilon\mapsto\partial_{x}\epsilon\cdot{\sigma}^{\oc}
and

\epsilon\mapsto\epsilon{\left[\sigma/x\right]}
,
it is sufficient to show that

e{\left[\sigma/x\right]}=\partial_{x}e\cdot{\sigma}^{\oc}

for all resource expression
e
.
The proof is then by induction on
e
,
using the previous Lemma in each case.
∎

By Lemma
4.4
,
we thus obtain

\partial_{x}{\sigma}^{\oc}\cdot{\tau}^{\oc}={\left(\partial_{x}\sigma\cdot{\tau}^{\oc}\right)}^{\oc}

which can be seen as a counterpart of the functoriality
of promotion in linear logic.
To our knowledge it is the first published proof of such a result
for resource vectors. This will enable us to prove the
commutation of Taylor expansion and substitution
(Lemma
4.5
), another unsurprising
yet non-trivial result.

4.5.
Taylor expansion of algebraic
\lambda
-terms

Since resource vectors form a module, there is no reason to restrict the source
language of Taylor expansion to the pure
\lambda
-calculus: we can consider formal
finite linear combinations of
\lambda
-terms.

We will thus consider the terms given by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rclcl}\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}&\ni&M,N,P&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}&x\mid\lambda x\,{M}\mid{\left(M\right)}\,{N}\mid 0\mid a.M\mid M+N\end{array}

where
a
ranges in
\mathbf{S}
.
12
12

12

We follow Krivine’s convention
[
Kri90
]
,
by writing
{\left(M\right)}\,{N}
for the application of term
M
to term
N
.
We more generally write
{\left(M\right)}\,{N_{1}\cdots N_{k}}
for
{\left(\cdots{\left(M\right)}\,{N_{1}}\cdots\right)}\,{N_{k}}
.
Moreover, among term constructors, we give sums the lowest priority so that

{\left(M\right)}\,{N}+P
should be read as
{\left({\left(M\right)}\,{N}\right)}+P
rather than
{\left(M\right)}\,{{\left(N+P\right)}}
.

For now, terms are considered up to the usual
\alpha
-equivalence only:
the null term
0
, scalar multiplication
a.M
and sum of terms

M+N
are purely syntactic constructs.

Definition
\thethm
.

We define the
Taylor expansion

\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\in\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}

of a term
M\in\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}
inductively as follows:

\displaystyle\mathord{\tau}{\left(x\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}x

\displaystyle\mathord{\tau}{\left(0\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}0

\displaystyle\mathord{\tau}{\left(\lambda x\,{M}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda x\,{\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\tau}{\left(a.M\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}a.\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}

\displaystyle\mathord{\tau}{\left({\left(M\right)}\,{N}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\langle\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right\rangle}\,{{\mathord{\tau}{\left(N\right)}}^{\oc}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\tau}{\left(M+N\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}+\mathord{\tau}{\left(N\right)}.

Lemma
\thethm
.

For all
M,N\in\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}
, and all variable
x
,

\mathord{\tau}{\left(M{\left[N/x\right]}\right)}=\partial_{x}\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\cdot{\mathord{\tau}{\left(N\right)}}^{\oc}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}{\left[\mathord{\tau}{\left(N\right)}/x\right]}.

Proof.

By induction on
M
,
using Lemmas
4.4

and
4.4
.
∎

Let us insist on the fact that, despite its very simple and unsurprising
statement, the previous lemma relies on the entire technical development
of the previous subsections. Again, to our knowledge, it
is the first proof that Taylor expansion commutes with substitution, in an
untyped and non-uniform setting, without any additional assumption.

By contrast, one can forget everything about the semiring of coefficients
and consider only the support of Taylor expansion.
Recall that
\mathbf{B}
denotes the semiring of booleans.
Then we can consider that

\mathbf{B}^{(\oc)\Delta}=\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left((\oc)\Delta\right)}

and write,
e.g.
,
\lambda x\,{\mathcal{S}}={\left\{\lambda x\,{s}\mathrel{;}s\in\mathcal{S}\right\}}
for all set
\mathcal{S}
of
resource terms.

Definition
\thethm
.

The
Taylor support

\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\subseteq\Delta

of
M\in\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}
is defined inductively as follows:
13
13

13

One might be tempted to make an exception in
case
a=0
and set
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(0.M\right)}=\emptyset

but this would only complicate the definition and further developments
for little benefit: what about
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(a.M+b.M\right)}

(resp.
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(a.b.M\right)}
) in a semiring where

a\not=0
,
b\not=0
and
a+b=0
(resp.
ab=0
)?
If we try and cope with those too,
we are led to make
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}
invariant under the equations
of
\mathbf{S}
-module, which is precisely what we want to avoid here:
see the case of
\mathord{\tau}
in the remaining of the present section.

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(x\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\{x\right\}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(0\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(\lambda x\,{M}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda x\,{\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(a.M\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left({\left(M\right)}\,{N}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\langle\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\right\rangle}\,{{\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(N\right)}}^{\oc}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M+N\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\mathbin{\cup}\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(N\right)}.

It should be clear that
{\left|\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right|}\subseteq\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}
,
but the inclusion might be strict,
if only because
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(0.M\right)}=\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}
.
By contrast with the technicality of the previous subsection,
the following
qualitative
analogue of Lemma
4.5

is easily established:

Lemma
\thethm
.

For all
M,N\in\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}
, and all variable
x
,

\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M{\left[N/x\right]}\right)}=\partial_{x}\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\cdot{\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(N\right)}}^{\oc}=\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}{\left[\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(N\right)}/x\right]}.

Proof.

The qualitative version of Lemma

4.4

is straightforward.
The result follows by induction on
M
.
∎

The restriction of
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}
to the set
\Lambda
of pure
\lambda
-terms
was used by Ehrhard and Regnier
[
ER08
]

in their study of Taylor expansion. They showed
that if
M\in\Lambda
then
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}
is uniform:
all the resource terms in
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}
have
the same outermost syntactic construct and this property is preserved
inductively on subterms. They moreover proved that
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}
, and in fact
M
itself, is
entirely characterized by
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}
: in this case,

\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}=\sum_{s\in\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}}\frac{1}{m(s)}s

where
m(s)
is an integer coefficient depending only on
s
.
Of course this property fails in the non uniform setting of
\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}
.

Now, let us consider the equivalence induced on terms by Taylor expansion:
write
M\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}N
if
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(N\right)}
.

Lemma
\thethm
.

The following equations hold:

\displaystyle 0+M

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}M

\displaystyle M+N

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}N+M

\displaystyle(M+N)+P

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}M+(N+P)

\displaystyle 0.M

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}0

\displaystyle 1.M

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}M

\displaystyle a.M+b.M

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}(a+b).M

\displaystyle a.0

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}0

\displaystyle a.(b.M)

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}(ab).M

\displaystyle a.(M+N)

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}a.M+a.N

\displaystyle\lambda x\,{0}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}0

\displaystyle\lambda x\,{\left(a.M\right)}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}a.\lambda x\,{M}

\displaystyle\lambda x\,{\left(M+N\right)}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}\lambda x\,{M}+\lambda x\,{N}

\displaystyle{\left(0\right)}\,{P}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}0

\displaystyle{\left(a.M\right)}\,{P}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}a.{\left(M\right)}\,{P}

\displaystyle{\left(M+N\right)}\,{P}

\displaystyle\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}{\left(M\right)}\,{P}+{\left(N\right)}\,{P}

Moreover,
\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}
is compatible with syntactic constructs:
if
M\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}M^{\prime}
then
\lambda x\,{M}\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}\lambda x\,{M}^{\prime}
,
{\left(M\right)}\,{N}\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}\,{N}
,
{\left(N\right)}\,{M}\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}{\left(N\right)}\,{M^{\prime}}
,
a.M\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}a.M^{\prime}
,
M+N\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}M^{\prime}+N
and
N+M\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}N+M^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Up to Taylor expansion, these equations reflect the
fact that
\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
forms a semimodule (first three lines), and
that all the constructions used in the definition
of
\mathord{\tau}
are multilinear-continuous, except for promotion
(last two lines). Compatibility
follows from the inductive definition of
\mathord{\tau}
.
∎

Let us write
\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}
for the least compatible equivalence relation containing
the equations of the previous lemma, and call
vector
\lambda
-terms
the elements
of the quotient
\mathord{\mathord{\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}}/\mathord{\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}}}
: these are the terms of the previously studied
algebraic
\lambda
-calculus
[
Vau09
,
Alb14
]
.
14
14

14

In those previous works, the elements of
\mathord{\mathord{\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}}/\mathord{\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}}}
were called
algebraic
\lambda
-terms, but here we reserve this name for another, simpler,
notion.

It is clear that
\mathord{\mathord{\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}}/\mathord{\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}}}
forms a
\mathbf{S}
-semimodule.
In fact, one can show
[
Vau09
]
that
\mathord{\mathord{\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}}/\mathord{\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}}}

is freely generated by the
\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}
-equivalence classes of base terms,
i.e.
those described by the following grammar:

\begin{array}[]{rclcl}\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{b}}&\ni&B&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}&x\mid\lambda x\,{B}\mid{\left(B\right)}\,{M}.\end{array}

Hence we could write
\mathord{\mathord{\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}}/\mathord{\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}}}=\mathbf{S}{\left[\mathord{\mathord{\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{b}}}/\mathord{\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}}}\right]}
.

Notice however that Taylor expansion is not injective on vector
\lambda
-terms in general.

Example
\thethm
.

We can consider that

\mathord{\mathord{\Sigma_{\mathbf{B}}}/\mathord{\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}}}=\mathord{\mathfrak{P}_{f}}{\left(\mathord{\mathord{\Sigma_{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathrm{b}}}/\mathord{\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}}}\right)}

and
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\subseteq\Delta
for all
M\in\Sigma_{\mathbf{B}}
.
It is then easy to check that,

e.g.
,
\mathord{\tau}{\left({\left(x\right)}\,{\emptyset}\right)}\subseteq\mathord{\tau}{\left({\left(x\right)}\,{x}\right)}
,
hence
{\left(x\right)}\,{\emptyset}+_{\mathbf{B}}{\left(x\right)}\,{x}\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}{\left(x\right)}\,{x}
.
15
15

15

This discrepancy is also present in the non-deterministic Böhm trees of
de’Liguoro and Piperno
[
dLP95
]
: in that qualitative
setting, they can solve it by introducing a preorder on trees based on set
inclusion. They moreover show that this preorder coincides with that
induced by a well chosen domain theoretic model, as well as with the
observational preorder associated with must-solvability. This preorder
should be related with that induced by
the inclusion of normal forms of Taylor expansions (which are always defined
since we then work with support sets rather than general vectors).

This contrasts with the case of pure
\lambda
-terms, for which
\mathord{\tau}
is always injective:
in this case, it is in fact sufficient to look at the linear resource terms
in supports of Taylor expansions.

Fact 1
.

For all
M,N\in\Lambda
,

\mathord{\ell}{\left(M\right)}\in{\left|\mathord{\tau}{\left(N\right)}\right|}
iff
M=N
,
where
\mathord{\ell}
is defined inductively
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathord{\ell}{\left(x\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}x

\displaystyle\mathord{\ell}{\left(\lambda x\,{M}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda x\,{\mathord{\ell}{\left(M\right)}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\ell}{\left({\left(M\right)}\,{N}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\langle\mathord{\ell}{\left(M\right)}\right\rangle}\,{{\left[\mathord{\ell}{\left(N\right)}\right]}}.

To our knowledge, finding sufficient conditions on
\mathbf{S}
ensuring that

\mathord{\tau}
becomes injective on
\mathord{\mathord{\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}}/\mathord{\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}}}
is still an open question.

Observe moreover that the
\mathbf{S}
-semimodule structure of
\mathord{\mathord{\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}}/\mathord{\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}}}
gets in the way when
we want to study
\beta
-reduction and normalization: it is well known

[
Vau07
,
AD08
,
Vau09
]
that
\beta
-reduction in a semimodule of terms is
inconsistent in presence of negative coefficients.

Example 4.1
.

Consider
\delta_{M}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda x\,{\left(M+{\left(x\right)}\,{x}\right)}
and
\infty_{M}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left(\delta_{M}\right)}\,{\delta_{M}}
.
Observe that
\infty_{M}

\beta
-reduces to
M+\infty_{M}
. Suppose
\mathbf{S}
is a ring.
Then any congruence
\simeq
on
\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}
containing
\beta
-reduction
and the equations of
\mathbf{S}
-module is inconsistent:

0\simeq\infty_{M}+(-1).\infty_{M}\simeq(M+\infty_{M})+(-1).\infty_{M}\simeq M
.

The problem is of course the identity
0\simeq\infty_{M}+(-1).\infty_{M}
. Another difficulty is that,
if
\mathbf{S}
has fractions then, up to
\mathbf{S}
-semimodule equations, one can
split a single
\beta
-reduction step into infinitely many fractional steps: if

M\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}
then

\textstyle M\simeq\frac{1}{2}.M+\frac{1}{2}.M\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\beta}}}\frac{1}{2}.M+\frac{1}{2}.M^{\prime}\simeq{\left(\frac{1}{4}.M+\frac{1}{4}.M\right)}+\frac{1}{2}.M^{\prime}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\beta}}}{\left(\frac{1}{4}.M+\frac{1}{4}.M^{\prime}\right)}+\frac{1}{2}.M^{\prime}\simeq\cdots

It is not our purpose here to explore the various possible fixes to the
rewriting theory of
\beta
-reduction on vector
\lambda
-terms.
We rather refer the reader to the literature on algebraic
\lambda
-calculi

[
Vau09
,
AD08
,
Alb14
,
DC11
]
for various proposals.
Our focus being on Taylor expansion, we propose to consider vector
\lambda
-terms as
intermediate objects: the reduction relation induced on resource vectors by
\beta
-reduction
through Taylor expansion contains
\beta
-reduction on vector terms — which is mainly
useful to understand what may go wrong.

We still need to introduce some form of quotient in the syntax, though, if only
to allow formal sums to retain a computational meaning: otherwise, for
instance, no
\beta
-redex can be fired in
{\left(\lambda x\,{M}+\lambda x\,{N}\right)}\,{P}
; and more
generally there are
\beta
-normal terms whose Taylor expansion is not normal,
and conversely (consider,
e.g.
,
{\left(\lambda x\,{0}\right)}\,{P}
).

Write
\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}
for the quotient of
\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}
by the least compatible
equivalence
\simeq_{+}
containing the following six equations:

\displaystyle\lambda x\,{0}

\displaystyle\simeq_{+}0

\displaystyle\lambda x\,{\left(a.M\right)}

\displaystyle\simeq_{+}a.\lambda x\,{M}

\displaystyle\lambda x\,{\left(M+N\right)}

\displaystyle\simeq_{+}\lambda x\,{M}+\lambda x\,{N}

\displaystyle{\left(0\right)}\,{P}

\displaystyle\simeq_{+}0

\displaystyle{\left(a.M\right)}\,{P}

\displaystyle\simeq_{+}a.{\left(M\right)}\,{P}

\displaystyle{\left(M+N\right)}\,{P}

\displaystyle\simeq_{+}{\left(M\right)}\,{P}+{\left(N\right)}\,{P}

We call
algebraic
\lambda
-terms
the elements of
\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}
.
We will abuse notation and denote an algebraic
\lambda
-term by
any of its representatives.

Observe that
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}
is preserved under
\simeq_{+}
so
it is well defined on algebraic terms, although not on vector terms.

Fact 2
.

An algebraic
\lambda
-term
M
is
\beta
-normal (
i.e.
each of its representatives is

\beta
-normal) iff
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}
contains only normal resource terms.

We do not claim that
\simeq_{+}
is minimal with the above property
(for this, the bottom three equations are sufficient) but it
is quite natural for anyone familiar with the decomposition of
\lambda
-calculus
in linear logic, as it reflects the linearity of
\lambda
-abstraction
and the function position in an application. Moreover it retains the two-level
structure of vector
\lambda
-terms, seen as sums of base terms.

It is indeed a routine exercise to show that orienting the defining equations of
\simeq_{+}

from left to right defines a confluent and terminating rewriting system. We
call canonical terms the normal forms of this system, which we can describe as
follows. The sets
\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{c}}
of canonical terms and
\Sigma^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathbf{S}}
of simple
canonical terms are mutually generated by the following grammars:

\begin{array}[]{rclcl}\Sigma^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathbf{S}}&\ni&S,T&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}&x\mid\lambda x\,{S}\mid{\left(S\right)}\,{M}\\
\Sigma_{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{c}}&\ni&M,N,P&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}&S\mid 0\mid a.M\mid M+N\end{array}

so that each algebraic term
M
admits a unique canonical
\simeq_{+}
-representative.

In the remaining of this paper we will systematically identify algebraic
terms with their canonical representatives and keep
\simeq_{+}
implicit.
Moreover, we write
\Lambda^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathbf{S}}
for the set of simple algebraic
\lambda
-terms,
i.e.
those that admit a simple canonical representative.

Fact 3
.

Every simple term
S\in\Lambda^{\mathrm{s}}_{\mathbf{S}}
is of one of the following two forms:

•

S=\lambda x_{1}\,{\cdots\lambda x_{n}\,{{\left(x\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{k}}}}
:
S
is a head normal form;

•

S=\lambda x_{1}\,{\cdots\lambda x_{n}\,{{\left(\lambda x\,{T}\right)}\,{M_{0}\cdots M_{k}}}}
:

{\left(\lambda x\,{T}\right)}\,{M_{0}}
is the head redex of
S
.

So each algebraic
\lambda
-term can be considered as a formal linear combination
of head normal forms and head reducible simple terms, which will structure
the notions of weak solvability and hereditarily determinable terms in section

9
.

5.
On the reduction of resource vectors

Observe that

\mathord{\tau}{\left({\left(\lambda x\,{M}\right)}\,{N}\right)}={\left\langle\lambda x\,{\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}}\right\rangle}\,{{\mathord{\tau}{\left(N\right)}}^{\oc}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\Delta\\
\overline{t}\in\oc\Delta\end{subarray}}\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}_{s}{\mathord{\tau}{\left(N\right)}}^{\oc}_{\overline{t}}.{\left\langle\lambda x\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}

and

\mathord{\tau}{\left(M{\left[N/x\right]}\right)}=\partial_{x}\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\cdot{\mathord{\tau}{\left(N\right)}}^{\oc}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\Delta\\
\overline{t}\in\oc\Delta\end{subarray}}\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}_{s}{\mathord{\tau}{\left(N\right)}}^{\oc}_{\overline{t}}.\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{t}

In order to simulate
\beta
-reduction through Taylor expansion we might be
tempted to consider the reduction given by

\epsilon\to\epsilon^{\prime}
as soon as
\epsilon=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.e_{i}

and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
with
e_{i}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in I
.
16
16

16

We must of course require that
\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e_{i}\right)}
is finite
but, again, we will keep such requirements implicit in the following.

Observe indeed that, as soon as
{\left(a_{i}.e_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}\in(\oc)\Delta

is summable (
i.e.
for all
e\in(\oc)\Delta
, there are finitely many

i\in I
such that
a_{i}\not=0
and
e_{i}=e
),
the family
{\left(a_{i}.\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}
is summable too:
if
e^{\prime}\in{\left|a_{i}.\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right|}
then
a_{i}\not=0
and
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right|}

hence by Lemma
3.4
,

\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e_{i}\right)}=\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}
and
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e_{i}\right)}\leq 2\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}+2
;

e^{\prime}
being fixed, there are thus finitely many possible values for
e_{i}
hence for
i
.
So we do not need any additional condition for this reduction step to be well defined.

This reduction, however, is not suitable for simulating
\beta
-reduction
because whenever the reduced
\beta
-redex is
not in linear position, we need to reduce arbitrarily many
resource redexes.

Example 5.1
.

Observe that

\mathord{\tau}{\left({\left(y\right)}\,{{\left(\lambda x\,{x}\right)}\,{z}}\right)}=\sum_{n,k_{1},\dotsc,k_{n}\in\mathbf{N}}\frac{1}{n!k_{1}!\cdots k_{n}!}{\left\langle y\right\rangle}\,{{\left[{\left\langle\lambda x\,{x}\right\rangle}\,{z^{k_{1}}},\dotsc,{\left\langle\lambda x\,{x}\right\rangle}\,{z^{k_{n}}}\right]}}

and

\mathord{\tau}{\left({\left(y\right)}\,{z}\right)}=\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}\frac{1}{n!}{\left\langle y\right\rangle}\,{z^{n}}.

Then the reduction from
{\left[{\left\langle\lambda x\,{x}\right\rangle}\,{z^{k_{1}}},\dotsc,{\left\langle\lambda x\,{x}\right\rangle}\,{z^{k_{n}}}\right]}

to
z^{n}
if each
k_{i}=1
(resp. to
0
if one
k_{i}\not=1
) requires firing
n

independent redexes (resp. one of those
n
redexes).

5.1.
Parallel resource reduction

One possible fix would be to replace
\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}
with
\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{*}}}
in the above definition,

i.e.
set
\epsilon\to\epsilon^{\prime}
as soon as
\epsilon=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.e_{i}

and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
with
e_{i}\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{*}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in I
,
but then the study of the reduction subsumes that of
normalization, which we treat in Section
8
, and this relies
on the possibility to simulate
\beta
-reduction steps.

A reasonable middle ground is to consider a parallel variant
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}
of

\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}
, where any number of redexes can be reduced simultaneously in one
step. The parallelism involved in the translation of a
\beta
-reduction step is
actually quite constrained: like in the previous example, the redexes that need
to be reduced in the Taylor expansion are always pairwise independent and no
nesting is involved. However, in order to prove the confluence of the reduction
on resource vectors, or its conservativity w.r.t.
\beta
-reduction, it is much more
convenient to work with a fully parallel reduction relation, both on algebraic

\lambda
-terms and on resource vectors. Indeed, parallel reduction relations generally
allow, e.g., to close confluence diagrams in one step or to define a maximal
parallel reduction step: the relevance of this technical choice will be made
clear all through Section
6
.

Definition 5.2
.

We define
parallel resource reduction

\mathord{\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}\subseteq(\oc)\Delta\times\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}

inductively as follows:

•

x\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}x
;

•

{\left\langle\lambda x\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\tau^{\prime}}

as soon as
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
;

•

\lambda x\,{s}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\lambda x\,{\sigma^{\prime}}
as soon as
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\sigma^{\prime}
;

•

{\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}{\left\langle\sigma^{\prime}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau^{\prime}}}
as soon as
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\overline{\tau}^{\prime}
;

•

{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right]}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}{\left[\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{n}\right]}
as soon as
s_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
for each
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
.

We extend this reduction to sums of resource expressions by linearity:

\epsilon\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
if
\epsilon=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}

with
e_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
.

It should be clear that
\mathord{\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}}\subseteq\mathord{\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}\subset\mathord{\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{*}}}}
,
Moreover observe that, because all term constructors are linear, the reduction
rules extend naturally to finite sums of resource expressions:
for instance,
\lambda x\,{\sigma}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\lambda x\,{\sigma^{\prime}}
as
soon as
\sigma\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\sigma^{\prime}
.

We will prove in Sections
6
and
7

that this solution is indeed a good one:
parallel resource reduction is strongly confluent, and there is a way
to extend it to resource vectors so that not only the resulting
reduction is strongly confluent and allows to simulate
\beta
-reduction, but
any reduction step from the Taylor expansion of an algebraic term
can be completed into a parallel
\beta
-reduction step.
There are two pitfalls with this approach, though.

5.2.
Size collapse

First, parallel reduction
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}
(like iterated reduction
\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{*}}}
) lacks the
combinatorial regularity properties of
\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}
given by Lemma
3.4
:
write
e\Succ_{\partial}e^{\prime}
if
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
with
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}\right|}
;

e^{\prime}\in(\oc)\Delta
being fixed, there is no bound on the size of the

\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}
-antecedents of
e^{\prime}
,
i.e.
those
e\in(\oc)\Delta
such that

e\Succ_{\partial}e^{\prime}
.

Example 5.3
.

Fix
s\in\Delta
.
Consider the sequences
\overrightarrow{u}(s)
and
\overrightarrow{v}(s)
of resource terms given by:

\left\{\begin{aligned} u_{0}(s)&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}s\\
u_{n+1}(s)&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\langle\lambda y\,{y}\right\rangle}\,{{\left[u_{n}(s)\right]}}\end{aligned}\right.\quad\text{and}\quad\left\{\begin{aligned} v_{0}(s)&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}s\\
v_{n+1}(s)&\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\langle\lambda y\,{v_{n}(s)}\right\rangle}\,{{\left[\right]}}\end{aligned}\right..

Observe that for all
n\in\mathbf{N}
,
u_{n+1}(s)\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}u_{n}(s)
and
v_{n+1}(s)\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}}}v_{n}(s)
,
and more generally, for all
n^{\prime}\leq n
,
u_{n}(s)\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}u_{n^{\prime}}(s)
and
v_{n}(s)\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}v_{n^{\prime}}(s)
.
In particular
u_{n}(s)\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}s
and
v_{n}(s)\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}s
for all
n\in\mathbf{N}
.

Reducing all resource expressions in a resource vector simultaneously is thus no longer
possible in general: consider,
e.g.
,
\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}u_{n}(x)
.
As a consequence, when we introduce a reduction relation on resource vectors
by extending a reduction relation on resource expressions
as above, we must in general impose the summability of the family of reducts
as a side condition:

Definition 5.4
.

Fix an arbitrary relation

\mathord{\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}}\subseteq(\oc)\Delta\times\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}
.
For all
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
,
we write
\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\rightarrow}}}\epsilon^{\prime}

whenever there exist families
(a_{i})_{i\in I}\in\mathbf{S}^{I}
,

{\left(e_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}\in(\oc)\Delta^{I}

and
{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}^{I}

such that:

•

{\left(e_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}
is summable and
\epsilon=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.e_{i}
;

•

{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}
is summable and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
;

•

for all
i\in I
,
e_{i}\mathrel{{\rightarrow^{?}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
.

The necessity of such a side condition forbids confluence. Indeed:

Example 5.5
.

Let
\sigma=\sum_{n}u_{n}(v_{n}(x))
. Then
\sigma\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}\sum u_{n}(x)
and
\sigma\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}\sum v_{n}(x)
,
but since the only common reduct of
u_{p}(x)
and
v_{q}(x)
is
x
, there is no
way
17
17

17

In fact, this argument is only valid if
\mathbf{S}
is zerosumfree
(
i.e.
if
a+b=0\in\mathbf{S}
entails
a=b=0
; see below, in particular Lemma
5.7
),
for instance if
\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{N}
:
we rely on the fact that if
\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.s_{i}=\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}u_{n}(x)
then
for all
i\in I
such that
a_{i}\not=0
, there is
n\in\mathbf{N}
such that
s_{i}=u_{n}(x)
.

to close this pair of reductions:
{\left(x\right)}_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
is not summable.

These considerations lead us to study the combinatorics of parallel resource reduction
more closely: in Section
6
, we introduce successive variants
of parallel reduction, based on restrictions on the nesting of fired redexes,
and provide bounds for the size of antecedents of a resource expression.
We moreover consider sufficient conditions for these restrictions
to be preserved under reduction.

We then observe in Section
7
that, when applied to
Taylor expansions, parallel reduction is automatically of the most restricted
form, which allows us to provide uniform bounds and obtain the desired
confluence and simulation properties.

5.3.
Reduction structures

The other,
a priori
unrelated pitfall is the fact that the reduction can interact
badly with the semimodule structure of
\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
: we can reproduce
Example
4.1
in
\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
through Taylor
expansion (see the discussion in Section
7.1
,
p.
7.1
).
Even more simply, we can use the terms of Example
5.3
:

Example 5.6
.

Let
s\in\Delta
and
\sigma=\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}u_{n+1}(s)\in\mathbf{S}^{\Delta}
.
Assuming
\mathbf{S}
is a ring:

0=\sigma+(-1).\sigma\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}u_{n}(s)+(-1).\sigma=s
.

Of course, this kind of issue does not arise when the semiring of coefficients is
zerosumfree
:
recall that
\mathbf{S}
is zerosumfree if
a+b=0
implies
a=b=0
, which holds for
all semirings of non-negative numbers, as well as for booleans.
This prevents interferences between reductions and the semimodule structure:

Lemma 5.7
.

Assume
\mathbf{S}
is zerosumfree and fix a relation

\mathord{\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}}\subseteq(\oc)\Delta\times\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}
.
If
\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\rightarrow}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
then, for all
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}\right|}
there exists

e\in{\left|\epsilon\right|}
and
\epsilon_{0}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}

such that
e\mathrel{{\rightarrow^{?}}}\epsilon_{0}
and
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon_{0}\right|}
.

Proof 5.8
.

Assume
\epsilon=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.e_{i}

and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
with
e_{i}\mathrel{{\rightarrow^{?}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in I
.
If
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}\right|}
then there is
i\in I
such that

e^{\prime}\in{\left|a_{i}.\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right|}
hence
a_{i}\not=0
and
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right|}
.
Then, since
\mathbf{S}
is zerosumfree,
e_{i}\in{\left|\epsilon\right|}
.

Various alternative approaches to get rid of this
restriction in the setting of the algebraic
\lambda
-calculus
can be adapted to the reduction of resource vectors:
we refer the reader to the literature on algebraic
\lambda
-calculi

[
Vau09
,
AD08
,
Alb14
,
DC11
]
for several proposals.
The linear-continuity of the resource
\lambda
-calculus allows us to propose a novel
approach: consider possible restrictions on
the families of resource expressions simultaneously reduced in a

\mathrel{{\widetilde{\rightarrow}}}
-step.

Definition 5.9
.

We call
resource support
any set
\mathcal{E}\subseteq(\oc)\Delta
of
resource expressions such that
\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\mathcal{E}\right)}=\bigcup_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}
is finite.
Then a
resource structure
is any set

\mathfrak{E}\subseteq\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left((\oc)\Delta\right)}
of resource supports such that:

•

\mathfrak{E}
contains all finite resource supports;

•

\mathfrak{E}
is closed under finite unions;

•

\mathfrak{E}
is downwards closed for inclusion.

The maximal resource structure is
(\oc)\mathfrak{F}_{\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\{\mathcal{E}\subseteq(\oc)\Delta\mathrel{;}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\mathcal{E}\right)}\text{ is finite}\right\}}
, which is also
a finiteness structure
[
Ehr10
]
. Observe that any
finiteness structure
\mathfrak{F}\subseteq(\oc)\mathfrak{F}_{\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}}
is a resource structure:
all three additional conditions are automatically satisfied.

Definition 5.10
.

Fix a relation

\mathord{\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}}\subseteq(\oc)\Delta\times\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}
.
For all resource support
\mathcal{E}
, we write
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\rightarrow}_{\mathcal{E}}}}
for

\mathrel{{\widetilde{\rightharpoonup_{\mathcal{E}}}}}
where
\rightharpoonup_{\mathcal{E}}

denotes
{\mathord{\to}\mathbin{\cap}(\mathcal{E}\times\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]})}
.
For all resource structure
\mathfrak{E}
, we then write
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\rightarrow}_{\mathfrak{E}}}}

for
\bigcup_{\mathcal{E}\in\mathfrak{E}}\mathord{\mathrel{{\widetilde{\rightarrow}_{\mathcal{E}}}}}
.

We have

\mathord{\mathrel{{\widetilde{\rightarrow}_{\mathcal{E}}}}}\subseteq\mathord{\mathrel{{\widetilde{\rightarrow}}}}\mathbin{\cap}(\mathbf{S}^{\mathcal{E}}\times\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta})
,
but in general the reverse inclusion holds only if
\mathbf{S}
is zerosumfree:
in this latter case
\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
iff
\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{{\left|\epsilon\right|}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
.

Definition 5.11
.

We call
\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}
-reduction structure
any resource structure
\mathfrak{E}
such that
if
\mathcal{E}\in\mathfrak{E}
then
\bigcup{\left\{{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}\right|}\mathrel{;}e\in\mathcal{E}\text{ and }e\mathrel{{\rightarrow^{?}}}\epsilon^{\prime}\right\}}\in\mathfrak{E}
.

We will consider some particular choices of reduction structure in the following,
but the point is that our approach is completely generic.
The results of Section
7
will imply
that if
\mathfrak{S}\subseteq\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(\mathbf{S}^{\Delta}\right)}
is a
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}
-reduction structure
containing
{\left|\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right|}
then one can translate
any
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}
-reduction sequence from
M

into a
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{S}}}}
-reduction sequence from
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}
.
Additional properties such as the confluence of
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{S}}}}
,
its conservativity over
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}
, or its compatibility with
normalization will depend on additional conditions on
\mathfrak{S}
.

6.
Taming the size collapse of parallel resource reduction

In this section, we study successive families of restrictions of the parallel
resource reduction
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}
. Our purpose is to enforce some control on the
size collapse induced by
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}
, so as to obtain a confluent restriction of

\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}
, all the while retaining enough parallelism to simulate parallel

\beta
-reduction on algebraic
\lambda
-terms, ideally in a conservative way.

First observe that parallel resource reduction itself is strongly confluent as expected:
following a classic argument, we define
\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(e\right)}
as the
result of firing all redexes in
e
and then, whenever
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
,
we have
\epsilon^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(e\right)}
. Formally:

Definition 6.1
.

For all
e\in(\oc)\Delta
we define
the
full parallel reduct

\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(e\right)}
of
e
by induction on
e
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(x\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}x

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\lambda x\,{s}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda x\,{\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(s\right)}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left({\left\langle\lambda x\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\partial_{x}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(s\right)}\cdot\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left({\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\langle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(s\right)}\right\rangle}\,{\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}}

(if
s
is not an abstraction)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left({\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right]}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left[\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(s_{1}\right)},\dotsc,\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(s_{n}\right)}\right]}.

Then if
\epsilon=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}
,
we set
\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(e_{i}\right)}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

For all
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}
,
if
\epsilon\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
then
\epsilon^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}
.

Proof 6.3
.

Follows directly from the definitions.

In general, however, if we fix
e^{\prime}\in(\oc)\Delta
then there is no
bound on those
e\in(\oc)\Delta
such that
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(e\right)}\right|}
,
so we cannot extend
\mathord{\mathsf{F}}
on
\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
, nor generalize Lemma

6.2
to
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}
.
Indeed, we have shown that
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}
is not even confluent.

In order to understand what restrictions are necessary to recover confluence,
we first provide a close inspection of the combinatorial effect of

\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}
on the size of resource expressions: we show in subsection

6.1
that bounding the length of chains
of immediately nested fired redexes is enough to bound the size of

\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}
-antecedents of a fixed resource expression.

In order to close a pair of reductions
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
and
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime\prime}
,
we have to reduce at least the residuals in
\epsilon^{\prime}
of the redexes fired
in the reduction
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime\prime}
(and vice versa). So we want the above
bounds to be stable under taking the unions of sets of redexes in a term:
it is not the case if we consider chains of immediately nested redexes.
In Subsection
6.2
, we extend the boundedness condition
to all chains of nested fired redexes and introduce the family

{\left(\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\right)}_{b\in\mathbf{N}}
of boundedly nested parallel reductions.
We then show that this family enjoys a kind of diamond property (Lemma

6.21
), which can then be extended to

\mathord{\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{(\partial)}}}}}}=\bigcup_{b\in\mathbf{N}}\mathord{\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}}}
.
We must require that
\mathbf{S}
enjoys an additional
additive splitting
property (see
Definition
6.23
), in order to
“align” the
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}
-reductions involved in both sides of a pair
of
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}}
-reductions from the same resource vector
(see the proof of Lemma
6.26
).

To get rid of the additive splitting hypothesis we must further restrict
resource reduction so as to recover a notion of full reduct
at bounded depth
.
It is not sufficient to bound the depth of fired redexes because this is not
stable under reduction.
In Subsection
6.3
, we rather introduce
the parallel reduction
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}
where
substituted variables occur at depth at most
d
.
We then show that

\mathord{\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor\partial\rfloor}}}}}}=\bigcup_{d\in\mathbf{N}}\mathord{\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}}}

is strongly confluent by proving that any
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}
-step from
\epsilon

can be followed by a
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}
-reduction to
\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}
,
where
\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}
is obtained by firing all redexes in
\epsilon
for which
the bound variables occur at depth at most
d
, and
d^{\prime}
depends only on
d
.

Finally, we consider resource vectors of bounded height: these contain the
Taylor expansions of algebraic
\lambda
-terms. We show that all the above restrictions
actually coincide with
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}
on bounded resource vectors. In this particular
case, we can actually extend
\mathord{\mathsf{F}}
by linear-continuity and
obtain a proof of the diamond property for
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}
.
18
18

18

Note that, although they involve increasing constraints on parallel
reduction, Subsections
6.1
to
6.3

are essentially pairwise independent.
Moreover, we obtain the diamond property for
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}
on bounded resource
vectors as a consequence of the results of Subsection
6.3
,
but it could as well be proved directly, using similar techniques
(see Footnote
21
, p.
21
).
So, the reader who only wants the proofs necessary for the main results of
the paper can skip Subsections
6.1
and
6.2
;
the reader who is not interested in checking proofs can also skip subsection

6.3
.
We chose to present the successive families of restrictions anyway, because
their construction provides a precise understanding of the combinatorics of
parallel resource reduction, and of the various ingredients involved in
designing a strongly confluent version of
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}
: we start by avoiding
the size collapse by putting a restriction on families of redexes that can be
fired in parallel; then we ensure that this restriction is stable under
reduction.
This understanding plays a key rôle in enabling the generalization of our
approach to linear logic proof nets or infinitary
\lambda
-calculus:
with Chouquet, we have recently established that our restrictions on
the nesting of redexes, as well as their preservation under reduction, can be
adapted to the setting of proof nets
[
CA18
]
;
and preliminary work on infinitary
\lambda
-calculus indicates that it could be amenable
to the technique of Subsection
6.2
, whereas
it does not make sense to restrict the depth of substituted variables in this setting.

At this point of the discussion, it is worth noting that, if we extend
a relation
\mathord{\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}}\subseteq{(\oc)\Delta\times\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}}

to a binary relation on finite sums of resource expressions so that

\epsilon\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}\epsilon^{\prime}
iff
\epsilon=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}

with
e_{i}\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
, then
for all
\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}
-reduction structure
\mathfrak{E}
and all
resource vectors
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
, we have

\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\rightarrow}_{\mathfrak{E}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
iff there exist a set
I
of indices,
a resource support
\mathcal{E}\in\mathfrak{E}
,
a family
(a_{i})_{i\in I}\in\mathbf{S}^{I}
of scalars
and families
{\left(\epsilon_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[\mathcal{E}\right]}^{I}

and
{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}^{I}

such that:

•

{\left(\epsilon_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}
is summable and
\epsilon=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.\epsilon_{i}
;

•

{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}
is summable and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
;

•

for all
i\in I
,
\epsilon_{i}\mathrel{{\rightarrow}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
.

We will use this fact for confluence proofs:
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}
and its variants are
all of this form.

6.1.
Bounded chains of redexes

Definition 6.4
.

We define a family of relations
\mathord{\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m|k\right)}}}}}\subseteq(\oc)\Delta\times\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}

for
m\leq k\in\mathbf{N}
inductively as follows:

•

x\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m|k\right)}}}}x
for all
m\leq k\in\mathbf{N}
;

•

\lambda x\,{s}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m|k\right)}}}}\lambda x\,{\sigma^{\prime}}
if
m\leq k
and
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m_{1}|k\right)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
for some
m_{1}\leq k
;

•

{\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m|k\right)}}}}{\left\langle\sigma^{\prime}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau^{\prime}}}
if
m\leq k
,

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m_{1}|k\right)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m_{2}|k\right)}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}

for some
m_{1}\leq k
and
m_{2}\leq k
;

•

{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{r}\right]}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m|k\right)}}}}{\left[\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{r}\right]}

if
s_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m|k\right)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,r\right\}}
;

•

{\left\langle\lambda x\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m|k\right)}}}}\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
if

0<m\leq k
,
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m-1|k\right)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m-1|k\right)}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
.

Intuitively, we have
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m|k\right)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
iff
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
and
that reduction fires chains of redexes of length at most
k
,
those starting at top level being of length at most
m
.
In particular, it should be clear that if
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m|k\right)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}

then
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
, and
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m^{\prime}|k^{\prime}\right)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
as soon as
m\leq m^{\prime}\leq k^{\prime}
and
k\leq k^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
iff
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}|\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}\right)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
.

Definition 6.5
.

We define
\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m\right)}\in\mathbf{N}
for all
k,l,m\in\mathbf{N}
,
by induction on the lexicographically ordered pair
(l,m)
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(0,0\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l+1,0\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,k\right)}+1

\displaystyle\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m+1\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}

\displaystyle 4\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m\right)}.

We then write
\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,k\right)}
.

For all
k,l,m\in\mathbf{N}
, the following identities follow straightforwardly
from the definition and will be used throughout this subsection:

\displaystyle\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4^{m}\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,0\right)}

\displaystyle\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(0,m\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(1,m\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4^{m}.

Lemma 6.6
.

For all
k,l,l^{\prime},m\in\mathbf{N}
,
\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l+l^{\prime},m\right)}\geq\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m\right)}+\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l^{\prime},m\right)}
.

Proof 6.7
.

By induction on
l^{\prime}
.
The case
l^{\prime}=0
is direct.
Assume the result holds for
l^{\prime}
, we prove it for
l^{\prime}+1
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l+l^{\prime}+1,m\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4^{m}{\left(\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l+l^{\prime}+1,0\right)}\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4^{m}{\left(\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l+l^{\prime},k\right)}+1\right)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 4^{m}{\left(\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,k\right)}+\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l^{\prime},k\right)}+1\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4^{m}{\left(4^{k}\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,0\right)}+\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l^{\prime}+1,0\right)}\right)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 4^{m}\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,0\right)}+4^{m}\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l^{\prime}+1,0\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m\right)}+\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l^{\prime}+1,m\right)}.

The following generalization follows directly:

Corollary 6.8
.

For all
l_{1}\dotsc,l_{n}\in\mathbf{N}

\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}l_{i},m\right)}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l_{i},m\right)}.

Lemma 6.9
.

For all
k,l,m\in\mathbf{N}
,

\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m\right)}\geq l
.

Proof 6.10
.

By Corollary
6.8
,

\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m\right)}\geq l\times\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(1,m\right)}=l\times 4^{m}
.

Lemma 6.11
.

For all
k,k^{\prime},l,l^{\prime},m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbf{N}

if
k\leq k^{\prime}
,
l\leq l^{\prime}
and
m\leq m^{\prime}
, then:

\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m\right)}\leq\mathbf{gb}_{k^{\prime}}{\left(l^{\prime},m^{\prime}\right)}.

Proof 6.12
.

We prove the monotonicity of
\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m\right)}
in
m
,
l
and then
k
, separately.

First, if
m\leq m^{\prime}
then

\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m\right)}=4^{m}\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,0\right)}\leq 4^{m^{\prime}}\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,0\right)}=\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m^{\prime}\right)}
.

By Lemma
6.6
, if
l\leq l^{\prime}
,

\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l^{\prime},m\right)}\geq\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m\right)}+\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l^{\prime}-l,m\right)}\geq\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m\right)}
.

Finally, we prove that if
k\leq k^{\prime}
then
\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m\right)}\leq\mathbf{gb}_{k^{\prime}}{\left(l,m\right)}

by induction on the lexicographically ordered pair
(l,m)
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(0,0\right)}&~=~0\\
&~=~\mathbf{gb}_{k^{\prime}}{\left(0,0\right)}\\
\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l+1,0\right)}&~=~\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,k\right)}+1\\
&~\leq~\mathbf{gb}_{k^{\prime}}{\left(l,k\right)}+1\\
&~\leq~\mathbf{gb}_{k^{\prime}}{\left(l,k^{\prime}\right)}+1\\
&~=~\mathbf{gb}_{k^{\prime}}{\left(l+1,0\right)}\\
\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m+1\right)}&~=~4\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(l,m\right)}\\
&~\leq~4\mathbf{gb}_{k^{\prime}}{\left(l,m\right)}\\
&~=~\mathbf{gb}_{k^{\prime}}{\left(l,m+1\right)}\end{aligned}

Write
e\Succ_{{\left(m|k\right)}}e^{\prime}
if
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m|k\right)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
with
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}\right|}
.

Lemma 6.13
.

If
e\Succ_{{\left(m|k\right)}}e^{\prime}
then

\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}\leq\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)},m\right)}
.

Proof 6.14
.

By induction on the reduction
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m|k\right)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
such that
e^{\prime}\in\epsilon^{\prime}
.

If
e=x=\epsilon^{\prime}
then
e^{\prime}=x
and
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}=1=\mathbf{gb}_{0}{\left(1,0\right)}\leq\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)},m\right)}
.

If
e=\lambda x\,{s}
,
\epsilon^{\prime}=\lambda x\,{\sigma^{\prime}}
,
m\leq k
and
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m_{1}|k\right)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
with
m_{1}\leq k
,
then
e^{\prime}=\lambda x\,{s^{\prime}}
with
s\Succ_{{\left(m_{1}|k\right)}}s^{\prime}
. We obtain:

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s\right)}+1

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)},m_{1}\right)}+1

(by induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)},k\right)}+1

\displaystyle=\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}+1,0\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)},m\right)}.

If
e={\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}
,

\epsilon^{\prime}={\left\langle\sigma^{\prime}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau^{\prime}}}
,

m\leq k
,

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m_{1}|k\right)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and

\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m_{2}|k\right)}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}

with
m_{i}\leq k
for all
i\in{\left\{1,2\right\}}
,
then
e^{\prime}={\left\langle s^{\prime}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t^{\prime}}}
with

s\Succ_{{\left(m_{1}|k\right)}}s^{\prime}

and
\overline{t}\Succ_{{\left(m_{2}|k\right)}}\overline{t^{\prime}}
.
We obtain:

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}+1

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)},m_{1}\right)}+\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)},m_{2}\right)}+1

(by induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)},k\right)}+\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)},k\right)}+1

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)},k\right)}+1

\displaystyle=\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)}+1,0\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)},m\right)}.

If
e={\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{r}\right]}
,

\epsilon^{\prime}={\left[\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{r}\right]}
and

s_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m|k\right)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}

for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,r\right\}}
,
then
e^{\prime}={\left[s^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,s^{\prime}_{r}\right]}

with
s_{i}\Succ_{{\left(m|k\right)}}s^{\prime}_{i}

for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,r\right\}}
.
We obtain:

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s_{i}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}_{i}\right)},m\right)}

(by induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}_{i}\right)},m\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)},m\right)}.

If
e={\left\langle\lambda x\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}
,

\epsilon^{\prime}=\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
,

0<m\leq k
,
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m-1|k\right)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and

\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m-1|k\right)}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
,
then there are
s^{\prime}\in{\left|\sigma^{\prime}\right|}
and

\overline{t^{\prime}}\in{\left|\overline{\tau^{\prime}}\right|}
such that

e^{\prime}\in{\left|\partial_{x}s^{\prime}\cdot\overline{t}^{\prime}\right|}
.
In particular,
s\Succ_{{\left(m-1|k\right)}}s^{\prime}

and
\overline{t}\Succ_{{\left(m-1|k\right)}}\overline{t^{\prime}}

and we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}+2

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)},m-1\right)}+\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)},m-1\right)}+2

(by induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)},m-1\right)}+2

(
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}\geq\max{\left\{\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)},\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)}\right\}}
)

\displaystyle\leq 4\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)},m-1\right)}

(
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}\geq\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}\geq 1
)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{gb}_{k}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)},m\right)}.

As a direct consequence, for all
m\leq k\in\mathbf{N}
, for all summable family

{\left(e_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}
and all family
{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}
such that

e_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m|k\right)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in I
,
{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}

is also summable: we can thus drop the side condition in the definition of

\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m|k\right)}}}}}
.

Observe however that those reduction relations are not stable under taking the
unions of fired redexes in families of reduction steps: using,
e.g.
,
the terms
u_{n}(s)
from Example
5.3
,
for all
n\in\mathbf{N}
, we have
u_{2n}(s)\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(1|1\right)}}}}u_{n}(s)
by
firing all redexes at even depth,

u_{2n}(s)\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(0|1\right)}}}}u_{n}(s)
by
firing all redexes at odd depth,
and
u_{2n}(s)\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(2n|2n\right)}}}}s
by firing both families,
but there is obviously no
k\in\mathbf{N}
such that

u_{2n}(s)\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(k|k\right)}}}}s
uniformly for all
n\in\mathbf{N}
.
Although we can close the induced critical pair

\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}u_{2n}(s)\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\left(0|1\right)}}}}}\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}u_{n}(s)

and

\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}u_{2n}(s)\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\left(1|1\right)}}}}}\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}u_{n}(s)

trivially in this case, this phenomenon is an obstacle to confluence:

Example 6.15
.

Fix
s\in\Delta
and
consider the sequence
\overrightarrow{w}(s)
of resource terms given by

w_{0}(s)=s
and:

\displaystyle w_{2n+1}(s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\left\langle\lambda y\,{y}\right\rangle}\,{{\left[w_{2n}(s)\right]}}

\displaystyle w_{2n+2}(s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\left\langle\lambda y\,{w_{2n+1}(s)}\right\rangle}\,{{\left[\right]}}

Then for all
n\in\mathbf{N}
,
w_{2n}(s)\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(1|1\right)}}}}u_{n}(s)
,

w_{2n+1}(s)\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(0|1\right)}}}}u_{n}(s)
,
w_{2n}(s)\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(0|1\right)}}}}v_{n}(s)
,
and
w_{2n+1}(s)\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(1|1\right)}}}}v_{n}(s)
.
Then for instance

\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}w_{2n}(s)\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\left(1|1\right)}}}}}\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}u_{n}(s)\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}w_{2n}(s)\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\left(0|1\right)}}}}}\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}v_{n}(s)

but we know from Example
5.5
that this pair
of reductions cannot be closed in general.

6.2.
Boundedly nested redexes

From the previous subsection, it follows that
bounding the length of chains of immediately nested redexes allows to tame
the size collapse of resource expressions under reduction,
but we need to further restrict this notion
in order to keep it stable under unions of fired redex sets.
A natural answer is to require a bound on the depth of the nesting of
fired redexes, regardless of the distance between them:

Definition 6.16
.

We define a family of relations
{\left(\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\right)}_{b\in{\mathbf{N}}}

inductively as follows:

•

x\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}x
for all
b\in\mathbf{N}
;

•

\lambda x\,{s}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\lambda x\,{\sigma^{\prime}}
if
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
;

•

{\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}{\left\langle\sigma^{\prime}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau^{\prime}}}

if
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
;

•

{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{r}\right]}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}{\left[\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{r}\right]}

if
s_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,r\right\}}
;

•

{\left\langle\lambda x\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\tau^{\prime}}

if
b\geq 1
,
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b-1)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b-1)}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
.

Intuitively, we have
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
iff
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
and
every branch of
e
(seen as a rooted tree) crosses at most
b
fired redexes.
In particular it should be clear that if
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}

then
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(b|b\right)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
, and moreover
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b^{\prime})}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}

for all
b^{\prime}\geq b
.
Moreover observe that
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)})}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
whenever
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
, hence

\mathord{\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}=\bigcup_{b\in\mathbf{N}}\mathord{\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}}
.

Write
e\Succ_{{(b)}}e^{\prime}
if
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
with
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}\right|}
.
If
e\Succ_{{(b)}}e^{\prime}
, then
e\Succ_{{\left(b|b\right)}}e^{\prime}
and we
thus know that
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}\leq\mathbf{gb}_{b}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}\right)}
.
In this special case, we can in fact provide a much better bound:

Lemma 6.17
.

If
e\Succ_{{(b)}}e^{\prime}
then
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}\leq 4^{b}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}
.

Proof 6.18
.

By induction on the reduction
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}{\epsilon^{\prime}}

such that
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}\right|}
.

If
e=x=\epsilon^{\prime}
then
e^{\prime}=x
and
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}=1\leq 4^{b}=4^{b}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}
.

If
e=\lambda x\,{s}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\lambda x\,{\sigma^{\prime}}
with
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
,
then
e^{\prime}=\lambda x\,{s^{\prime}}
with
s\Succ_{{(b)}}s^{\prime}
.
By induction hypothesis,
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s\right)}\leq 4^{b}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}
.
Then

\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}=\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s\right)}+1\leq 4^{b}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}+1\leq 4^{b}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}+1\right)}=4^{b}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}
.

If
e={\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}
,
\epsilon^{\prime}={\left\langle\sigma^{\prime}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau^{\prime}}}
,

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
,
then
e^{\prime}={\left\langle s^{\prime}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t^{\prime}}}
with

s\Succ_{{(b)}}s^{\prime}
and
\overline{t}\Succ_{{(b)}}\overline{t^{\prime}}
.
By induction hypothesis,
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s\right)}\leq 4^{b}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}
and

\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\leq 4^{b}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)}
.
Then

\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}=\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}+1\leq 4^{b}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}+4^{b}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)}+1\leq 4^{b}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)}+1\right)}=4^{b}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}
.

If
e={\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{r}\right]}
,

\epsilon^{\prime}={\left[\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{r}\right]}
and

s_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}

for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,r\right\}}
,
then
e^{\prime}={\left[s^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,s^{\prime}_{r}\right]}

with
s_{i}\Succ_{{(b)}}s^{\prime}_{i}

for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,r\right\}}
.
By induction hypothesis,

\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s_{i}\right)}\leq 4^{b}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}_{i}\right)}

for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,r\right\}}
and then

\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s_{i}\right)}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r}4^{b}{\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}_{i}\right)}}=4^{b}{\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}}
.

If
e={\left\langle\lambda x\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}
,

\epsilon^{\prime}=\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
,
b>0
,

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b-1)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and

\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b-1)}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
,
then there are
s^{\prime}\in{\left|\sigma^{\prime}\right|}
and

\overline{t^{\prime}}\in{\left|\overline{\tau^{\prime}}\right|}
such that

e^{\prime}\in{\left|\partial_{x}s^{\prime}\cdot\overline{t^{\prime}}\right|}
.
In particular,
s\Succ_{{(b-1)}}s^{\prime}

and
\overline{t}\Succ_{{(b-1)}}\overline{t^{\prime}}
and,
by induction hypothesis,

\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s\right)}\leq 4^{b-1}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}
and

\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\leq 4^{b-1}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)}
.
Writing
n=\mathbf{n}_{x}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}=\mathord{\#}{\overline{t^{\prime}}}
,
we have:

\displaystyle 4^{b}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}

\displaystyle=4^{b}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)}-n\right)}

\displaystyle=4^{b-1}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)}+3\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}+3\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)}-4n\right)}

(
n\leq\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}
and
n\leq\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)}
)

\displaystyle\geq 4^{b-1}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)}+2\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}\right)}

(
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}\geq 1
)

\displaystyle\geq 4^{b-1}{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)}\right)}+2

\displaystyle\geq\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(s\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}+2

\displaystyle=\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}.

Like for parallel reduction (Definition
5.2
),
we extend each
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}
to sums of resource expressions by linearity:

\epsilon\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
if
\epsilon=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}

with
e_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
.
Again, because all term constructors are linear, the reduction rules extend
naturally to finite sums of resource expressions:
for instance,
{\left\langle\lambda x\,{\sigma}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau}}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\tau^{\prime}}

as soon as
b\geq 1
,
\sigma\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b-1)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and
\overline{\tau}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b-1)}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
.

The relations
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}
are then stable under unions of
families of fired redexes, avoiding pitfalls such as that of Example

6.15
.

Lemma 6.19
.

If
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0})}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
and
\overline{u}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{1})}}}}\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}
then

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{u}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0}+b_{1})}}}}\partial_{x}\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}
.

Proof 6.20
.

Write
\overline{u}={\left[u_{1},\dotsc,u_{n}\right]}
. Then we can
write
\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}={\left[\upsilon^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\upsilon^{\prime}_{n}\right]}

with
u_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{1})}}}}{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{i}

for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
.
Recall that whenever

I={\left\{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{k}\right\}}\subseteq{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
with
\mathord{\#}{I}=k
,
we write
\overline{u}_{I}={\left[u_{i_{1}},\dotsc,u_{i_{k}}\right]}

and
\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{I}={\left[\upsilon^{\prime}_{i_{1}},\dotsc,\upsilon^{\prime}_{i_{k}}\right]}
.

The proof is by induction on the reduction
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0})}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
.
If
