e=y=\epsilon^{\prime}
then:

•

if
y=x
and
n=1
then

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{u}=u_{1}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{1})}}}}\upsilon^{\prime}_{1}=\partial_{x}\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}^{\prime}
;

•

if
y\not=x
and
\overline{u}={\left[\right]}
then

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{u}=y\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(0)}}}}y=\partial_{x}\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}
;

•

otherwise,

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{u}=0\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(0)}}}}0=\partial_{x}\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}
.

If
e=\lambda y\,{s}
(choosing
y\not=x
and
y\not\in\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\overline{u}\right)}
),

\epsilon^{\prime}=\lambda y\,{\sigma^{\prime}}
and
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0})}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
then, by induction hypothesis,

\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0}+b_{1})}}}}\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}
.
We obtain:

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{u}=\lambda y\,{\left(\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}\right)}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0}+b_{1})}}}}\lambda y\,{\left(\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}\right)}=\partial_{x}\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}
.

If
e={\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}
,
\epsilon^{\prime}={\left\langle\sigma^{\prime}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau^{\prime}}}
,

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0})}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and

\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0})}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
then,
by induction hypothesis,

\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}_{I}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0}+b_{1})}}}}\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{I}
and

\partial_{x}\overline{t}\cdot\overline{u}_{I}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0}+b_{1})}}}}\partial_{x}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{I}
,
for all
I\subseteq{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
.
We obtain:

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{u}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$(I,J)$ partition}\\
\text{of ${\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}$}\end{subarray}}{\left\langle\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}_{I}\right\rangle}\,{\partial_{x}\overline{t}\cdot\overline{u}_{J}}\\
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0}+b_{1})}}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$(I,J)$ partition}\\
\text{of ${\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}$}\end{subarray}}{\left\langle\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{I}\right\rangle}\,{\partial_{x}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{J}}=\partial_{x}\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}.

If
e={\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{r}\right]}
,
\epsilon^{\prime}={\left[\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{r}\right]}

and
s_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0})}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,r\right\}}
then,
by induction hypothesis,

\partial_{x}s_{i}\cdot\overline{u}_{I}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0}+b_{1})}}}}\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{I}

for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,r\right\}}
and all
I\subseteq{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
.
We obtain:

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{u}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$(I_{1},\dotsc,I_{r})$ partition}\\
\text{of ${\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}$}\end{subarray}}{\left[\partial_{x}s_{1}\cdot\overline{u}_{I_{1}},\dotsc,\partial_{x}s_{r}\cdot\overline{u}_{I_{r}}\right]}\\
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0}+b_{1})}}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$(I_{1},\dotsc,I_{r})$ partition}\\
\text{of ${\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}$}\end{subarray}}{\left[\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{I_{1}},\dotsc,\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}_{r}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{I_{r}}\right]}=\partial_{x}\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}.

If
e={\left\langle\lambda y\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}

(choosing
y\not=x
and
y\not\in\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\mathbin{\cup}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\overline{u}\right)}
),

\epsilon^{\prime}=\partial_{y}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
,
b_{0}\geq 1
,

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0}-1)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0}-1)}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
then,
by induction hypothesis,

\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}_{I}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0}+b_{1}-1)}}}}\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{I}
and

\partial_{x}\overline{t}\cdot\overline{u}_{I}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0}+b_{1}-1)}}}}\partial_{x}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{I}
,
for all
I\subseteq{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
.
We obtain:

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{u}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$(I,J)$ partition}\\
\text{of ${\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}$}\end{subarray}}{\left\langle\lambda y\,{\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}_{I}}\right\rangle}\,{\partial_{x}\overline{t}\cdot\overline{u}_{J}}\\
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{0}+b_{1})}}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$(I,J)$ partition}\\
\text{of ${\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}$}\end{subarray}}\partial_{y}{\left(\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{I}\right)}\cdot{\left(\partial_{x}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{J}\right)}=\partial_{x}{\left(\partial_{y}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\tau^{\prime}}\right)}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}=\partial_{x}\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}

using Lemma
3.3
.

Lemma 6.21
.

Let
K
be a finite set, and assume
\epsilon\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{k})}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{k}
for all
k\in K
.
Then, setting
b=\sum_{k\in K}b_{k}
, there is
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}
such that

\epsilon^{\prime}_{k}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(2^{\scriptstyle b_{k}}b)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime\prime}
for all
k\in K
.

Proof 6.22
.

By the linearity of the definition of reduction on finite sums,
it is sufficient to address the case of
\epsilon=e\in(\oc)\Delta
.
The proof is then by induction on the family of reductions

e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{k})}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{k}
.

If
e=x=\epsilon^{\prime}_{k}
for all
k\in K
, then we set
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}=x
.

If
e=\lambda x\,{s}
, and
\epsilon^{\prime}_{k}=\lambda x\,{\sigma^{\prime}_{k}}
with

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{k})}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{k}
for all
k\in K
then,
by induction hypothesis, we have
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
such that

\sigma^{\prime}_{k}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(2^{\scriptstyle b_{k}}b)}}}}\sigma^{\prime\prime}
for all
k\in K
,
and then we set
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}=\lambda x\,{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}
.

If
e={\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{r}\right]}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}_{k}={\left[\sigma^{\prime}_{1,k},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{r,k}\right]}

with
s_{j}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{k})}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{j,k}
for all
j\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,r\right\}}
and
k\in K
then,
by induction hypothesis, we have
\sigma^{\prime\prime}_{j}
such that

\sigma^{\prime}_{j,k}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(2^{\scriptstyle b_{k}}b)}}}}\sigma^{\prime\prime}_{j}

for all
j\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,r\right\}}
and
k\in K
, and then we set

\epsilon^{\prime\prime}={\left[\sigma^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime\prime}_{r}\right]}
.

Finally, assume
K=K_{0}+K_{1}
,
e={\left\langle\lambda x\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}
and:

•

for all
k\in K_{0}
,
\epsilon^{\prime}_{k}={\left\langle\lambda x\,{\sigma^{\prime}_{k}}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau^{\prime}}_{k}}

with
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{k})}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{k}
and
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{k})}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}_{k}
;

•

for all
k\in K_{1}
,
b_{k}\geq 1
and
\epsilon^{\prime}_{k}=\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}_{k}\cdot\overline{\tau^{\prime}}_{k}

with
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{k}-1)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{k}
and
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{k}-1)}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}_{k}
.

Write
b^{\prime}=b-\mathord{\#}{K_{1}}
.
By induction hypothesis, there are
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
and
\overline{\tau^{\prime\prime}}
such that,
for all
k\in K_{0}
,
\sigma^{\prime}_{k}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(2^{\scriptstyle b_{k}}b^{\prime})}}}}\sigma^{\prime\prime}

and
\overline{\tau^{\prime}}_{k}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(2^{\scriptstyle b_{k}}b^{\prime})}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime\prime}}
, and
for all
k\in K_{1}
,
\sigma^{\prime}_{k}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\bigl(2^{\scriptstyle(b_{k}-1)}b^{\prime}\bigr)}}}}\sigma^{\prime\prime}

and
\overline{\tau^{\prime}}_{k}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\bigl(2^{\scriptstyle(b_{k}-1)}b^{\prime}\bigr)}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime\prime}}
.

If
K_{1}=\emptyset
then
b=b^{\prime}
and we set
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}={\left\langle\lambda x\,{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau^{\prime\prime}}}
:
we obtain
\epsilon^{\prime}_{k}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(2^{\scriptstyle b_{k}}b)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime\prime}
, for all
k\in K=K_{0}
.

Otherwise,
b>b^{\prime}
and we set
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}=\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime\prime}\cdot\overline{\tau^{\prime\prime}}
so that:

•

for all
k\in K_{0}
,

\epsilon^{\prime}_{k}={\left\langle\lambda x\,{\sigma^{\prime}_{k}}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau^{\prime}}_{k}}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(2^{\scriptstyle b_{k}}b^{\prime}+1)}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{k}

with
2^{b_{k}}b^{\prime}+1\leq 2^{b_{k}}b
;

•

for all
k\in K_{1}
,
by the previous lemma,

\epsilon^{\prime}_{k}=\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}_{k}\cdot\overline{\tau^{\prime}}_{k}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(2^{\scriptstyle b_{k}}b^{\prime})}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{k}

and
2^{b_{k}}b^{\prime}<2^{b_{k}}b
.

We already know
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}
is not confluent,
and the counter examples we provided actually
show that no single
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}}
is confluent either.
Setting
19
19

19

Our notation is somehow abusive as
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{(\partial)}}}}}

is not of the form described in
Definition
5.10
:
there should not be any ambiguity as we have not
defined any relation
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(\partial)}}}}
.
Similarly, we may also write

\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{(\partial)}}}_{\mathfrak{E}}}}
for

\bigcup_{b\in\mathbf{N}}\mathord{\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}_{\mathfrak{E}}}}}

in the following.

\mathord{\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{(\partial)}}}}}}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left(\bigcup_{b\in\mathbf{N}}\mathord{\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}}}\right)}\subseteq\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}\times\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}

however, we will obtain a strongly confluent reduction relation, under the assumption
that
\mathbf{S}
has the following additive splitting property:
20
20

20

The additive splitting property was previously used by Carraro, Ehrhard and
Salibra
[
CES10
,
Car11
]
in their study of linear logic
exponentials with infinite multiplicities. There is no clear connection
between that work and our present contributions, though.

Definition 6.23
.

We say
\mathbf{S}
has the
additive splitting property
if:
whenever
a_{1}+a_{2}=b_{1}+b_{2}\in\mathbf{S}
, there exists

c_{1,1},c_{1,2},c_{2,1},c_{2,2}\in\mathbf{S}
such that

a_{i}=c_{i,1}+c_{i,2}
and
b_{j}=c_{1,j}+c_{2,j}
for
i,j\in{\left\{1,2\right\}}
.

This property is satisfied by any ring, but also by
the usual semirings of non-negative numbers (
\mathbf{N}
,

\mathbf{Q}^{+}
,
etc.
) as well as booleans.
We will in fact rely on the following generalization of the property to finite
families of finite sums of any size:

Lemma 6.24
.

Assume
\mathbf{S}
has the additive splitting property.
Let
a\in\mathbf{S}
,
J_{1},\dotsc,J_{n}
be finite sets and,
for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
,
let
{\left(b_{i,j}\right)}_{j\in J_{i}}\in\mathbf{S}^{J_{i}}
be a family
such that
a=\sum_{j\in J_{i}}b_{i,j}
.
Write
J=J_{1}\times\cdots\times J_{n}
and,
for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
, write
J^{\prime}_{i}=J_{1}\times\cdots\times J_{i-1}\times J_{i+1}\times\cdots\times J_{n}
.
Whenever
\overrightarrow{\jmath}^{\prime}=(j_{1},\dotsc,j_{i-1},j_{i+1},\dotsc,j_{n})\in J^{\prime}_{i}

and
j_{i}\in J_{i}
, write
\overrightarrow{\jmath}^{\prime}\cdot_{i}j_{i}=(j_{1},\dotsc,j_{n})\in J
.
Then there exists a family
{\left(c_{\overrightarrow{\jmath}}\right)}\in\mathbf{S}^{J}

such that, for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
and all
j\in J_{i}
,

b_{i,j}=\sum_{\overrightarrow{\jmath}^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}_{i}}c_{\overrightarrow{\jmath}^{\prime}\cdot_{i}j}
.

Proof 6.25
.

By induction on
n
, and then on
\mathord{\#}{J_{n}}
for
n>0
,
using the binary additive splitting property to enable the induction.

Lemma 6.26
.

Assume
\mathbf{S}
has the additive splitting property
and fix a
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}
-reduction structure
\mathfrak{E}
.
For all finite set
K
and all reductions
\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{(\partial)}}}_{\mathfrak{E}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{k}
for
k\in K
,
there is
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}
such that
\epsilon^{\prime}_{k}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{(\partial)}}}_{\mathfrak{E}}}}\epsilon^{\prime\prime}
for all
k\in K
.

Proof 6.27
.

For all
k\in K
, there are
b_{k}\in\mathbf{N}
,
a resource support
\mathcal{E}_{k}\in\mathfrak{E}
,
a set
I_{k}
of indices,
a family
{\left(a_{k,i}\right)}_{i\in I_{k}}
of scalars,
and summable families
{\left(e_{k,i}\right)}_{i\in I_{k}}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{I_{k}}
and

{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}_{k,i}\right)}_{i\in I_{k}}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}^{I_{k}}
such that

\epsilon=\sum_{i\in I_{k}}a_{k,i}.e_{k,i}
,

\epsilon^{\prime}_{k}=\sum_{i\in I_{k}}a_{k,i}.\epsilon^{\prime}_{k,i}

and
e_{k,i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{k})}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{k,i}
for all
i\in I_{k}
.

Write
\mathcal{E}={\left\{e_{k,i}\mathrel{;}k\in K,\ i\in I_{k}\right\}}
: since

\mathcal{E}\subseteq\bigcup_{k\in K}\mathcal{E}_{k}
and
\mathfrak{E}
is a resource structure,
we have
\mathcal{E}\in\mathfrak{E}
. Write
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}=\bigcup{\left\{{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}_{k,i}\right|}\mathrel{;}k\in K,\ i\in I_{k}\right\}}
:
since
\mathfrak{E}
is a reduction structure, we also have
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{E}
.

Now fix
e\in(\oc)\Delta
and write
a=\epsilon_{e}
.
For all
k\in K
, the set
I_{e,k}={\left\{i_{k}\in I_{k}\mathrel{;}e_{k,i_{k}}=e\right\}}

is finite, and then
\sum_{i_{k}\in I_{e,k}}a_{k,i_{k}}=a
.
Write
I_{e}=\prod_{k\in K}I_{e,k}
and, for all
k\in K
,

K^{\prime}_{k}=K\setminus{\left\{k\right\}}
and

I^{\prime}_{e,k}=\prod_{l\in K^{\prime}_{k}}I_{e,l}
.
If
\overrightarrow{\imath}={\left(i_{l}\right)}_{l\in K^{\prime}_{k}}\in I^{\prime}_{e,k}

and
i_{k}\in I_{e,k}
, write
\overrightarrow{\imath}\cdot_{k}i_{k}={\left(i_{k}\right)}_{k\in K}\in I_{e}
.
By Lemma
6.24
,
we obtain a family of scalars
{\left(a^{\prime}_{e,\overrightarrow{\imath}}\right)}_{\overrightarrow{\imath}\in I_{e}}

such that, for all
k\in K
and all
i_{k}\in I_{e,k}
,

a_{k,i_{k}}=\sum_{\overrightarrow{\imath}\in I^{\prime}_{e,k}}a^{\prime}_{e,\overrightarrow{\imath}\cdot_{k}i_{k}}
.
Moreover,
a=\sum_{\overrightarrow{\imath}\in I_{e}}a^{\prime}_{e,\overrightarrow{\imath}}
.

Since each
I_{e}
is finite, the family

{\left(e\right)}_{e\in(\oc)\Delta,\overrightarrow{\imath}\in I_{e}}
is summable.
Moreover, if we fix
k\in K
and
i_{k}\in I_{k}
,
there are finitely many
e\in(\oc)\Delta
and
\overrightarrow{\imath}\in I^{\prime}_{e,k}

such that
\overrightarrow{\imath}\cdot_{k}i_{k}\in I_{e}
:
indeed in this case
e=e_{k,i_{k}}
.
Since
{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}_{k,i_{k}}\right)}_{i_{k}\in I_{k}}
is summable too,
it follows that
{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}_{k,i_{k}}\right)}_{e\in(\oc)\Delta,\overrightarrow{\imath}\in I_{e}}
is summable.
By associativity, we obtain

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in(\oc)\Delta\\
\overrightarrow{\imath}\in I_{e}\end{subarray}}a^{\prime}_{e,\overrightarrow{\imath}}.e=\sum_{e\in(\oc)\Delta}{\left(\sum_{\overrightarrow{\imath}\in I_{e}}a^{\prime}_{e,\overrightarrow{\imath}}\right)}e=\epsilon

and

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in(\oc)\Delta\\
\overrightarrow{\imath}\in I_{e}\end{subarray}}a^{\prime}_{e,\overrightarrow{\imath}}.\epsilon^{\prime}_{k,i_{k}}=\sum_{i_{k}\in I_{k}}{\left(\sum_{\overrightarrow{\imath}\in I^{\prime}_{e_{k,i_{k}},k}}a^{\prime}_{e_{k,i_{k}},\overrightarrow{\imath}\cdot_{k}i_{k}}\right)}\epsilon^{\prime}_{k,i_{k}}=\epsilon^{\prime}_{k}

for all
k\in K
.

Write
b=\sum_{k\in K}b_{k}
.
For all
e\in(\oc)\Delta
and all
\overrightarrow{\imath}={\left(i_{k}\right)}_{k\in K}\in I_{e}
,
we have
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(b_{k})}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{k,i_{k}}
for all
k\in K
hence
Lemma
6.21
gives

\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{e,\overrightarrow{\imath}}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}

such that
\epsilon^{\prime}_{k,i_{k}}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(2^{\scriptstyle b_{k}}b)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{e,\overrightarrow{\imath}}
for all
k\in K
.
Moreover, for all
k\in K
and
e^{\prime\prime}\in(\oc)\Delta
,
if
e^{\prime\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{e,\overrightarrow{\imath}}\right|}
then there is
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}_{k,i_{k}}\right|}

such that
e^{\prime}\Succ_{{(2^{\scriptstyle b_{k}}b)}}e^{\prime\prime}
, and then
e\Succ_{{(b_{k})}}e^{\prime}
:
it follows that
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}\leq 4^{b_{k}+2^{b_{k}}b}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime\prime}\right)}
and
\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}=\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e^{\prime\prime}\right)}
.
Since each
I_{e}
is finite, there are finitely many pairs

{\left(e,\overrightarrow{\imath}\right)}\in\sum_{e\in(\oc)\Delta}I_{e}

such that
e^{\prime\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{e,\overrightarrow{\imath}}\right|}
.
Hence the family
{\left(\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{e,\overrightarrow{\imath}}\right)}_{e\in(\oc)\Delta,\overrightarrow{\imath}\in I_{e}}

is summable. Recall moreover that
\epsilon^{\prime}_{k,i_{k}}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\right]}
for all
k\in K
and
i_{k}\in I_{k}
:
we obtain

\epsilon^{\prime}_{k}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{(2^{\scriptstyle b_{k}}b)}}}_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in(\oc)\Delta\\
\overrightarrow{\imath}\in I_{e}\end{subarray}}a^{\prime}_{e,\overrightarrow{\imath}}.\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{e,\overrightarrow{\imath}}

for all
k\in K
, which concludes the proof.

6.3.
Bounded depth of substitution

In the previous subsection, we relied on the additive splitting property
to establish the confluence of
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{(\partial)}}}}}
: this is because there
is no maximal way to
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{(b)}}}}}
-reduce a resource vector,
hence we must track precisely the different redexes that are fired
in each reduction of a critical pair.

We can get rid of this hypothesis by considering a more uniform bound
on reductions. A first intuition would be to bound the depth at
which redexes are fired, but as with
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(m|k\right)}}}}
this boundedness
condition is not preserved in residuals: rather, we have to bound
the depth at which variables are substituted.
First recall from Definition
3.1
that
\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s\right)}=\max\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(s\right)}
is the maximum depth of an occurrence of
x
in

s
. Then:

Definition 6.28
.

We define a family of relations
{\left(\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\right)}_{d\in{\mathbf{N}}}

inductively as follows:

•

e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 0\rfloor}}}}e
for all
e\in(\oc)\Delta
;

•

x\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d+1\rfloor}}}}x
for all
x\in\mathcal{V}
;

•

\lambda x\,{s}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d+1\rfloor}}}}\lambda x\,{\sigma^{\prime}}
if
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
;

•

{\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d+1\rfloor}}}}{\left\langle\sigma^{\prime}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau^{\prime}}}

if
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d+1\rfloor}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
;

•

{\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{r}\right]}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d+1\rfloor}}}}{\left[\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{r}\right]}

if
s_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d+1\rfloor}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,r\right\}}
;

•

{\left\langle\lambda x\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d+1\rfloor}}}}\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\tau^{\prime}}

if
\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s\right)}\leq d
,

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
.

It should be clear that if
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}

then
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(d)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
, and moreover
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}

for all
d^{\prime}\geq d
. We also have
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}\rfloor}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}

as soon as
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
.

Definition 6.29
.

For all
e\in(\oc)\Delta
we define
the
full parallel reduct
\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(e\right)}
at substitution depth
d
of
e
by induction on the pair
(d,e)
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor 0\rfloor}}{\left(e\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}e

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d+1\rfloor}}{\left(x\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}x

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d+1\rfloor}}{\left(\lambda x\,{s}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda x\,{\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(s\right)}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d+1\rfloor}}{\left({\left\langle\lambda x\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\partial_{x}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(s\right)}\cdot\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}

(if
\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s\right)}\leq d
)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d+1\rfloor}}{\left({\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\langle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d+1\rfloor}}{\left(s\right)}\right\rangle}\,{\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}}

(in the other cases)

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d+1\rfloor}}{\left({\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{n}\right]}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left[\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d+1\rfloor}}{\left(s_{1}\right)},\dotsc,\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d+1\rfloor}}{\left(s_{n}\right)}\right]}

Then if
\epsilon=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}
,
we set
\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(e_{i}\right)}
.

Lemma 6.30
.

For all
e\in(\oc)\Delta
,
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(e\right)}
.

Proof 6.31
.

By a straightforward induction on
d
then on
e
.

It follows that
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(d)}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(e\right)}
, hence if
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(e\right)}\right|}
then
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}\leq 4^{d}\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}
.
In particular
\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}
defines a linear-continuous function on
\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
.

Lemma 6.32
.

If
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d_{0}\rfloor}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
,

\overline{u}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d_{1}\rfloor}}}}{\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}}

and
d\geq\max{\left({\left\{d_{0}\right\}}\mathbin{\cup}{\left\{d_{x}+d_{1}-1\mathrel{;}d_{x}\in\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(e\right)}\right\}}\right)}
then

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{u}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\partial_{x}\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}
.

Proof 6.33
.

Write
n=\mathord{\#}{\overline{u}}
,
\overline{u}={\left[u_{1},\dotsc,u_{n}\right]}

and
\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}={\left[\upsilon^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\upsilon^{\prime}_{n}\right]}
so that

u_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d_{1}\rfloor}}}}\upsilon^{\prime}_{i}
for
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
.

The proof is by induction on the reduction
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d_{0}\rfloor}}}}{\epsilon^{\prime}}
.
We treat the cases
d_{0}=0
and
d_{0}>0
uniformly by a further induction on
e
,
setting
d^{\prime}_{0}=\max{\left\{0,d_{0}-1\right\}}
.

If
d_{0}=d^{\prime}_{0}+1
,
e={\left\langle\lambda y\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}

and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\partial_{y}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\tau}^{\prime}

with
y\not\in{\left\{x\right\}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\mathbin{\cup}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\overline{u}\right)}
,

\mathbf{md}_{y}{\left(s\right)}\leq{d^{\prime}_{0}}
,

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}_{0}\rfloor}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and

\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}_{0}\rfloor}}}}\overline{\tau}^{\prime}
,
then we have

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{u}=\sum_{(I,J)\text{ partition of }{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}}{\left\langle\lambda y\,{\left(\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}_{I}\right)}\right\rangle}\,{\partial_{x}\overline{t}\cdot\overline{u}_{J}}

and

\partial_{x}\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}=\sum_{(I,J)\text{ partition of }{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}}\partial_{y}{\left(\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}_{I}\right)}\cdot{\left(\partial_{x}\overline{t}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}_{J}\right)}.

Observe that
d>0
and
d-1\geq\max{\left\{d^{\prime}_{0}\right\}}\mathbin{\cup}{\left\{d^{\prime}_{x}+d_{1}-1\mathrel{;}d^{\prime}_{x}\in\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(s\right)}\mathbin{\cup}\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\right\}}
.
By induction hypothesis,
we obtain
\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}_{I}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d-1\rfloor}}}}\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}_{I}

and
\partial_{x}\overline{t}\cdot\overline{u}_{J}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d-1\rfloor}}}}\partial_{x}\overline{\tau}^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}_{J}
,
and we conclude since
\mathbf{md}_{y}{\left(\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}_{I}\right)}=\mathbf{md}_{y}{\left(s\right)}\leq d^{\prime}_{0}\leq d-1
.

If
e=y=\epsilon^{\prime}
, with
y\not=x
, then
\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{u}=\partial_{x}\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}=y

and we conclude directly by the definition of
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}
.

If
e=x=\epsilon^{\prime}
, then
\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(e\right)}={\left\{1\right\}}
hence
d\geq d_{1}
and we conclude since

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{u}=\overline{u}
,
\partial_{x}\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}=\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}

and
\overline{u}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d_{1}\rfloor}}}}{\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}}
.

If
e=\lambda y\,{s}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\lambda y\,{\sigma^{\prime}}
with
y\not\in{\left\{x\right\}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\overline{u}\right)}

and
s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}_{0}\rfloor}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
, then
write
d^{\prime}=\max{\left\{d^{\prime}_{0}\right\}}\mathbin{\cup}{\left\{d^{\prime}_{x}+d_{1}-1\mathrel{;}d^{\prime}_{x}\in\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(s\right)}\right\}}
.
By induction hypothesis,
we obtain
\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}
.
Observe that either
d=d^{\prime}+1
or
d=d^{\prime}=0
(in that latter case,

\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}=\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}
),
and then we conclude since

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{u}=\lambda y\,{\left(\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}\right)}

and

\partial_{x}\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}=\lambda y\,{\left(\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}\right)}
.

If
e={\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}

and
\epsilon^{\prime}={\left\langle\sigma^{\prime}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau}^{\prime}}
, with

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d_{0}\rfloor}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and

\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}_{0}\rfloor}}}}\overline{\tau}^{\prime}
,
then we have

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{u}=\sum_{(I,J)\text{ partition of }{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}}{\left\langle\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}_{I}\right\rangle}\,{\partial_{x}\overline{t}\cdot\overline{u}_{J}}

and

\partial_{x}\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}=\sum_{(I,J)\text{ partition of }{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}}{\left\langle\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}_{I}\right\rangle}\,{{\left(\partial_{x}\overline{t}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}_{J}\right)}}.

Write
d^{\prime}=\max{\left\{d^{\prime}_{0}\right\}}\mathbin{\cup}{\left\{d^{\prime}_{x}+d_{1}-1\mathrel{;}d^{\prime}_{x}\in\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\right\}}
.
By induction hypothesis,
we obtain
\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}_{I}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}_{I}

and
\partial_{x}\overline{t}\cdot\overline{u}_{J}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\partial_{x}\overline{\tau}^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}_{J}
.
Then we conclude observing that
d=d^{\prime}+1
or
d=d^{\prime}=0
(in that latter case,

\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{u}_{I}=\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}_{I}
and

\partial_{x}\overline{t}\cdot\overline{u}_{J}=\partial_{x}\overline{\tau}^{\prime}\cdot\overline{u}^{\prime}_{J}
).

If
e={\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{k}\right]}

and
\epsilon^{\prime}={\left[\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{k}\right]}
, with

s_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d_{0}\rfloor}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
for
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}}
,
then we have

\partial_{x}e\cdot\overline{u}=\sum_{(I_{1},\dotsc,I_{k})\text{ partition of }{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}}{\left[\partial_{x}s_{1}\cdot\overline{u}_{I_{1}}\dotsc,\partial_{x}s_{k}\cdot\overline{u}_{I_{k}}\right]}

and

\partial_{x}\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\upsilon}^{\prime}=\sum_{(I_{1},\dotsc,I_{k})\text{ partition of }{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}}{\left[\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{I_{1}}\dotsc,\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}_{k}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{I_{k}}\right]}.

By induction hypothesis, we obtain

{\left[\partial_{x}s_{1}\cdot\overline{u}_{I_{1}}\dotsc,\partial_{x}s_{k}\cdot\overline{u}_{I_{k}}\right]}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}{\left[\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}_{1}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{I_{1}}\dotsc,\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}_{k}\cdot\overline{\upsilon^{\prime}}_{I_{k}}\right]}

for all partition
(I_{1},\dotsc,I_{k})
of
{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
and we conclude.

Lemma 6.34
.

If
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}\right|}

then
\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}\leq 2^{d}\max{\left\{d,\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(e\right)}\right\}}
.

Proof 6.35
.

By induction on the reduction
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
.

If
d=0
, then
e^{\prime}=\epsilon^{\prime}=e
and the result is trivial.
For the other inductive cases, write
d=d^{\prime}+1
.

If
e={\left\langle\lambda y\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}

and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\partial_{y}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\tau}^{\prime}
with

\mathbf{md}_{y}{\left(s\right)}\leq d^{\prime}
,

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\overline{\tau}^{\prime}
,
choosing
y\not\in{\left\{x\right\}}\mathbin{\cup}\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}
,
then
e^{\prime}\in{\bigl|\partial_{y}s^{\prime}\cdot\overline{t}^{\prime}\bigr|}
with

s^{\prime}\in{\left|\sigma^{\prime}\right|}
and
\overline{t}^{\prime}\in{\left|\overline{\tau}^{\prime}\right|}
.
By induction hypothesis,
\mathbf{md}_{z}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}\leq 2^{d^{\prime}}\max{\left\{d^{\prime},\mathbf{md}_{z}{\left(s\right)}\right\}}

and
\mathbf{md}_{z}{(\overline{t}^{\prime})}\leq 2^{d^{\prime}}\max{\left\{d^{\prime},\mathbf{md}_{z}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\right\}}

for any
z\in\mathcal{V}
.
By Lemma
3.3
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\max{\left(\mathbf{d}_{x}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}\mathbin{\cup}{\left\{d^{\prime}_{y}+d^{\prime}_{x}-1\mathrel{;}d^{\prime}_{y}\in\mathbf{d}_{y}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)},\ d^{\prime}_{x}\in\mathbf{d}_{x}{(\overline{t}^{\prime})}\right\}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\max{\left\{2^{d^{\prime}}\max{\left\{d^{\prime},\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s\right)}\right\}},2^{d^{\prime}}\max{\left\{d^{\prime},\mathbf{md}_{y}{\left(s\right)}\right\}}+2^{d^{\prime}}\max{\left\{d^{\prime},\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\right\}}\right\}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{d^{\prime}+1}\max{\left\{d^{\prime},\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s\right)},\mathbf{md}_{y}{\left(s\right)},\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\right\}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{d}\max{\left\{d,\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(e\right)}\right\}}.

If
e=\lambda y\,{s}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\lambda y\,{\sigma^{\prime}}
with

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
,
choosing
y\not=x
,
then
e^{\prime}=\lambda x\,{s^{\prime}}
with

s^{\prime}\in{\left|\sigma^{\prime}\right|}
.
By induction hypothesis,
\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}\leq 2^{d^{\prime}}\max{\left\{d^{\prime},\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s\right)}\right\}}
.
Then
\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}\leq\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}+1\leq 2^{d^{\prime}}\max{\left\{d^{\prime},\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s\right)}\right\}}+1\leq 2^{d}\max{\left\{d,\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s\right)}\right\}}\leq 2^{d}\max{\left\{d,\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(e\right)}\right\}}
.

If
e={\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}={\left\langle\sigma^{\prime}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau}^{\prime}}
with

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\overline{\tau}^{\prime}
,
then
e^{\prime}={\left\langle s^{\prime}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}^{\prime}}
with

s^{\prime}\in{\left|\sigma^{\prime}\right|}
and
\overline{t}^{\prime}\in{\left|\overline{\tau}^{\prime}\right|}
.
By induction hypothesis,
\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}\leq 2^{d}\max{\left\{d,\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s\right)}\right\}}

and
\mathbf{md}_{x}{(\overline{t}^{\prime})}\leq 2^{d^{\prime}}\max{\left\{d^{\prime},\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\right\}}
.
Then:

\displaystyle\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\max{\left\{\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)},\mathbf{md}_{x}{\bigl(\overline{t}^{\prime}\bigr)}+1\right\}}

\displaystyle\leq\max{\left\{2^{d}\max{\left\{d,\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s\right)}\right\}},2^{d^{\prime}}\max{\left\{d^{\prime},\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\right\}}+1\right\}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{d}\max{\left\{d,\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s\right)},\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\right\}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{d}\max{\left\{d,\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(e\right)}\right\}}.

If
e={\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{k}\right]}

and
\epsilon^{\prime}={\left[\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{k}\right]}
, with

s_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}}
,
then
e^{\prime}={\left[s^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,s^{\prime}_{k}\right]}

with
s^{\prime}_{i}\in{\left|\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\right|}
for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}}
.
By induction hypothesis, for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}}
,

\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s^{\prime}_{i}\right)}\leq 2^{d}\max{\left\{d,\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s_{i}\right)}\right\}}
,
hence

\displaystyle\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}

\displaystyle=\max{\left\{\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s^{\prime}_{1}\right)},\dotsc,\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s^{\prime}_{k}\right)}\right\}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{d}\max{\left\{d,\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s_{1}\right)},\dotsc,\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s_{k}\right)}\right\}}

\displaystyle=2^{d}\max{\left\{d,\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(e\right)}\right\}}.

Lemma 6.36
.

If
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
then

\epsilon^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d}d\rfloor}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(e\right)}
.

Proof 6.37
.

By induction on the reduction
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
.

If
d=0
, then
\epsilon^{\prime}=e
and the result follows from Lemma
6.30
.
For the other inductive cases, set
d=d^{\prime}+1
.

If
e={\left\langle\lambda x\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}

and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\tau}^{\prime}

with
\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s\right)}\leq{d^{\prime}}
,

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and

\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\overline{\tau}^{\prime}

then by induction hypothesis,
we have
\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d^{\prime}}d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}{\left(s\right)}

and
\overline{\tau}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d^{\prime}}d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}
.
By the previous lemma,
we moreover have
\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(\sigma^{\prime}\right)}\leq 2^{d^{\prime}}\max{\left\{d^{\prime},\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(s\right)}\right\}}=2^{d^{\prime}}d^{\prime}
.
It follows that
2^{d}d\geq 2^{d^{\prime}}d^{\prime}
and
2^{d}d\geq\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(\sigma^{\prime}\right)}+2^{d^{\prime}}d^{\prime}-1

hence we can apply Lemma
6.32

to obtain

\epsilon^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d}{d}\rfloor}}}}\partial_{x}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}{\left(s\right)}\cdot\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(e\right)}
.

If
e=\lambda y\,{s}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\lambda y\,{\sigma^{\prime}}
with

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
,
then by induction hypothesis,

\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d^{\prime}}d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}{\left(s\right)}
,
hence

\epsilon^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d^{\prime}}d^{\prime}+1\rfloor}}}}\lambda x\,{\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}{\left(s\right)}}=\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(e\right)}

and we conclude since
2^{d^{\prime}}d^{\prime}+1\leq 2^{d}d
.

If
e={\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}={\left\langle\sigma^{\prime}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau}^{\prime}}
with

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\overline{\tau}^{\prime}
,
there are two subcases:

•

If moreover
s=\lambda x\,{u}
and
\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(u\right)}\leq d^{\prime}
then

\sigma^{\prime}=\lambda x\,{\upsilon^{\prime}}
with

u\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\upsilon^{\prime}
.
Then by induction hypothesis,

\upsilon^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d^{\prime}}d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}{\left(u\right)}
,
and

\overline{\tau}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d^{\prime}}d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}
.
By the previous lemma,
we moreover have
\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(\upsilon^{\prime}\right)}\leq 2^{d^{\prime}}\max{\left\{d^{\prime},\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(u\right)}\right\}}=2^{d^{\prime}}d^{\prime}
,
hence

\epsilon^{\prime}={\left\langle\lambda x\,{\upsilon^{\prime}}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau}^{\prime}}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d^{\prime}}d^{\prime}+1\rfloor}}}}\partial_{x}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}{\left(u\right)}\cdot\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(e\right)}
,
and we conclude since
2^{d^{\prime}}d^{\prime}+1\leq 2^{d}d
.

•

Otherwise
s
is not an abstraction or
s=\lambda x\,{u}
with
\mathbf{md}_{x}{\left(u\right)}>d^{\prime}
.
By induction hypothesis,

\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d}d\rfloor}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(\sigma^{\prime}\right)}
,
and

\overline{\tau}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d^{\prime}}d^{\prime}\rfloor}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}
.
Since
2^{d^{\prime}}d^{\prime}<2^{d}d
, we obtain

\overline{\tau}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d}d-1\rfloor}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}

and then

\epsilon^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d}d\rfloor}}}}{\left\langle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(s\right)}\right\rangle}\,{\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d^{\prime}\rfloor}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}}=\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(e\right)}
.

If
e={\left[s_{1},\dotsc,s_{k}\right]}

and
\epsilon^{\prime}={\left[\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{k}\right]}
, with

s_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}}
,
then by induction hypothesis, for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}}
,

\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d}d\rfloor}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(s_{i}\right)}
and we conclude directly.

Lemma 6.38
.

For all
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}
-reduction structure
\mathfrak{E}
,
if
\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}_{\mathfrak{E}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}

then
\epsilon^{\prime}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d}d\rfloor}}}_{\mathfrak{E}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}\right)}
.

Proof 6.39
.

Assume there is
\mathcal{E}\in\mathfrak{E}
, summable families

{\left(e_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}\in\mathcal{E}^{I}
and

{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}^{I}
,
and a family of scalars

{\left(a_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}

such that
\epsilon=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.e_{i}
,

\epsilon^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}

and
e_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in I
.
Write
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i\in I}{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right|}
:
since
\mathfrak{E}
is a reduction structure,
we obtain
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{E}
.
The family
{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(e_{i}\right)}\right)}_{i\in I}
is summable,
and by the previous lemma,
\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d}d\rfloor}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(e_{i}\right)}

for all
i\in I
.
We conclude
that
\epsilon^{\prime}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor 2^{d}d\rfloor}}}}}\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(e_{i}\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}
.

Similarly to
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{(\partial)}}}}}
, we set

\mathord{\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor\partial\rfloor}}}}}}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\bigcup_{d\in\mathbf{N}}\mathord{\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}}}}}

and we obtain:

Corollary 6.40
.

For all
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}
-reduction structure
\mathfrak{E}

and all
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,\epsilon^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
such that

\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor\partial\rfloor}}}_{\mathfrak{E}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
for
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
,
there exists
d\in\mathbf{N}
such that

\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor\partial\rfloor}}}_{\mathfrak{E}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor d\rfloor}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}
for
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
.

6.4.
Parallel reduction of resource vectors of bounded height

Recall that we have
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
iff
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\left(\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}|\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}\right)}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}

iff
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)})}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
iff
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}\rfloor}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
.

Definition 6.41
.

We say a resource vector
\epsilon\in\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
is
bounded
if
{\left\{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}\mathrel{;}e\in{\left|\epsilon\right|}\right\}}
is finite.
We then write
\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}=\max{\left\{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}\mathrel{;}e\in{\left|\epsilon\right|}\right\}}
.

If
\mathcal{E}\subseteq(\oc)\Delta
, we also write

\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\mathcal{E}\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}\mathrel{;}e\in\mathcal{E}\right\}}

and then

(\oc)\mathfrak{B}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\{\mathcal{E}\subseteq(\oc)\Delta\mathrel{;}\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\mathcal{E}\right)}\text{ and }\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\mathcal{E}\right)}\text{ are finite}\right\}}

which is a resource structure (see Definition
5.9
).
Indeed,
(\oc)\mathfrak{B}\subseteq(\oc)\mathfrak{F}_{\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}}
,
and if we write

(\oc)\Delta_{h,V}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\{e\in(\oc)\Delta\mathrel{;}\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}\leq h\text{ and }\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}\subseteq V\right\}}

for all
h\in\mathbf{N}
and all
V\subseteq\mathcal{V}
,
we have

(\oc)\mathfrak{B}={\left\{(\oc)\Delta_{h,V}\mathrel{;}h\in\mathbf{N}\text{ and }V\in\mathord{\mathfrak{P}_{f}}{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}\right\}}^{\perp\perp}
:
this is a consequence of a generic
transport lemma

[
TV16
]
.
The semimodule of bounded resource vectors is then
\mathbf{S}{\left\langle(\oc)\mathfrak{B}\right\rangle}
.

Lemma 6.42
.

For all
h\in\mathbf{N}
and
V\in\mathord{\mathfrak{P}_{f}}{\left(\mathcal{V}\right)}
,

{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(e\right)}\right)}_{e\in(\oc)\Delta_{h,V}}
is summable.
Moreover, for all
\epsilon\in\mathbf{S}{\left\langle(\oc)\mathfrak{B}\right\rangle}
, we have

{\left|\epsilon\right|}\subseteq(\oc)\Delta_{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\epsilon\right)},\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}}
and then,
setting
\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\sum_{e\in{\left|\epsilon\right|}}\epsilon_{e}.\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(e\right)}
,
we obtain
\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{{\left|\epsilon\right|}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}
.

Proof 6.43
.

Follows from Lemmas
6.30
and
6.17

using the fact that, if
\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}\leq h
then
\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(e\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{F}}_{{\lfloor h\rfloor}}{\left(e\right)}
.

If
\mathbf{S}
is zerosumfree, we have:

\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
iff
\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}\rfloor}}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
as soon as
\epsilon
is bounded.
More generally, without any assumption on
\mathbf{S}
,
we have

\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{(\oc)\Delta_{h,V}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}

iff
\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor h\rfloor}}}_{(\oc)\Delta_{h,V}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
.
We can moreover show that bounded vectors are stable under
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{(\oc)\mathfrak{B}}}}
:

Lemma 6.44
.

If
e\Succ_{\partial}e^{\prime}
then
\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}\leq 2^{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}}{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}}
.

Proof 6.45
.

The proof is by induction on the reduction
e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
such that
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}\right|}
,
and is very similar to that of Lemma
6.34
.
We detail only the base case.

If
e={\left\langle\lambda x\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}

and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\tau}^{\prime}
with

s\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and
\overline{t}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\overline{\tau}^{\prime}
.
Then
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\partial_{x}s^{\prime}\cdot\overline{t^{\prime}}\right|}
with

s^{\prime}\in{\left|\sigma^{\prime}\right|}
and
\overline{t^{\prime}}\in{\left|\overline{\tau}^{\prime}\right|}
.
By induction,
\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}\leq 2^{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(s\right)}}\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(s\right)}

and
\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)}\leq 2^{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}}\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}
.
By Lemma
3.2
,

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}+\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\overline{t^{\prime}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(s\right)}}\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(s\right)}+2^{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}}\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq 2\times 2^{\max{\left\{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(s\right)},\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\right\}}}\max{\left\{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(s\right)},\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\right\}}

\displaystyle<2^{\max{\left\{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(s\right)},\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\right\}}+1}{\left(\max{\left\{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(s\right)},\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)}\right\}}+1\right)}

\displaystyle=2^{\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}}\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}.

It follows that
(\oc)\mathfrak{B}
is a
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}
-reduction structure:
since
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{(\oc)\mathfrak{B}}}}
coincides with
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor\partial\rfloor}}}_{(\oc)\mathfrak{B}}}}
,
Corollary
6.40
entails that

\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{(\oc)\mathfrak{B}}}}
is strongly confluent.
We can even refine this result following Lemma
6.42
.
First, let us call
bounded reduction structure
any
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}
-reduction
structure
\mathfrak{E}
such that
\mathfrak{E}\subseteq(\oc)\mathfrak{B}
.
Then Lemma
6.38
entails:

Corollary 6.46
.

For all bounded reduction structure
\mathfrak{E}
,
and all reduction
\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{E}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
,

\epsilon^{\prime}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{E}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}
.

It should moreover be clear that
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}
is bounded for all
M\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}
.
In the next section, we show that
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{(\oc)\mathfrak{B}}}}
allows to simulate
parallel
\beta
-reduction via Taylor expansion.
21
21

21

Observe that it is possible to establish Corollary

6.46
quite directly, following the
proof of Lemma
6.38
, and using only Lemma

6.44
and a variant of Lemma

6.17
(replacing
b
with
\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(e\right)}
).
This is the path adopted in the extended abstract

[
Vau17
]
presented at
CSL 2017
.

7.
Simulating
\beta
-reduction under Taylor expansion

From now on, for all
M,N\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}
, we write

M\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}N
if
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}\mathord{\tau}{\left(N\right)}
.
More generally, for all
M\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}
and all
\sigma\in\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
,
we write
M\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}\sigma
(resp.
\sigma\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}M
) if
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}\sigma

(resp.
\sigma\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}
).
We will show in Subsection
7.1
that

M\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}N
as soon as
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}N

where
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}
is the parallel
\beta
-reduction defined as follows:

Definition 7.1
.

We define
parallel
\beta
-reduction
on algebraic terms

\mathord{\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}}\subseteq\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}\times\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}

by the following inductive rules:

•

x\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}x
;

•

if
S\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}
then
\lambda x\,{S}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}\lambda x\,{M^{\prime}}
;

•

if
S\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}
and
N\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}N^{\prime}
then

{\left(S\right)}\,{N}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}\,{N^{\prime}}
;

•

if
S\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}
and
N\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}N^{\prime}
then

{\left(\lambda x\,{S}\right)}\,{N}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}{\left[N^{\prime}/x\right]}
;

•

0\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}0
;

•

if
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}
then
a.M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}a.M^{\prime}
;

•

if
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}
and
N\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}N^{\prime}
then
M+N\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}+N^{\prime}
.

In particular, if
1\in\mathbf{S}
admits an opposite element
-1\in\mathbf{S}

then
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{(\oc)\mathfrak{B}}}}
is degenerate. Indeed, we can consider
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}
up to
the equality of vector
\lambda
-terms by setting
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\widetilde{\beta}}}}N
if
there are
M^{\prime}\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}M
and
N^{\prime}\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}N
such that
M^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}N^{\prime}
.
Since
\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}
subsumes
\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}
, the results of Subsection
7.1

will imply that
M\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{(\oc)\mathfrak{B}}}}N
as soon as
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\widetilde{\beta}}}}N
.
If
-1\in\mathbf{S}
, we have
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\widetilde{\beta}}^{*}}}N

for all
M,N\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}
by Example
4.1
,
hence
M\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{(\oc)\mathfrak{B}}^{*}}}N
.

Using reduction structures, we will nonetheless be able to define a consistent
reduction relation containing
\beta
-reduction, but restricted to
those algebraic
\lambda
-terms that have a normalizable Taylor expansion, in the
sense to be defined in Section
8
.

On the other hand, even assuming
\mathbf{S}
is zerosumfree,
Taylor expansions are not stable under
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}
:
if
M\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{B}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
, we know from the previous
section that
\sigma^{\prime}
is bounded and
M\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{{\lfloor\partial\rfloor}}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
,
but there is no reason why
\sigma^{\prime}
would be the Taylor expansion of
an algebraic
\lambda
-term.

We do know, however, that
\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{B}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}
, which
will allow us to obtain a weak conservativity result w.r.t. parallel
\beta
-reduction:
for all reduction
M\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}}}\sigma^{\prime}

there is a reduction
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}^{*}}}M^{\prime}

such that
\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}}}M^{\prime}
,
i.e.
any
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{B}}}}
-reduction sequence from a Taylor expansion can be completed into a
parallel
\beta
-reduction sequence (Subsection
7.2
).
Restricted to normalizable pure
\lambda
-terms, this will enable us to obtain an actual
conservativity result.

7.1.
Simulation of parallel
\beta
-reduction

We show that
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{(\oc)\mathfrak{B}}}}
allows to simulate
\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}

on
\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
, without any particular assumption
on
\mathbf{S}
.

Lemma 7.2
.

If
\sigma\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathcal{S}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
and

\overline{\tau}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\overline{\mathcal{T}}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
then

{\left\langle\lambda x\,{\sigma}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau}}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{{\left\langle\lambda x\,{\mathcal{S}}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\mathcal{T}}}}}}\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
.

Proof 7.3
.

Assume there are summable families

{\left(s_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}
,

{\left(\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}
,

{\left(\overline{t}_{j}\right)}_{j\in J}
and

{\left(\overline{\tau}^{\prime}_{j}\right)}_{j\in J}
,
and families of scalars

{\left(a_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}\in\mathcal{S}^{I}
and
{\left(b_{j}\right)}_{j\in J}\in\overline{\mathcal{T}}^{J}

such that:

•

\sigma=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.s_{i}
,

\sigma^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.\sigma^{\prime}_{i}

and
s_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in I
;

•

\overline{\tau}=\sum_{j\in J}b_{j}.\overline{t}_{j}
,

\overline{\tau}^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in J}b_{j}.\overline{\tau}^{\prime}_{j}
,
and
\overline{t}_{j}\in\overline{\mathcal{T}}
and
\overline{t}_{j}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\overline{\tau}^{\prime}_{j}
for all
j\in J
.

By multilinear-continuity, the families

{\left({\left\langle\lambda x\,{s_{i}}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}_{j}}\right)}_{i\in I,j\in J}

and

{\left(\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\overline{\tau}^{\prime}_{j}\right)}_{i\in I,j\in J}

are summable,

{\left\langle\lambda x\,{\sigma}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau}}=\sum_{i\in I,j\in J}a_{i}b_{j}.{\left\langle\lambda x\,{s_{i}}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}_{j}}

and

\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\overline{\tau}^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in I,j\in J}a_{i}b_{j}.\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\overline{\tau}^{\prime}_{j}
.
It is then sufficient to observe that

{\left\langle\lambda x\,{s_{i}}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}_{j}}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\partial_{x}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\overline{\tau}^{\prime}_{j}

for all
(i,j)\in I\times J
.

The additional requirement on resource supports is straightforwardly satisfied, since

{\left\langle\lambda x\,{s_{i}}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}_{j}}\in{\left\langle\lambda x\,{\mathcal{S}}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\mathcal{T}}}
for all
(i,j)\in I\times J
.

Lemma 7.4
.

If
\sigma\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathcal{S}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
then

\lambda x\,{\sigma}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\lambda x\,{\mathcal{S}}}}}\lambda x\,{\sigma^{\prime}}
.
If moreover

\overline{\tau}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\overline{\mathcal{T}}}}}\overline{\tau^{\prime}}
then

{\left\langle\sigma\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau}}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{{\left\langle\mathcal{S}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\mathcal{T}}}}}}{\left\langle\sigma^{\prime}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau^{\prime}}}
.

Proof 7.5
.

Similarly to the previous lemma, each result follows from the
multilinear-continuity of syntactic operators, and the contextuality of

\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}
.

Lemma 7.6
.

If
\sigma\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathcal{S}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
then

{\sigma}^{\oc}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\oc}}}}{{\sigma^{\prime}}}^{\oc}
.

Proof 7.7
.

Assume there are summable families

{\left(s_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}
and

{\left(\sigma^{\prime}_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}
,
and a family of scalars

{\left(a_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}

such that
\sigma=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.s_{i}
,

\sigma^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.\sigma^{\prime}_{i}

and
s_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in I
.

Then by multilinear-continuity of the monomial construction,
for all
n\in\mathbf{N}
,
the families

{\left({\left[s_{i_{1}},\dotsc,s_{i_{n}}\right]}\right)}_{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{n}\in I}

and

{\left({\left[\sigma^{\prime}_{i_{1}},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{i_{n}}\right]}\right)}_{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{n}\in I}

are summable, and

{\sigma}^{n}=\sum_{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{n}\in I}a_{i_{1}}\cdots a_{i_{n}}{\left[s_{i_{1}},\dotsc,s_{i_{n}}\right]}

and

{\sigma^{\prime}}^{n}=\sum_{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{n}\in I}a_{i_{1}}\cdots a_{i_{n}}.{\left[\sigma^{\prime}_{i_{1}},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{i_{n}}\right]}.

Since the supports of the monomial vectors
\sigma^{n}
(resp.
{\sigma^{\prime}}^{n}
)
for
n\in\mathbf{N}
are pairwise disjoint,
we obtain that the families

{\left({\left[s_{i_{1}},\dotsc,s_{i_{n}}\right]}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbf{N}\\
i_{1},\dotsc,i_{n}\in I\end{subarray}}

and

{\left({\left[\sigma^{\prime}_{i_{1}},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{i_{n}}\right]}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbf{N}\\
i_{1},\dotsc,i_{n}\in I\end{subarray}}

are summable,
and

{\sigma}^{\oc}=\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}\frac{1}{n\,\mathord{!}}.{\sigma}^{n}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbf{N}\\
i_{1},\dotsc,i_{n}\in I\end{subarray}}\frac{a_{i_{1}}\cdots a_{i_{n}}}{n\,\mathord{!}}.{\left[s_{i_{1}},\dotsc,s_{i_{n}}\right]}

and

{{\sigma^{\prime}}}^{\oc}=\sum_{n\in\mathbf{N}}\frac{1}{n\,\mathord{!}}.{\sigma^{\prime}}^{n}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbf{N}\\
i_{1},\dotsc,i_{n}\in I\end{subarray}}\frac{a_{i_{1}}\cdots a_{i_{n}}}{n\,\mathord{!}}.{\left[\sigma^{\prime}_{i_{1}},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{i_{n}}\right]}

which concludes the proof since each

{\left[s_{i_{1}},\dotsc,s_{i_{n}}\right]}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}{\left[\sigma^{\prime}_{i_{1}},\dotsc,\sigma^{\prime}_{i_{n}}\right]}
.

Lemma 7.8
.

If
\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathcal{E}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
and
\varphi\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathcal{F}}}}\varphi^{\prime}
then

a.\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathcal{E}}}}a.\epsilon^{\prime}
and
\epsilon+\varphi\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathcal{E}\mathbin{\cup}\mathcal{F}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}+\varphi^{\prime}
.

Proof 7.9
.

Follows directly from the definitions, using the fact that
summable families form a
\mathbf{S}
-semimodule.

Lemma 7.10
.

If
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}

then
M\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}}}}M^{\prime}
.

Proof 7.11
.

By induction on the reduction
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}

using Lemmas
7.2
to
7.6

in the cases of reduction from a simple term,
and Lemma
7.8

in the case of reduction from an algebraic term.

Recalling that
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\in\mathfrak{B}
we obtain:

Corollary 7.12
.

If
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}

then
M\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{B}}}}M^{\prime}
.

Observe that these results hold on Taylor supports as well,
which will be useful in the treatment of Taylor normalizable
terms in Section
8
:

Lemma 7.13
.

If
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}

then
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}}}}\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}

in
\mathbf{B}^{\Delta}
.

Proof 7.14
.

The proof is again by induction on the reduction
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}
using Lemmas

7.2
to Lemma
7.8

in
\mathbf{B}^{\Delta}
.

7.2.
Conservativity

Definition 7.15
.

We define the
full parallel reduct
of simple terms and algebraic terms
inductively as follows:

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(x\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}x

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(0\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}0

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\lambda x\,{S}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda x\,{\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(S\right)}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(a.M\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}a.\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(M\right)}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left({\left(\lambda x\,{S}\right)}\,{N}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(S\right)}{\left[\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(N\right)}/x\right]}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(M+N\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(M\right)}+\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(N\right)}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left({\left(S\right)}\,{N}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(S\right)}\right)}\,{\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(N\right)}}\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\quad\text{(if $S$ is not an abstraction).}$\hss}

As can be expected, we have

M^{\prime}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(M\right)}

as soon as

M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}
.
In this subsection, we will show that a similar property holds
for
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{(\oc)\mathfrak{B}}}}
.

Recall that, by Lemma
6.42
,
the full reduction operator
\mathord{\mathsf{F}}
on resource expressions
extends to bounded resource vectors.
We obtain:

Lemma 7.16
.

For all bounded
\sigma_{0}\in\mathbf{S}^{\Delta}
,
\overline{\tau}\in\mathbf{S}^{\oc\Delta}
,
\epsilon,\varphi\in\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
,

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(x\right)}

\displaystyle=x

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left({\sigma}^{\oc}\right)}

\displaystyle={\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\sigma\right)}}^{\oc}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\lambda x\,{\sigma}\right)}

\displaystyle=\lambda x\,{\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\sigma\right)}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(a.\epsilon\right)}

\displaystyle=a.{\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left({\left\langle\lambda x\,{\sigma}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\sigma\right)}\cdot\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\overline{\tau}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\epsilon+\varphi\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}+\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\varphi\right)}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left({\left\langle\sigma_{0}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\tau}}\right)}

\displaystyle={\left\langle\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\sigma_{0}\right)}\right\rangle}\,{\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\overline{\tau}\right)}}\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\quad\text{(if there is no abstraction term in ${\left|\sigma_{0}\right|}$).}$\hss}

Proof 7.17
.

The proofs of those identities are basically the same as those of Lemmas

7.2
to
7.8
,
the necessary summability conditions following from Lemma

6.42
.

Lemma 7.18
.

For all
M\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}
,

\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(M\right)}\right)}
.

Proof 7.19
.

We know that
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}
is bounded.
The identity is then proved by induction on simple terms and algebraic terms,
using the previous lemma in each case.

Lemma 7.20
.

For all bounded term reduction structure
\mathfrak{S}

and all
M\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}
,
if
M\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
then
\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{S}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(M\right)}
.

Proof 7.21
.

By Corollary
6.46
,

\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{S}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}

and we conclude by the previous lemma.

This result can then be generalized to sequences of
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}
-reductions.

Lemma 7.22
.

For all bounded term reduction structure
\mathfrak{S}

and all
M\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}
,
if
M\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{S}}^{n}}}\sigma^{\prime}

then
\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{S}}^{n}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}^{n}{\left(M\right)}
.

Proof 7.23
.

By induction on
n
. The case
n=0
is trivial, and the inductive case follows
from the previous lemma and strong confluence of
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{S}}}}
:
if
M\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{S}}^{n}}}\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{S}}}}\tau

then by induction hypothesis
\sigma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{S}}^{n}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}^{n}{\left(M\right)}
,
hence by strong confluence, there exists
\tau^{\prime}
such that

\tau\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{S}}^{n}}}\tau^{\prime}
and

\mathord{\mathsf{F}}^{n}{\left(M\right)}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{S}}}}\tau^{\prime}
;
by the previous lemma,

\tau^{\prime}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}^{n+1}{\left(M\right)}
.

We have thus obtained some weak kind of conservativity of
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{B}}}}
w.r.t.

\beta
-reduction, but it is not very satisfactory: the same result would hold
for the tautological relation

\mathbf{S}{\left\langle\mathfrak{B}\right\rangle}\times\mathbf{S}{\left\langle\mathfrak{B}\right\rangle}
,
which is indeed the same as
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{B}}}}
if
1
has an opposite
element in
\mathbf{S}
.
Even when
\mathbf{S}
is zerosumfree, the converse to Lemma

7.10
cannot hold in general if only because there can
be distinct
\beta
-normal forms
M\not\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}N
such that
M\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}N
(see Example

4.5
).
Under this hypothesis, we can nonetheless obtain an actual conservativity
result on normalizable pure
\lambda
-terms as follows.

We write
\simeq_{\beta}
for the symmetric, reflexive and transitive closure of

\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}
.
Similarly, if
\mathfrak{E}
is a reduction structure, we write
\simeq_{\partial\,\mathfrak{E}}
for the
equivalence on
\mathbf{S}{\left\langle\mathfrak{E}\right\rangle}
induced by
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{E}}}}
.

Lemma 7.24
.

Assume
\mathbf{S}
is zerosumfree.
Let
M,N\in\Lambda
be such that
M
is normalizable.
Then
M\simeq_{\partial\,\mathfrak{B}}N
iff
M\simeq_{\beta}N
.

Proof 7.25
.

Corollary
6.46
entails that,
if
\mathfrak{E}
is a bounded reduction structure, then

\epsilon\simeq_{\partial\,\mathfrak{E}}\epsilon^{\prime}
iff
\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{E}}^{*}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}^{n}{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}\right)}
for some
n\in\mathbf{N}
.
Now assume
M\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}
is normalizable and
write
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(M\right)}
for its normal form:
in particular
M\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}}}\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(M\right)}
, by
Corollary
7.12
.
If
M\simeq_{\partial\,\mathfrak{B}}N
, we thus have

\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(M\right)}\simeq_{\partial\,\mathfrak{B}}N
, hence

\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(M\right)}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathfrak{B}}^{*}}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}^{n}{\left(N\right)}

for some
n\in\mathbf{N}
.
In particular, if
\mathbf{S}
is zerosumfree, we obtain

\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(M\right)}\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}\mathord{\mathsf{F}}^{n}{\left(N\right)}
.
If moreover
M,N\in\Lambda
, we deduce
M\simeq_{\beta}N
by the injectivity
of
\mathord{\tau}
on
\Lambda
.

The next section will allow us to establish a similar conservativity result,
without any assumption on
\mathbf{S}
, at the cost of restricting the reduction
relation to normalizable resource vectors.

8.
Normalizing Taylor expansions

Previous works on the normalization of Taylor expansions were restricted

a priori
, to a strict subsystem of the algebraic
\lambda
-calculus:

•

the uniform setting of pure
\lambda
-terms
[
ER08
,
ER06
]
;

•

the typed setting of an extension
of system
F
to the algebraic
\lambda
-calculus
[
Ehr10
]
;

•

a
\lambda
-calculus extended with formal finite sums,
rather than linear combinations
[
PTV16
,
TAO17
]
.

In all these, pathological terms were avoided, e.g. those involved
in the inconsistency Example
4.1
.
Moreover observe that the very notion of normalizability is not compatible with

\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}
, and in particular the identity
0\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}0.M
: those previous works
circumvented this incompatibility, either by imposing normalizability
via typing, or by excluding the formation of the term
0.M
.

Our approach is substantially different.
We introduce a notion of normalizability on resource vectors such that:

•

both pure
\lambda
-terms and normalizable algebraic
\lambda
-terms
(in particular typed algebraic
\lambda
-terms and normalizable
\lambda
-terms with sums)
have a normalizable Taylor expansion;

•

the restriction of
\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}
to normalizable resource vectors
is a consistent extension of both
\beta
-reduction on pure
\lambda
-terms and
normalization on algebraic
\lambda
-terms, without any assumption on the
underlying semiring of scalars.

8.1.
Normalizable resource vectors

We say
\epsilon\in\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
is
normalizable
whenever the family

{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(e\right)}\right)}_{e\in{\left|\epsilon\right|}}

is summable. In this case, we write

\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\sum_{e\in(\oc)\Delta}\epsilon_{e}.\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(e\right)}
.

Normalizable vectors form a finiteness space.
Recall indeed from Subsection
3.1

that
e\geq_{\partial}e^{\prime}
iff
e\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{*}}}\epsilon^{\prime}
with
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}\right|}
.
If
e\in(\oc)\Delta
, we write

\mathord{\uparrow}{e}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\{e^{\prime}\in(\oc)\Delta\mathrel{;}e^{\prime}\geq_{\partial}e\right\}}
.
Then
\epsilon
is normalizable iff
for each normal resource expression
e
,
{\left|\epsilon\right|}\mathbin{\cap}\mathord{\uparrow}{e}
is finite:
writing
(\oc)\mathcal{N}={\left\{e\in(\oc)\Delta\mathrel{;}\text{$e$ is normal}\right\}}

and
(\oc)\mathfrak{N}={\left\{\mathord{\uparrow}{e}\mathrel{;}e\in(\oc)\mathcal{N}\right\}}^{\perp}\mathbin{\cap}(\oc)\mathfrak{F}_{\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}}
,
we obtain that
\mathbf{S}{\left\langle(\oc)\mathfrak{N}\right\rangle}
is the set of normalizable resource vectors.
Observe that
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}
is defined on all
\mathbf{S}{\left\langle(\oc)\mathfrak{N}\right\rangle}

but is guaranteed to be linear-continuous only when restricted to
subsemimodules of the form
\mathbf{S}^{\mathcal{E}}
with
\mathcal{E}\in(\oc)\mathfrak{N}
.

For our study of hereditarily determinable terms in Section
9
,
it will be useful to decompose
(\oc)\mathfrak{N}
into a decreasing sequence
of finiteness structures.

Definition 8.1
.

We define the
monomial depth

\mathord{\mathbf{d}}{\left(e\right)}\in\mathbf{N}
of a
resource expression
e\in(\oc)\Delta
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{d}}{\left(x\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}0

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{d}}{\left({\left\langle s\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\max(\mathord{\mathbf{d}}{\left(s\right)},\mathord{\mathbf{d}}{\left(\overline{t}\right)})

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{d}}{\left(\lambda x\,{s}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\mathord{\mathbf{d}}{\left(s\right)}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbf{d}}{\left({\left[t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n}\right]}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}1+\max{\left\{\mathord{\mathbf{d}}{\left(t_{i}\right)}\mathrel{;}1\leq i\leq n\right\}}

We write
(\oc)\mathcal{N}_{d}={\left\{e\in(\oc)\mathcal{N}\mathrel{;}\mathord{\mathbf{d}}{\left(e\right)}\leq d\right\}}

so that
(\oc)\mathcal{N}=\bigcup_{d\in\mathbf{N}}(\oc)\mathcal{N}_{d}
.
We then write
(\oc)\mathfrak{N}_{d}={\left\{\mathord{\uparrow}{e}\mathrel{;}e\in(\oc)\mathcal{N}_{d}\right\}}^{\perp}\mathbin{\cap}(\oc)\mathfrak{F}_{\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}}

so that
(\oc)\mathfrak{N}=\bigcap_{d\in\mathbf{N}}(\oc)\mathfrak{N}_{d}
.
Each finiteness structure
(\oc)\mathfrak{N}_{d}
is moreover a reduction structure
for any reduction relation contained in
\mathrel{{\rightarrow_{\partial}^{*}}}

(and so is
(\oc)\mathfrak{N}
). Indeed,
writing
\mathord{\downarrow}{e}={\left\{e^{\prime}\in(\oc)\Delta\mathrel{;}e\geq_{\partial}e^{\prime}\right\}}

and
\mathord{\downarrow}{\mathcal{E}}=\bigcup_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\mathord{\downarrow}{e}
, we obtain:

Lemma 8.2
.

If
\mathcal{E}\in(\oc)\mathfrak{N}_{d}
then

\mathord{\downarrow}{\mathcal{E}}\in(\oc)\mathfrak{N}_{d}
.

Proof 8.3
.

Let
e^{\prime\prime}\in(\oc)\mathcal{N}_{d}
and

e^{\prime}\in\mathord{\downarrow}{\mathcal{E}}\mathbin{\cap}\mathord{\uparrow}{e^{\prime\prime}}
.
Necessarily, there is
e\in\mathcal{E}
such that
e\geq_{\partial}e^{\prime}
.
Then
e\in\mathcal{E}\mathbin{\cap}\mathord{\uparrow}{e^{\prime\prime}}
:
since
\mathcal{E}\in(\oc)\mathfrak{N}_{d}
, there are finitely many values for
e

hence for
e^{\prime}
by Lemma
3.4
.

It follows that normalizable vectors are stable under reduction:

Lemma 8.4
.

If
\epsilon\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{(\oc)\mathfrak{N}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}

then
\epsilon^{\prime}\in\mathbf{S}{\left\langle(\oc)\mathfrak{N}\right\rangle}

and
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}\right)}
.

Proof 8.5
.

Assume there exists
\mathcal{E}\in(\oc)\mathfrak{N}
and families
(a_{i})_{i\in I}\in\mathbf{S}^{I}
,

{\left(e_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}\in(\oc)\Delta^{I}

and
{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}\in\mathbf{N}{\left[(\oc)\Delta\right]}^{I}

such that:

•

{\left(e_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}
is summable and
\epsilon=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.e_{i}
;

•

{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right)}_{i\in I}
is summable and
\epsilon^{\prime}=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
;

•

for all
i\in I
,
e_{i}\in\mathcal{E}
and
e_{i}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}}\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
.

We obtain that
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\bigcup_{i\in I}{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right|}\in(\oc)\mathfrak{N}

by Lemma
8.2
,
hence
\epsilon^{\prime}\in\mathbf{S}{\left\langle(\oc)\mathfrak{N}\right\rangle}
since
{\left|\epsilon^{\prime}\right|}\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
.
Then, by the linear-continuity of

\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}
on
\mathbf{S}^{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
,

\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(e_{i}\right)}=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}\right)}.

As a direct consequence, we obtain that
\simeq_{\partial\,(\oc)\mathfrak{N}}
is consistent,
without any additional condition on the semiring
\mathbf{S}
:

Corollary 8.6
.

If
\epsilon\simeq_{\partial\,(\oc)\mathfrak{N}}\epsilon^{\prime}

(in particular
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in\mathbf{S}{\left\langle(\oc)\mathfrak{N}\right\rangle}
)
then
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\epsilon^{\prime}\right)}
.

We can moreover show that the normal form of a Taylor normalizable
term is obtained as the limit of the parallel left reduction strategy.
Let us first precise the kind of convergence we consider.
With the notations of Subsection
2.3
, we say a sequence

\overrightarrow{\xi}={\left(\xi_{n}\right)}_{n\in\mathbf{N}}\in{\left(\mathbf{S}^{X}\right)}^{\mathbf{N}}

of vectors converges to
\xi^{\prime}
if, for all
x\in X
there exists

n_{x}\in\mathbf{N}
such that, for all
n\geq n_{x}
,
\xi_{n,x}=\xi^{\prime}_{x}
.
In other words we consider the product topology on
\mathbf{S}^{X}
,

\mathbf{S}
being endowed with the discrete topology.
Similarly to the notion of summability,
this notion of convergence coincides with that induced
by the linear topology on
\mathbf{S}^{X}
associated
with the maximal finiteness structure
