\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(X\right)}
on
X
:
in this particular case, a base of neighbourhoods of
0
is given by the sets

{\left\{\xi\in\mathbf{S}^{X}\mathrel{;}{\left|\xi\right|}\mathbin{\cap}\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\emptyset\right\}}

for
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\in\mathord{\mathfrak{P}}{\left(X\right)}^{\perp}=\mathord{\mathfrak{P}_{f}}{\left(X\right)}
,
or equivalently by the the sets

{\left\{\xi\in\mathbf{S}^{X}\mathrel{;}x\not\in{\left|\xi\right|}\right\}}

for
x\in X
.

The parallel left reduction strategy on resource vectors is defined as follows.

Definition 8.7
.

We define the
left reduct of a resource expression
inductively as follows:

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(\lambda x\,{s}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda x\,{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(s\right)}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left({\left[t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n}\right]}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left[\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(t_{1}\right)},\dotsc,\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(t_{n}\right)}\right]}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left({\left\langle x\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}_{1}\cdots\overline{t}_{n}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\langle x\right\rangle}\,{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(\overline{t}_{1}\right)}\cdots\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(\overline{t}_{n}\right)}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left({\left\langle\lambda x\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}_{0}\,\overline{t}_{1}\cdots\overline{t}_{n}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left\langle\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{t}_{0}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}_{1}\cdots\overline{t}_{n}}.

This is extended to finite sums of resource expressions by linearity:

\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\right)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(e_{i}\right)}
.

Lemma 8.8
.

For all resource expression
e\in(\oc)\Delta
,

e\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{{(1)}}}}\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(e\right)}
.

Proof 8.9
.

Easy by induction on
e
.

In particular
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(e\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(e\right)}\right)}
for all
e\in(\oc)\Delta
.
By Lemma
6.17
, we moreover obtain
that if
e^{\prime}\in{\left|\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(e\right)}\right|}
then

\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e\right)}\leq 4\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}
and
\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e\right)}=\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(e^{\prime}\right)}
.
As a consequence

{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(e\right)}\right)}_{e\in(\oc)\Delta}
is summable.
For all
\epsilon\in\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
, we set

\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\sum_{e\in(\oc)\Delta}\epsilon_{e}.\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(e\right)}

and obtain a linear-continuous map on resource vectors.

For all
\epsilon\in\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\Delta}
, we write
\epsilon\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{(\oc)\mathcal{N}}

for the
projection of
\epsilon
on normal resource expressions
:

\epsilon\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{(\oc)\mathcal{N}}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\sum_{e\in(\oc)\mathcal{N}}\epsilon_{e}.e\in\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\mathcal{N}}
.
We obtain:

Theorem 8.9
.

For all normalizable resource vector
\epsilon\in\mathbf{S}{\left\langle(\oc)\mathfrak{N}\right\rangle}
,

{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(\epsilon\right)}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{(\oc)\mathcal{N}}\right)}_{k\in\mathbf{N}}

converges to
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}
in
\mathbf{S}^{(\oc)\mathcal{N}}
.

Proof 8.10
.

Fix
{e^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{N}
.
Since
{\left|\epsilon\right|}\in(\oc)\mathfrak{N}
,

\mathcal{E}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left|\epsilon\right|}\mathbin{\cap}\mathord{\uparrow}{e^{\prime}}
is finite.
Let
{k^{\prime}}
be such that
\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k^{\prime}}{\left(e\right)}
is normal
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
.
Then
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\epsilon\right)}_{e^{\prime}}=\sum_{e\in{\left|\epsilon\right|}}\epsilon_{e}.{\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(e\right)}}_{e^{\prime}}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\epsilon_{e}.{\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(e\right)}}_{e^{\prime}}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\epsilon_{e}.{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k^{\prime}}{\left(e\right)}}_{e^{\prime}}
.
Moreover, by the linear-continuity of
\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}

on resource vectors,

{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(\epsilon\right)}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{(\oc)\mathcal{N}}\right)}_{e^{\prime}}=\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(\epsilon\right)}_{e^{\prime}}=\sum_{e\in\Delta}\epsilon_{e}.{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(e\right)}}_{e^{\prime}}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\epsilon_{e}.{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(e\right)}}_{e^{\prime}}=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}}\epsilon_{e}.{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k^{\prime}}{\left(e\right)}}_{e^{\prime}}
.

Observe that the projection on normal expressions is essential:

Example 8.11
.

Consider the looping term
\Omega\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left(\lambda x\,{{\left(x\right)}\,{x}}\right)}\,{\lambda x\,{{\left(x\right)}\,{x}}}
:
one can check that
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(\Omega\right)}\right)}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(\Omega\right)}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\mathcal{N}}=0
,
but it will follow from the results of subsection

8.2
that
\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(\Omega\right)}\right)}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(\Omega\right)}\not=0
for all
k\in\mathbf{N}
.

Analyzing this phenomenon was fundamental in the characterization of strongly
normalizable
\lambda
-terms by a finiteness structure on resource terms,
obtained by Pagani, Tasson and the author
[
PTV16
]
.

8.2.
Taylor normalizable terms

It is possible to transfer some of the good properties of reduction on
normalizable vectors to those algebraic
\lambda
-terms that have a normalizable Taylor
expansion.
More precisely, we say
M\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}
is
Taylor normalizable
if

\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\in(\oc)\mathfrak{N}
.
Then:

Lemma 8.12
.

Assume
M,M^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}
are such that
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}
.
Then
M
is Taylor normalizable iff
M^{\prime}
is Taylor normalizable.

Proof 8.13
.

First observe that by Lemma
7.13
,
we have
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}}}}\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}
in

\mathbf{B}^{\Delta}
.
Moreover observe that
\mathbf{B}{\left\langle\mathfrak{N}\right\rangle}
is nothing but

\mathfrak{N}
.

Assume
M
is Taylor normalizable,
i.e.

\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\in\mathfrak{N}
:
by Lemma
8.4
,
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}\in\mathbf{B}{\left\langle\mathfrak{N}\right\rangle}
,

i.e.

M^{\prime}
is Taylor normalizable.

Conversely, assume
M^{\prime}
is Taylor normalizable and
let
s^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{S}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\mathbin{\cap}\mathord{\uparrow}{s^{\prime\prime}}
:
we prove
\mathcal{S}
is finite.
Fix an enumeration
{\left(s_{k}\right)}_{k\in K}\in\mathcal{S}^{K}
of
\mathcal{S}
:

\mathcal{S}={\left\{s_{k}\mathrel{;}k\in K\right\}}
.
Since
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\mathrel{{\widetilde{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}_{\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}}}}\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}
, we have

\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}={\left\{t_{i}\mathrel{;}i\in I\right\}}
and

\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}=\bigcup_{i\in I}{\left|\tau^{\prime}_{i}\right|}
with
t_{i}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\partial}}}\tau^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in I
.
Now for all
k\in K
, there exists
i\in I
such that

s_{k}=t_{i}
. Since
s_{k}\geq_{\partial}s^{\prime\prime}
,
\tau^{\prime}_{i}\not=0

and we can fix
s^{\prime}_{k}\in{\left|\tau^{\prime}_{i}\right|}\subseteq\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}

such that
s_{k}\Succ_{\partial}s^{\prime}_{k}\geq_{\partial}s^{\prime\prime}
.
Since
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}\in\mathfrak{N}
, the set
{\left\{s^{\prime}_{k}\mathrel{;}k\in K\right\}}
is finite.
Then
\mathcal{S}\subseteq{\left\{s\in\Delta\mathrel{;}k\in K\text{, }s\Succ_{{(\mathord{\mathbf{h}}{\left(M\right)})}}s^{\prime}_{k}\right\}}

which is finite by Lemma
6.17
.

The consistency of
\beta
-reduction on Taylor normalizable terms follows.

Theorem 8.13
.

Assume
M,M^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}
are such that
M\simeq_{\beta}M^{\prime}
.
Then
M
is Taylor normalizable iff
M^{\prime}
is Taylor normalizable,
and in this case
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}\right)}
.
22
22

22

In the standard terminology of denotational semantics,
Theorem
5
expresses
the soundness of
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(\cdot\right)}\right)}
on
Taylor normalizable terms.

Proof 8.14
.

The first part is a direct corollary of Lemma
8.12
.
By Lemma
7.10
, it follows that

M\simeq_{\partial\,(\oc)\mathfrak{N}}M^{\prime}
, and then we conclude by Corollary

8.6
.

In other words, when restricted to Taylor normalizable terms,
the normal form of Taylor expansion is a valid notion of denotation.
Remark that, in general, it is not possible to generalize this result to those
terms
M
such that
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}
is normalizable because of the interaction
with coefficients:
consider,
e.g.
,
0\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}{\left(I\right)}\,{\infty_{x}}+(-1).{\left(I\right)}\,{\infty_{x}}\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}\infty_{x}+(-1).{\left(I\right)}\,{\infty_{x}}
, and observe that

\mathord{\tau}{\left(\infty_{x}+(-1).{\left(I\right)}\,{\infty_{x}}\right)}\not\in\mathbf{S}{\left\langle\mathfrak{N}\right\rangle}
.

Definition 8.15
.

We define the
left reduct of an algebraic
\lambda
-term
inductively as follows:

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(\lambda x\,{S}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda x\,{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(S\right)}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(0\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}0

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left({\left(x\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left(x\right)}\,{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M_{1}\right)}\cdots\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M_{n}\right)}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(a.M\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}a.\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M\right)}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left({\left(\lambda x\,{S}\right)}\,{M_{0}\,M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left(S{\left[M_{0}/x\right]}\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M+N\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M\right)}+\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(N\right)}

Observe that this definition is exhaustive by Fact

3
.
It should be clear that
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M\right)}
for all term
M
,
and that
\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M\right)}=M
when
M
is in normal form
(although the converse may not hold).
Now we can establish that
\mathord{\mathsf{L}}
commutes with Taylor expansion.

Lemma 8.16
.

For all
\sigma\in\mathbf{S}^{\Delta}
,
\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left({\sigma}^{\oc}\right)}={\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(\sigma\right)}}^{\oc}
.

Proof 8.17
.

First observe that by the definition of
\mathord{\mathsf{L}}
and
the linear-continuity of both
\mathord{\mathsf{L}}
and the monomial construction,
for all
\sigma_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{n}\in\mathbf{S}^{\Delta}
, we have

\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left({\left[\sigma_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{k}\right]}\right)}={\left[\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(\sigma_{1}\right)},\dotsc,\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(\sigma_{k}\right)}\right]}
.
In particular,
\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(\sigma^{k}\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(\sigma\right)}^{k}
.
We deduce that

\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left({\sigma}^{\oc}\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbf{N}}\frac{1}{k\,\mathord{!}}.{\sigma^{k}}\right)}=\sum_{k\in\mathbf{N}}\frac{1}{k\,\mathord{!}}.\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(\sigma\right)}^{k}={\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(\sigma\right)}}^{\oc}
, by the linear-continuity of
\mathord{\mathsf{L}}
.

Lemma 8.18
.

For all
M\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}
,
\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M\right)}\right)}
.

Proof 8.19
.

By induction on the definition of
\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M\right)}
:
in addition to the inductive hypothesis
and the linear-continuity of
\mathord{\mathsf{L}}
, we use
Lemma
8.16
in the case of a head variable,
and Lemmas
4.4
,

4.5
and

8.16

in the case of a head
\beta
-redex.

As a direct corollary of Theorem
4
,
we obtain:

Theorem 8.19
.

For all Taylor normalizable term
M
,
the sequence of normal resource vectors

{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M\right)}\right)}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\mathcal{N}}\right)}_{k\in\mathbf{N}}

converges to
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}
in
\mathbf{S}^{\mathcal{N}}
.

This property is very much akin to the fact that the Böhm tree
\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}{\left(M\right)}
of
a pure
\lambda
-term
M
is obtained as the limit (in an order theoretic sense)
of normal form approximants of the left reducts of
M
.
This analogy will be made explicit in Section
9
.
Before that, we apply our results to normalizable algebraic
\lambda
-terms.

8.3.
Taylor expansion and normalization commute on the nose

By a general standardization argument, we can show that parallel reduction is a
normalization strategy:

Lemma 8.20
.

An algebraic
\lambda
-term
M
is normalizable iff there exists
k\in\mathbf{N}
,
such that
\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(M\right)}
.

Proof 8.21
.

Recall that we consider algebraic
\lambda
-terms up to
\simeq_{+}
only.
Then one can for instance use the general standardization technique
developed by Leventis for a slightly different presentation of the calculus

[
Lev16
]
.

A direct consequence is that
M
normalizes iff the judgement
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}

can be derived inductively by the following rules:
23
23

23

Moreover, it seems natural to conjecture that if
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}
then
M
(or,
rather, its
\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}
-class) is normalizable in the sense of Alberti

[
Alb14
]
, and then the obtained normal forms are the same (up to

\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}
).

{S}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}
{\lambda x\,{S}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}

{M_{1}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}
\cdots
{M_{n}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}
{{\left(x\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}

{{\left(S{\left[M_{0}/x\right]}\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}
{{\left(\lambda x\,{S}\right)}\,{M_{0}\,M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}

{0}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}

{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}
{a.M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}

{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}
{N}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}
{\left(M+N\right)}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}

In the remaining of this subsection, we prove that normalizable algebraic

\lambda
-terms are Taylor normalizable, using a reducibility technique:
like in Ehrhard’s work for the typed case
[
Ehr10
]
, or
our previous work for the strongly normalizable case
[
PTV16
]
,

(\oc)\mathfrak{N}
is the analogue of a reducibility candidate.
We prove each key property (Lemmas
8.22

to
8.29
)
using the family of structures
(\oc)\mathfrak{N}_{d}

rather than
(\oc)\mathfrak{N}
directly: this will be useful in section

9
, while
the corresponding results for
(\oc)\mathfrak{N}
are immediately derived from those.

Lemma 8.22
.

If
\mathcal{S}\in\mathfrak{N}_{d}
then
\lambda x\,{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathfrak{N}_{d}
.

Proof 8.23
.

Let
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{d}
and
t\in{\left(\lambda x\,{\mathcal{S}}\right)}\mathbin{\cap}\mathord{\uparrow}{t^{\prime}}
.
Necessarily,
t=\lambda x\,{s}
and
t^{\prime}=\lambda x\,{s^{\prime}}
with
s\in{\mathcal{S}}\mathbin{\cap}\mathord{\uparrow}{s^{\prime}}

which is finite by assumption.

Lemma 8.24
.

If
\mathcal{S}\in\mathfrak{N}_{d}
then

{\mathcal{S}}^{\oc}\in\oc\mathfrak{N}_{d+1}
.

Proof 8.25
.

Let
\overline{t}^{\prime}\in\oc\mathcal{N}_{d+1}
and
\overline{t}\in{{\mathcal{S}}^{\oc}}\mathbin{\cap}\mathord{\uparrow}{\overline{t}^{\prime}}
.
Write
n=\mathord{\#}{\overline{t}^{\prime}}
.
Without loss of generality, we can write

\overline{t}={\left[t_{1},\dotsc,t_{n}\right]}
and

\overline{t}^{\prime}={\left[t^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,t^{\prime}_{n}\right]}
so that

t_{i}\geq_{\partial}t^{\prime}_{i}
and
t^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathcal{N}_{d}
, for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
.
Since
\overline{t}\in{{\mathcal{S}}^{\oc}}
, each
t_{i}\in{\mathcal{S}}
.
Since
\mathcal{S}\in\mathfrak{N}_{d}
,
t^{\prime}_{i}
being fixed, there are finitely
many possible values for each
t_{i}
.

Lemma 8.26
.

If
\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{1},\dotsc,\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{n}\in\oc\mathfrak{N}_{d}
then

{\left\langle x\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{1}\cdots\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{n}}\in\mathfrak{N}_{d}
.

Proof 8.27
.

Let
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{d}
and

t\in{\left({\left\langle x\right\rangle}\,{\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{1}\cdots\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{n}}\right)}\mathbin{\cap}\mathord{\uparrow}{t^{\prime}}
.
Necessarily,
t={\left\langle x\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}_{1}\cdots\overline{t}_{n}}
and
t^{\prime}={\left\langle x\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}^{\prime}_{1}\cdots\overline{t}^{\prime}_{n}}

and, for each
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
,
\overline{t}_{i}\in\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{i}
,
\overline{t}_{i}\geq_{\partial}\overline{t}^{\prime}_{i}
and

\overline{t}^{\prime}_{i}\in\oc\mathcal{N}_{d}
: since
\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\in\oc\mathfrak{N}_{d}
,
there are finitely many possible values for each
\overline{t}_{i}
.

Corollary 8.28
.

If
\mathcal{T}_{1},\dotsc,\mathcal{T}_{n}\in\mathfrak{N}_{d}
then

{\left\langle x\right\rangle}\,{{\mathcal{T}_{1}}^{\oc}\cdots{{\mathcal{T}_{n}}^{\oc}}}\in\mathfrak{N}_{d+1}
.

Lemma 8.29
.

If

{\left\langle\partial_{x}\mathcal{S}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{0}\right\rangle}\,{{\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{1}}\cdots{\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{n}}}\in\mathfrak{N}_{d}

then

{\left\langle\lambda x\,{\mathcal{S}}\right\rangle}\,{{\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{0}}\,{\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{1}}\cdots{\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{n}}}\in\mathfrak{N}_{d}
.

Proof 8.30
.

Let
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{d}
,
and let
u\in{\left({\left\langle\lambda x\,{\mathcal{S}}\right\rangle}\,{{\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{0}}\,{\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{1}}\cdots{\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{n}}}\right)}\mathbin{\cap}\mathord{\uparrow}{u^{\prime}}
.
In other words,
u^{\prime}\in{\left|\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(u\right)}\right|}
and we can write

u={\left\langle\lambda x\,{s}\right\rangle}\,{{\overline{t}_{0}}\,{\overline{t}_{1}}\cdots{\overline{t}_{n}}}

with
s\in{\left|\mathcal{S}\right|}
and
\overline{t}_{i}\in{\left|\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\right|}
for
i\in{\left\{0,\dotsc,n\right\}}
.
Write
v={\left\langle\partial_{x}s\cdot\overline{t}_{0}\right\rangle}\,{{\overline{t}_{1}}\cdots{\overline{t}_{n}}}
:
Corollary
3.4
entails

v\geq_{\partial}u^{\prime}
, hence
we have
v\in{\left({\left\langle\partial_{x}\mathcal{S}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{0}\right\rangle}\,{{\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{1}}\cdots{\overline{\mathcal{T}}_{n}}}\right)}\mathbin{\cap}\mathord{\uparrow}{u^{\prime}}
.
By assumption, there are finitely many possible values for
v
.
Then,
v
being fixed, by Lemma
3.4
,
we have
\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(u\right)}=\mathord{\mathbf{fv}}{\left(v\right)}
and
\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(u\right)}\leq 2\mathord{\mathbf{s}}{\left(v\right)}+2
, hence
there are finitely many possible values for
u
.

Theorem 8.30
.

If
M
is normalizable, then
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\in\mathfrak{N}
,
and
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\in\mathbf{S}{\left\langle\mathfrak{N}\right\rangle}
.

Proof 8.31
.

By induction on the derivation of
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}
:
Lemma
8.22
,
Corollary
8.28
and
Lemma
8.29

respectively entail the translation
of the first three inductive rules through Taylor expansion.
The other three follow from the fact that
\mathfrak{N}

is a resource structure (because it is a finiteness structure).

It remains to prove that in this case,
\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(M\right)}\right)}
is indeed
the normal form of
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}
.

Theorem 8.31
.

If
M
is normalizable, then
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(M\right)}\right)}
.

Proof 8.32
.

By Theorem
7
,
M
is Taylor normalizable.
Then Theorem
5
entails

\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(M\right)}\right)}\right)}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(M\right)}\right)}
.

8.4.
Conservativity

The restriction to normalizable vectors allows us to prove
an analogue of Lemma
7.24
,
without any assumption on the semiring of scalars.

Lemma 8.33
.

Let
M,N\in\Lambda
be normalizable.
Then
M\simeq_{\partial\,\mathfrak{N}}N
iff
M\simeq_{\beta}N
.

Proof 8.34
.

Assume
M\simeq_{\partial\,\mathfrak{N}}N
.
By Corollary
8.6
,
we have
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}\right)}
.
By Theorem
8
,
we obtain
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(M\right)}\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}
.
Since
M
and
N
are pure
\lambda
-terms,
we deduce
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(M\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(N\right)}

from the injectivity of
\mathord{\tau}
on
\Lambda
.

The reverse direction is similar to Theorem

5

and does not depend on
M
and
N
being pure
\lambda
-terms:
apply Lemmas
7.10
,

8.4
and

8.12

to the reduction path from
M
to
N

We can adapt this result to non-normalizing pure
\lambda
-terms
thanks to previous work by Ehrhard and Regnier:
24
24

24

We could as well rely on Theorem
9
,
to be proved in the next section.

{thmC}

[
[
ER08
,
ER06
]
]

For all pure
\lambda
-term
M\in\Lambda
,

\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\in\mathfrak{N}

and
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}{\left(M\right)}\right)}

where
\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}{\left(M\right)}
denotes the Böhm tree of
M
.

Here
Böhm tree
is to be understood as
generalized normal form for left
\beta
-reduction
.
In particular it does not involve
\eta
-expansion. More formally,
the Böhm tree of a
\lambda
-term is the possibly infinite tree obtained
coinductively as follows:

•

if
M
is head normalizable and its head normal form is

\lambda x_{1}\,{\cdots\lambda x_{n}\,{{\left(x\right)}\,{N_{1}\cdots N_{k}}}}

then
\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}{\left(M\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda x_{1}\,{\cdots\lambda x_{n}\,{{\left(x\right)}\,{\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}{\left(N_{1}\right)}\cdots\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}{\left(N_{k}\right)}}}}

•

otherwise
\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}{\left(M\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\bot
, where
\bot
is a constant
representing unsolvability.

Taylor expansion can be generalized to Böhm trees
[
ER06
]
, setting in
particular
\mathord{\tau}{\left(\bot\right)}=0
: this is still injective.

Lemma 8.35
.

If
M,N\in\Lambda
and
M\simeq_{\partial\,\mathfrak{N}}N

then
\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}{\left(M\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}{\left(N\right)}
.

Proof 8.36
.

By Corollary
8.6
,
we have
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}\right)}
.
By Theorem
8.4
,
we obtain
\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}{\left(M\right)}\right)}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}{\left(N\right)}\right)}
.
We conclude since
\mathord{\tau}
is injective on Böhm trees.

In the next and final section, we prove a generalization of
Theorem
8.4
to
the non-uniform setting
which is made possible by the results
we have achieved so far.

9.
Normal form of Taylor expansion,
façon
Böhm trees

The Böhm tree construction is often introduced as the limit of an increasing sequence

(\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}_{d}{\left(M\right)})_{d\in\mathbf{N}}
of finite normal form approximants,

aka
finite Böhm trees, where
\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}_{d}{\left(M\right)}
is defined inductively as
follows:

•

\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}_{0}{\left(M\right)}=\bot
;

•

if
M
is head normalizable and its head normal form is

\lambda x_{1}\,{\cdots\lambda x_{n}\,{{\left(x\right)}\,{N_{1}\cdots N_{k}}}}

then
\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}_{d+1}{\left(M\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda x_{1}\,{\cdots\lambda x_{n}\,{{\left(x\right)}\,{\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}_{d}{\left(N_{1}\right)}\cdots\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}_{d}{\left(N_{k}\right)}}}}

•

otherwise
\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}_{d+1}{\left(M\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\bot
;

and the order on Böhm trees is the contextual closure of the inequality
\bot\leq M
for all
M
.

In this final section of our paper, we show that the
normal form of
Taylor expansion
operator generalizes this construction to the class
of
hereditarily determinable terms
: these encompass both all pure
\lambda
-terms and
all normalizable algebraic
\lambda
-terms, but exclude terms such as
\infty_{x}
, that
produce unbounded sums of head normal forms. More precisely, we show that any
hereditarily determinable term
M
is Taylor normalizable, and moreover admits
a sequence of approximants
{\left(\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}\right)}_{d\in\mathbf{N}}
, such that
each
\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}
is an algebraic
\lambda
-term in normal form, and the
sequence of normal term vectors
{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}\right)}\right)}_{d\in\mathbf{N}}

converges to
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}
.

The results in this section should not hide the fact that the more fundamental
notion is that of Taylor normalizable term, which arises naturally by combining
Taylor expansion with the normalization of resource terms, subject to a
summability condition. We believe this approach is quite robust, and may be
adapted modularly following both parameters: to other systems admitting Taylor
expansion; and to variants of summability, possibly associated with topological
conditions of the semiring of scalars.

By contrast, the definition of hereditarily determinable terms is essentially

ad-hoc
.
Its only purpose is to allow us to generalize Theorem

8.4
and support our claim that:

the normal form of Taylor expansion extends the notion of Böhm tree
to the non-uniform setting
.

9.1.
Taylor unsolvability

In the ordinary
\lambda
-calculus, head normalizable terms are exactly those with a
non trivial Böhm tree. This is reflected via Taylor expansion: it is easy to check that

\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}=0
iff
M
has no head normal form.
In the non uniform setting, a similar result holds, although
we need to be more careful about the interplay between reduction and coefficients.

Definition 9.1
.

We say an algebraic
\lambda
-term
M
(resp. simple term
S
)
is
weakly solvable
if the judgement
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}

can be derived inductively by the following rules:

{{\left(x\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}

{S}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
{\lambda x\,{S}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}

{{\left(S{\left[M_{0}/x\right]}\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
{{\left(\lambda x\,{S}\right)}\,{M_{0}\,M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}

{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
{a.M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}

{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
{M+N}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}

{N}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
{M+N}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}

It should be clear that, if
M
is a pure
\lambda
-term,
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
iff
M
is
head normalizable. In the general case, we show that
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}

iff normalizing the Taylor expansion of
M
yields a non trivial result.
More formally:

Definition 9.2
.

We say an algebraic
\lambda
-term
M\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}
is
Taylor unsolvable

and write
{M}\,\mathord{\Uparrow}
if
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(s\right)}=0
for all
s\in\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}
.

In particular, if
{M}\,\mathord{\Uparrow}
then
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\in\mathbf{S}{\left\langle\mathfrak{N}\right\rangle}

and
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}=0
: indeed,

{\left|\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right|}\subseteq\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}
.
Beware that the reverse implication does not hold in general.
We can then show that
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
iff
M
is Taylor solvable
(Lemmas
9.3
and
9.5
).

Lemma 9.3
.

If there exists
s\in\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}
such that
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(s\right)}\not=0

then
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

Proof 9.4
.

We prove by induction on
k\in\mathbf{N}
then on
M\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}

that if
{\left|\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(s\right)}\right|}
contains a normal resource term
and
s\in\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}
then
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

If
M={\left(x\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}
we conclude directly.

If
M=\lambda x\,{T}
then
s=\lambda x\,{t}
with
t\in\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(T\right)}
:
necessarily
{\left|\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(t\right)}\right|}
contains a normal resource term
and by induction hypothesis we obtain
{T}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
hence

{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

If
M={\left(\lambda x\,{T}\right)}\,{M_{0}\,M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}

then
s={\left\langle\lambda x\,{t}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{s}_{0}\,\overline{s}_{1}\cdots\overline{s}_{n}}

with
t\in\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(T\right)}
and
\overline{s}_{i}\in{\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M_{i}\right)}}^{\oc}
for

i\in{\left\{0,\dotsc,n\right\}}
. Necessarily
k>0
and there is

s^{\prime}\in{\left|\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(s\right)}\right|}={\left|{\left\langle\partial_{x}t\cdot\overline{s}_{0}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{s}_{1}\cdots\overline{s}_{n}}\right|}

such that
{\left|\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k-1}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}\right|}

contains a normal resource term. By Lemma
4.5
,

s^{\prime}\in\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left({\left(T{\left[M_{0}/x\right]}\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}\right)}
:
we obtain
{{\left(T{\left[M_{0}/x\right]}\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}

by induction hypothesis, and then
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

If
M=a.N
,
M=N+P
or
M=P+N
with
s\in\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(N\right)}

then we obtain
{N}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
by induction hypothesis,
and then
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

Lemma 9.5
.

If
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
, then there exists
s\in\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}
such that

\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(s\right)}\not=0
.

Proof 9.6
.

By induction on the derivation of
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
.

If
M={\left(x\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}
,
set
s={\left\langle x\right\rangle}\,{{\left[\right]}\cdots{\left[\right]}}
(
x
applied
n
times
to the empty monomial):
s\in\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}
and
s
is normal.

If
M=\lambda x\,{T}
with
{T}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
: by induction hypothesis, we obtain

t\in\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(T\right)}
with
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(t\right)}\not=0
and set
s=\lambda x\,{t}
.

If
M=\lambda x\,{T}{M_{0}\,M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}
and
M^{\prime}={\left(T{\left[M_{0}/x\right]}\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}

with
{M^{\prime}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
, the induction hypothesis gives

s^{\prime}\in\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}
such that
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(s^{\prime}\right)}\not=0
.
By Lemma
4.5
, there exist
t\in\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(T\right)}

and
\overline{u}_{i}\in{\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M_{i}\right)}}^{\oc}
for
i\in{\left\{0,\dotsc,n\right\}}
such that

s^{\prime}\in{\left|{\left\langle\partial_{x}t\cdot\overline{u}_{0}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{u}_{1}\cdots\overline{u}_{n}}\right|}
.
We then set
s={\left\langle\lambda x\,{t}\right\rangle}\,{\overline{u}_{0}\cdots\overline{u}_{n}}
.

If
M=a.N
,
M=N+P
or
M=P+N
with
{N}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\mathrm{w}}
: the induction hypothesis
gives
s\in\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(N\right)}\subseteq\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}
with
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(s\right)}\not=0

directly.

Taylor unsolvable terms are thus exactly those that are not weakly solvable.
25
25

25

If we restrict to non-deterministic
\lambda
-terms
(
i.e.
only add a sum operator to the usual
\lambda
-term constructs)
then we obtain
{M}\,\mathord{\Uparrow}
iff
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\right)}=\emptyset
,
which states the adequacy of
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(\cdot\right)}\right)}
for
the observational equivalence associated with may-style head normalization.

They are moreover stable under
\simeq_{\beta}
:

Lemma 9.7
.

If
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}
then
{M}\,\mathord{\Uparrow}
iff
{M^{\prime}}\,\mathord{\Uparrow}
.

Proof 9.8
.

If
\mathcal{E}\subseteq(\oc)\Delta
,
we write
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathcal{E}\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\bigcup{{\left\{{\left|\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(e\right)}\right|}\mathrel{;}e\in\mathcal{E}\right\}}}
.
We leave as an exercise to the reader the proof that

\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}\right)}

as soon as
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}
: this is the analogue of Lemma

8.4
on Taylor supports
(in particular there is no summability condition, and scalars
play absolutely no rôle).

9.2.
Hereditarily determinable terms

The Böhm tree construction is based on the fact that, for a pure
\lambda
-term
M
,
either
M
is unsolvable, or it reduces to a head normal form; and then the
same holds for the arguments of the head variable.
We will be able to follow a similar construction for the class of
hereditarily
determinable terms
: intuitively, a simple term is in
determinate form
if it is
either unsolvable or a head normal form; and a term is hereditarily
determinable if it reduces to a sum of determinate forms, and this holds
hereditarily in the arguments of head variables. Formally:

Definition 9.9
.

Let
M\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{S}}
be an algebraic
\lambda
-term.
We say
M
is
d
-determinable
if the judgement
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}

can be derived inductively from the following rules:

{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{0}

{M}\,\mathord{\Uparrow}
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}

{S}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
{\lambda x\,{S}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}

{M_{1}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
\cdots
{M_{n}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
{{\left(x\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d+1}

{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
{a.M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}

{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
{N}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
{M+N}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}

{{\left(S{\left[M_{0}/x\right]}\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
{{\left(\lambda x\,{S}\right)}\,{M_{0}\,M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}

We say
M
is
hereditarily determinable
and write
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\omega}

if
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
for all
d\in\mathbf{N}
.
We say
M
is in
d
-determinate form
and write
{M}\,\mathsf{df}_{d}

if
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
is derivable from the above rules excluding the
last one.

It should be clear that
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}
implies
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\omega}
.
Observing that
{M}\,\mathord{\Uparrow}
for all unsolvable pure
\lambda
-terms (
i.e.
those pure
\lambda
-terms having no head normal form), we moreover obtain

{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\omega}
for all
M\in\Lambda
.

We can already prove that hereditarily determinable terms are Taylor normalizable:
26
26

26

Observe that this fails if we replace
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}
with

{\left|\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right|}
in the definition of
{M}\,\mathord{\Uparrow}
:
write
I\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda x\,{x}
and
consider,
e.g.
,
M={\left(\lambda x\,{{\left(I\right)}\,{{\left(x+(-1).\infty_{y}\right)}}}\right)}\,{\infty_{y}}

which head-reduces to
{\left(I\right)}\,{{\left(\infty_{y}+(-1).\infty_{y}\right)}}\simeq_{\mathord{\tau}}{\left(I\right)}\,{0}
,
with
{{\left(I\right)}\,{0}}\,\mathord{\Uparrow}
but of course
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\not\in\mathfrak{N}_{0}
.
The very same problem would occur if we were to consider terms up to
\simeq_{\mathrm{v}}
.

Lemma 9.10
.

If
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
then
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\in\mathfrak{N}_{d}
.
If moreover
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{\omega}
then
\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M\right)}\in\mathfrak{N}
.

Proof 9.11
.

The second fact follows directly from the first one,
which we prove by induction on the derivation of
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
:
we use the definition of
{M}\,\mathord{\Uparrow}
for the base case,
and rely on Lemma
8.22
,
Corollary
8.28
,
Lemma
8.29
,
or the fact that
\mathfrak{N}_{d}
is a resource structure
to establish the induction in the other cases.

On the other hand, there are Taylor normalizable terms that do not follow this
pattern: intuitively, hereditarily determinable terms rule out any
representation of an infinite sum of head normal forms,
whereas Taylor normalizability allows to represent an infinite sum of normal forms
as long as their Taylor expansions are pairwise disjoint.
More formally:

Example 9.12
.

Write
s_{0}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda x\,{x}
,
and
s_{n+1}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda x\,{s_{n}}
.
Let
M_{step}=\lambda y\,{\lambda z\,{z}}+\lambda y\,{\lambda z\,{\lambda x\,{{\left(y\right)}\,{y\,z}}}}
and then

M_{loop}={\left(M_{step}\right)}\,{M_{step}\,\lambda x\,{x}}
.
Write
u=\lambda y\,{\lambda z\,{z}}
and

v_{n,k}=\lambda y\,{\lambda z\,{\lambda x\,{{\left\langle y\right\rangle}\,{y^{n}\,z^{k}}}}}
so that

\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M_{step}\right)}={\left\{u\right\}}\mathbin{\cup}{\left\{v_{n,k}\mathrel{;}n,k\in\mathbf{N}\right\}}
.
Let
s\in\mathord{\mathcal{T}}{\left(M_{loop}\right)}
be such that
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(s\right)}\not=0
:
a simple inspection shows that either
s={\left\langle u\right\rangle}\,{{\left[\right]}\,{\left[s_{0}\right]}}

and then
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(s\right)}=s_{0}
,
or
s={\left\langle v_{n,1}\right\rangle}\,{{\left[v_{0,1},\dotsc,v_{n-1,1},u\right]}\,{\left[s_{0}\right]}}

and then
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(s\right)}=s_{n+1}
.
It follows that
M_{loop}
is Taylor normalizable.
On the other hand, observe that
\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{2}{\left(M_{loop}\right)}=\lambda x\,{x}+\lambda x\,{M_{loop}}
, which is not
1
-determinate:
hence no
\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{2k}{\left(M_{loop}\right)}
is
1
-determinate and
it will follow from Lemma
9.15

that
M_{loop}
is not
1
-determinable.

Hence hereditarily determinable terms form a strict subclass of Taylor
normalizable terms, containing both pure
\lambda
-terms and normalizable algebraic

\lambda
-terms.
For each level
d\in\mathbf{N}
, the class of
d
-determinable terms (resp. of

d
-determinate terms) is moreover stable under left reduction:

Lemma 9.13
.

If
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
(resp.
{M}\,\mathsf{df}_{d}
)
then
{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M\right)}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
(resp.
{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M\right)}}\,\mathsf{df}_{d}
).

Proof 9.14
.

We give the proof for
d
-determinable terms,
by induction on the derivation of
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
:
the case of
d
-determinate terms is similar, except
that we do not consider head redexes.

If
d=0
the result is direct. Otherwise, write
d=d^{\prime}+1
.

If
{M}\,\mathord{\Uparrow}
then
{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M\right)}}\,\mathord{\Uparrow}

by Lemma
9.7
,
and we conclude directly.

If
M=\lambda x\,{S}
with
{S}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
: by induction hypothesis

{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(S\right)}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
, and then

{\lambda x\,{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(S\right)}}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
.

If
M={\left(x\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}

with
{M_{i}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d^{\prime}}
for
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
:
by induction hypothesis
{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M_{i}\right)}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d^{\prime}}

for
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
, and then

{{\left(x\right)}\,{{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M_{1}\right)}}\cdots{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M_{n}\right)}}}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
.

If
M={\left(\lambda x\,{S}\right)}\,{M_{0}\,M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}

with
{{\left(S{\left[M_{0}/x\right]}\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}

then we conclude directly since

\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M\right)}={\left(S{\left[M_{0}/x\right]}\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}
.

If
M=a.N
with
{N}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
: by induction hypothesis

{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(N\right)}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
, and then

{a.\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(N\right)}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
.

If
M=N+P
with
{N}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
and
{P}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
: by induction hypothesis

{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(N\right)}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
and
{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(P\right)}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
,
and then
{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(N\right)}+\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(P\right)}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
.

Now we can formally prove that applying the parallel left reduction strategy to

d
-determinable terms does reach
d
-determinate forms.

Lemma 9.15
.

If
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
then there exists
k\in\mathbf{N}

such that
{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M\right)}}\,\mathsf{df}_{d}
.

Proof 9.16
.

By induction on the derivation of
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
.

If
d=0
or
{M}\,\mathord{\Uparrow}
, then
{M}\,\mathsf{df}_{d}
.

If
M=\lambda x\,{S}
with
{S}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
:
by induction hypothesis, we have
k\in\mathbf{N}
such that

{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(S\right)}}\,\mathsf{df}_{d}

and then

\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M\right)}=\lambda x\,{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(S\right)}}
hence
{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M\right)}}\,\mathsf{df}_{d}
.

If
M={\left(x\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}
with
d>0
and
{M_{i}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d-1}
for each
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
:
by induction hypothesis, we obtain
k_{i}\in\mathbf{N}
such that

{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k_{i}}{\left(M_{i}\right)}}\,\mathsf{df}_{d-1}

for each
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
. Let
k=\max{\left\{k_{i}\mathrel{;}1\leq i\leq n\right\}}
:
by Lemma
9.13
, we also have

{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M_{i}\right)}}\,\mathsf{df}_{d-1}
for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
.
Since

\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left({\left(x\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}\right)}={\left(x\right)}\,{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M_{1}\right)}\cdots\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M_{n}\right)}}
we conclude that

{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M\right)}}\,\mathsf{df}_{d}
.

If
M={\left(\lambda x\,{S}\right)}\,{M_{0}\,M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}

with
{{\left(S{\left[M_{0}/x\right]}\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
:
by induction hypothesis, we have
k_{0}\in\mathbf{N}
such that

{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k_{0}}{\left({\left(S{\left[M_{0}/x\right]}\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}\right)}}\,\mathsf{df}_{d}
.
It is then sufficient to observe that

\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M\right)}={\left(S{\left[M_{0}/x\right]}\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}

and set
k=k_{0}+1
.

If
M=a.N
with
{N}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
:
by induction hypothesis, we have
k\in\mathbf{N}
such that

{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(S\right)}}\,\mathsf{df}_{d}

and then

\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M\right)}=a.{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(S\right)}}
hence
{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M\right)}}\,\mathsf{df}_{d}
.

If
M=N+P
with
{N}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
and
{P}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
:
by induction hypothesis, we have
k_{0},k_{1}\in\mathbf{N}
such that

{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k_{0}}{\left(N\right)}}\,\mathsf{df}_{d}

and

{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k_{1}}{\left(P\right)}}\,\mathsf{df}_{d}

and then, setting
k=\max(k_{0},k_{1})
,

\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M\right)}=\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(N\right)}+\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(P\right)}
hence
{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M\right)}}\,\mathsf{df}_{d}
by the previous lemma.

9.3.
Approximants of the normal form of Taylor expansion

Now we introduce the analogue of finite Böhm trees for hereditarily
determinable terms:

Definition 9.17
.

If
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
then we define the

normal
d
-approximant

\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}
of
M
inductively as follows:

\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}0
if
d=0
or
{M}\,\mathord{\Uparrow}
, and

\displaystyle\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(\lambda x\,{S}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\lambda x\,{\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(S\right)}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left({\left(x\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}{\left(x\right)}\,{\mathsf{NA}_{d-1}{\left(M_{1}\right)}\cdots\mathsf{NA}_{d-1}{\left(M_{n}\right)}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left({\left(\lambda x\,{S}\right)}\,{M_{0}\,M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left({\left(S{\left[M_{0}/x\right]}\right)}\,{M_{1}\cdots M_{n}}\right)}

\displaystyle\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(a.M\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}a.\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}

\displaystyle\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M+N\right)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}+\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(N\right)}

otherwise.

First observe that
d
approximants are stable under parallel left reduction:

Lemma 9.18
.

If
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
then
\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}=\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M\right)}\right)}
.

Proof 9.19
.

Recall indeed that, by Lemma
9.13
,

{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M\right)}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
so that
\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{L}}{\left(M\right)}\right)}

is well defined.
The proof is then straightforward, by induction on
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
.

We do not prove here that
d
-determinable terms and the associated

d
-approximants are stable under arbitrary reduction:
if
{M}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}
and
M\mathrel{{\Rightarrow_{\beta}}}M^{\prime}
then
{M^{\prime}}\,\mathord{\Downarrow}_{d}

and then
\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}=\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}
.
We believe it is a very solid conjecture, but it would require us to develop
a full standardization argument: in our non-deterministic setting, this is
known to be tedious at best
[
Alb14
,
Lev16
]
.
Since we introduced hereditarily determinable terms
ad-hoc
, only to be
able to define normal
d
-approximants, we feel that the general
study of their computational behaviour is not worth the effort.

Our next step is to show that if
M
is in
d+1
determinate form,
then
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\mathcal{N}_{d}}
depends only on
\mathsf{NA}_{d+1}{\left(M\right)}
.

Lemma 9.20
.

If
{M}\,\mathsf{df}_{d+1}

then, for all
s\in\mathcal{N}_{d}
,
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}_{s}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathsf{NA}_{d+1}{\left(M\right)}\right)}_{s}
.

Proof 9.21
.

By induction on the derivation of
{M}\,\mathsf{df}_{d+1}
,
writing
M^{\prime}=\mathsf{NA}_{d+1}{\left(M\right)}
.

If
{M}\,\mathord{\Uparrow}
then
M^{\prime}=0
and

\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}_{s}=0
for all
s\in\mathcal{N}
,
hence the result holds.

If
M=\lambda x\,{T}
with
{T}\,\mathsf{df}_{d+1}
then

M^{\prime}=\lambda x\,{\mathsf{NA}_{d+1}{\left(T\right)}}

and we can assume
s=\lambda x\,{t}
:
otherwise
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}_{s}=0=\mathord{\tau}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}_{s}
.
Then
t\in\mathcal{N}_{d}
and by induction hypothesis

\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}_{s}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(T\right)}_{t}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathsf{NA}_{d+1}{\left(T\right)}\right)}_{t}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}_{s}
.

If
M={\left(x\right)}\,{N_{1}\cdots N_{n}}
with
{N_{i}}\,\mathsf{df}_{d}

for all
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
then

M^{\prime}={\left(x\right)}\,{N^{\prime}_{1}\cdots N^{\prime}_{n}}
with
N^{\prime}_{i}=\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(N_{i}\right)}

and we can assume
s={\left\langle x\right\rangle}\,{\overline{t}_{1}\cdots\overline{t}_{n}}
:
otherwise
\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}_{s}=0=\mathord{\tau}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}_{s}
.
If
d=0
,
s\in\mathcal{N}_{0}
, hence
n=0
and then
M=x=M^{\prime}
.
Otherwise write
d=d^{\prime}+1
. For each
i\in{\left\{1,\dotsc,n\right\}}
,

{\left|\overline{t}_{i}\right|}\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{d^{\prime}}
.
By induction hypothesis we obtain

\mathord{\tau}{\left(N_{i}\right)}_{u}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(N^{\prime}_{i}\right)}_{u}

for all
u\in{\left|\overline{t}_{i}\right|}
: it follows that

{\mathord{\tau}{\left(N_{i}\right)}}^{\oc}_{\overline{t}_{i}}={\mathord{\tau}{\left(N^{\prime}_{i}\right)}}^{\oc}_{\overline{t}_{i}}

by the definition of promotion. Then

\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}_{s}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathord{\tau}{\left(N_{i}\right)}_{\overline{t}_{i}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathord{\tau}{\left(N^{\prime}_{i}\right)}_{\overline{t}_{i}}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}_{s}
.

If
M=a.N
with
{N}\,\mathsf{df}_{d+1}
then

\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}_{s}=a.\mathord{\tau}{\left(N\right)}_{s}=a.\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathsf{NA}_{d+1}{\left(N\right)}\right)}_{s}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}_{s}
by induction hypothesis.

Similarly, if
M=N+P
with
{N}\,\mathsf{df}_{d+1}
and
{P}\,\mathsf{df}_{d+1}
then

\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}_{s}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(N\right)}_{s}+\mathord{\tau}{\left(P\right)}_{s}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathsf{NA}_{d+1}{\left(N\right)}\right)}_{s}+\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathsf{NA}_{d+1}{\left(P\right)}\right)}_{s}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(M^{\prime}\right)}_{s}

by induction hypothesis.

We obtain our final theorem:

Theorem 9.21
.

For all hereditarily determinable term
M
,
the sequence

{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}\right)}\right)}_{d\in\mathbf{N}}

of normal vectors
converges to
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}
in
\mathbf{S}^{\mathcal{N}}
.

Proof 9.22
.

First observe that each

\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}\right)}\in\mathbf{S}^{\mathcal{N}}
, because

\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}
is in normal form. Let
s\in\mathcal{N}

and fix
d\geq\mathord{\mathbf{d}}{\left(s\right)}+1
:
by Lemmas
9.13
,

9.15
and

9.18
,
there exists
k_{0}\in\mathbf{N}
such that

{\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M\right)}}\,\mathsf{df}_{d}
and
\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M\right)}\right)}=\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}

whenever
k\geq k_{0}
.
By Lemma
9.20
, we moreover have

\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}\right)}_{s}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M\right)}\right)}\right)}_{s}=\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathord{\mathsf{L}}^{k}{\left(M\right)}\right)}_{s}
.
It follows that
\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}\right)}_{s}=\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}_{s}
,
by Theorem
6
.
Since this holds for any
d\geq\mathord{\mathbf{d}}{\left(s\right)}+1
, we have just proved that

{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}\right)}_{s}\right)}_{d\in\mathbf{N}}
converges to
\mathord{\mathsf{NF}}{\left(\mathord{\tau}{\left(M\right)}\right)}_{s}
,
for the discrete topology.

In the case of pure
\lambda
-terms, by identifying
0
with the unsolvable Böhm tree

\bot
, it should be clear that the sequence
{\left(\mathsf{NA}_{d}{\left(M\right)}\right)}_{d\in\mathbf{N}}
is nothing but the increasing sequence of finite
approximants of
\mathord{\mathsf{BT}}{\left(M\right)}
: Theorem
9
is thus
a proper generalization of Theorem
8.4

of which it provides a new proof.
